ACHIEVEMENTS OF NIETZSCHE

BY

LUDWIG KLAGES

LEIPZIG / JOHANN AMBROSIUS BARTH

Since it always affords men a curious pleasure when they believe they have noticed something like an “apparent intentionality of chance,” it may be permitted to mention that our first (two-hour) lecture on Nietzsche’s psychological achievements took place, quite by chance, on May 14, 1919, in the Bernoullianum of the University of Basel, that is, almost to the day fifty years after Nietzsche’s inaugural lecture there on “Homer and Classical Philology” (May 28, 1869), and that, again just as much by chance, the first fully worked-out publication, comprising the main content of our book, appeared exactly twenty-five years after Nietzsche’s death in the Jahrbuch der Charakterologie (edited by Emil Utitz, Panverlag Charlottenburg; Part One complete in vol. I, Parts Two and Three in excerpts in vols. II and III). In the meantime, to be sure, we had spoken on the subject in various places and had repeatedly made public at least the leading ideas in daily newspapers and reviews. This book at last presents the entire mass of ideas unabridged, and leads not only up to the points where our own philosophy would begin, but here and there a considerable stretch of the way into it. As is already evident from the “Introduction,” it is at the same time intended as a supplement to our characterology, inasmuch as it traverses in every direction the whole problem-field of the drive for self-esteem by the light of Nietzsche’s pioneering disclosures. — If in doing so the spiritual work of one of the greatest thinkers is admittedly shattered in a manner that may somewhat recall Uhland’s “Swabian Stroke,” since on the one side a poisonous half, on the other a golden half sinks to the ground, let it not be forgotten that, notwithstanding the personal interest we all take in the great figures of mankind, in the end it is not the philosophers and their systems that matter, but the truth-content of the systems; hence even reverence could scarcely render the genuine inquirer a better service than is done by separating his errors from his discoveries!

March 1926. The Author.

When, roughly a decade and a half ago, we published in excerpts, under the heading Prinzipien der Charakterologie, our lectures on characterology, which we had by then already been giving for years, and thereby — employing a coinage of the at that time rather forgotten Bahnsen (1867) — took up again for the first time systematically, and with the help of the present-day investigative means of the sciences of consciousness, a direction of research which Romanticism had cultivated and the intervening period had, so to speak, officially neglected, some critics found even the name painful and offensive to their ears, while they considered as euphonious as they were instructive such hybrid formations as “individual psychology” or even combinations like “differential psychology,” which, to be sure, left no doubt that their advocates had not even succeeded in finding the problem. Meanwhile the spiritual climate has changed curiously. The word “characterology” is beginning to enter into serious competition with “psychology,” which since 1850 had been favored almost exclusively, and the former faultfinders have long been eagerly at pains to give the impression that from childhood on they had longed for nothing so passionately, thought through nothing so persistently and thoroughly, as a science of personality and of the varieties of personal characters. The splitting of the substrate of traits into spirit and soul, which we demonstrated to be indispensable, is on the point of becoming, at least verbally, a common possession of thought, and as for the physiognomic endeavors, whose significance and scope we vindicated with our foundation of the science of expression, they are running so rampant, albeit for the time being more extra muros, that in the face of so many apostles and seers and arch-clever folk one would have to fear for the reason of mankind, if there were still very much there to spoil at all. Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 1

p. 2

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Since, as the foregoing sentences already allow one to glimpse, we are inclined to see in all this far more a fashion in catchwords than expressions of an inwardly directed will to truth, we know how to get over it when our writings are most carefully passed over in silence precisely by those authors who have drawn from them the comparatively best of what they offer and, in some cases, almost their entire “intellectual property,” and for the rest we console ourselves with Goethe’s:

Of what is base

Let no one complain;

For it is the power that holds sway,

Whatever they may tell you! But spiritual theft, insofar as it does not happen to concern inventions and patents, differs in one essential point from material theft, in that, though like the latter it is able to serve the personal advantage of the plagiarist, it is by no means able to preserve the stolen goods, namely the appropriated new finding. Genuine knowledge (thus not mere information) lives on in each person who acquired it through the mediation of another exactly and only to the extent that he was able to produce it again, or better, to renew it; whereas, when merely taken over externally, it degenerates into catchwords serviceable at best for technical and commercial purposes, in which no generative power capable of increasing knowledge dwells any longer. This holds even for the truths of physics. Every creative physicist will, as it were, discover Galileo or some other predecessor of outstanding importance anew, and each of his own findings infallibly signifies at the same time a hitherto unknown interpretation of findings of the prior history.

Therefore it is not only a demand of personal decency, but in a still far higher degree of the matter itself, that one should disclose the provenances of one’s own findings and in particular, so far as is in any way attainable, uncover the impulses which the thought of the present has in common with the thought of kindred periods of the past. We have attempted to do this to the best of our ability, albeit with the utmost brevity, in the first chapter of the Prinzipien, and we may be permitted to reproduce here verbatim some passages relating to it. “Since the inner life is even today still somewhat deeper than it appears in the mirror of conventional psychology, and therefore gives the investigative drive

p. 3

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

manifold stimuli lying outside its general considerations, it has in reality not accomplished what one would most readily have had to demand of it: the critical foundation of the ‘sciences of the spirit.’ If only because neither philology, historical science, ethnology, nor psychiatry and practical knowledge of human nature find any usable help in it, as we emphasized at the outset, a new treatment of the material must in time force its way through, one which, without renouncing the refined knowledge of the processes of cognition, nevertheless seeks its task in understanding the whole abundance of forms of the life of the soul. — Such a treatment, however, by no means lacks forerunners, nor even a certain tradition, even if we disregard the sages of all times and peoples, who never pursued psychology in the sense of intellectualism. And indeed the properly psychological drive is alive above all in the epoch of German spiritual life called ‘Romanticism,’ from which, as regards research in the science of the soul, we cite only Carl Gustav Carus; not the greatest name, to be sure, but a man in whose nature the roving element of those days found sufficiently shrewd circumspection to become, instead of streaming away in divining imaginings, capable of condensation into a doctrine still awaiting elaboration and extension. Him and similar beginnings of contemporary minds the coming development then washed away, like many a fruitful seed of the thirties and forties, so that today we have to tie the chain anew across a chasm of time. — This, however, we could not do with so sure an eye for everything elective-affine without the mighty achievement of that man of the most recent past whose appearance, if it does not give room to new hope, yet crowns the passing of aging peoples with a proud radiance: without the achievement of Friedrich Nietzsche. For reasons whose analysis would lead too far here, the deepest glow of metaphysical vision in him feeds almost solely the beam of criticism and sharpens it to a never yet attained power of penetration. The instrument of his seership, however, is the gift of the ‘discernment of spirits,’ armed with the arrows of the keenest understanding. In him, for the first time again at least since the Middle Ages, and in the more familiar forms of the most immediate present, we have an example of that thousand-year flower, the truly great seer-through of souls and knower of spirits, who does not, like the poets, bury the contours of fire-born truths

1*

p. 4

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

beneath the flowery meadows of fanciful feelings. . . The real thrust of his philosophizing, however, is the volatilization not merely of ethics, say, but of the intellectual phenomenon as such, which for the first time in the world history known to us is, paradoxically, tested for its disposition, i.e. for its biological value, and that, moreover, without prejudice or goodwill, with the eyes of enmity toward spirit. ‘The falsity of a judgment is not yet an objection to a judgment’: a proposition whose consequences we read off from its more positive counterpart: correctness does not yet make a judgment justified, or truth is no value in itself. The organ of thought, too, oriented as it is toward reasons and ends, proves to be conditioned by drives, and its standards too are subjective ones. One can take sides for and against logic, and — this is Nietzsche’s most significant turn — one does the latter insofar as one stands on the side of life, which is unspiritual and alogical. Life and spirit fall apart, and indeed, in Nietzsche’s conception, spirit is a form of disease of life” (pp. 9–10).

As certain, now, as it was the position toward Carus here declared that occasioned his resurrection just now beginning, so certainly is the preoccupation with Nietzsche, setting in of late ever more decidedly, fed chiefly from other sources, whose closer appraisal does not belong here. For what it comes down to is getting rid of him — be it ten times over under the mask of rhetorical veneration! Against this, the above sentences may indicate a kind of program, which we now undertake to carry out and in part also to improve. In laying the greatest weight on the point that without making use of Nietzsche’s central findings a science of personality could never succeed, we hope at the same time to make visible the quite extraordinary difficulties with which genuine characterology has to wrestle, and thus also to reject at once the all too cheap garrulity that would persuade us to confuse with it arbitrary typologies such as anyone may devise for household use. — Since the new edition of our Prinzipien, out of print for some four years, demands considerable work of reshaping that is not yet finished, our discussions naturally pursue the secondary aim of at least in some pieces both supplementing and also correcting what is offered there1).

p. 5

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

We give here in chronological order, with the years of publication (or of composition) added, the abbreviations of the works of Nietzsche from which evidence is adduced. We confine ourselves to the smallest possible number of supporting passages and remark once and for all that not a single one is offered to which numerous others of related content could not be added. Smaller omissions within the quotations are indicated by two dots (. .), larger ones by three dots (. . .). Emphases (letter-spacing) introduced by us are marked by an asterisk (*). Where a connecting word is needed to bridge the gaps, it is enclosed in brackets. With several successive quotations from the same work, the title of the work is noted only at the end. — Apart from special cases, we deliberately do not name edition and page number; for our book does not intend to serve as a substitute for the study of Nietzsche, but on the contrary seeks to entice the reader to the most emphatic engagement with him! Many a reader who checks the references and must first search for the passages adduced will on that occasion — so we hope — be drawn anew into the reading of our thinker’s writings and notice with surprise how incompletely he had until this hour been acquainted with them

N I E T Z S C H E ’ S A I M A N D M E T H O D O F I N Q U I RY

PDF 13

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

NIETZSCHE

AS EXPLORER OF THE SOUL

That Schiller’s countlessly quoted saying about the hatred and favor of the parties probably fitted no one ever to a higher degree than Nietzsche is grounded partly in the passionate vehemence of his manner of expression, partly in the combative stance he adopts toward all the “ideals” of mankind without exception: he cleaves them with the sword of dialectic and burns them in the fire of his hatred. But the clamor of dispute among the men of letters, unavoidable on that account — some of whom covered him with insult and ignominy or with the still more eloquent silence, while the others extravagantly praised him and consciously or unconsciously imitated him — has long been dying away, if it has not already died away completely, although an echo of it has admittedly survived in the never-ending discussions of Nietzsche’s type of achievement and endowment. Even his opponents admit today that he belongs among the most remarkable phenomena of all the periods of culture known to us; but now he is supposed to have been a philosopher, now a prophet, now a poet, now an artist of language and stylist, now a rhetorician, and more of the like; indeed we recall having read at some time, with reference to him, the neologism “lyricist of knowledge,” as affected as it is tasteless!

Even if we had not (for reasons that would lead too far here) arrived at the conviction that the previous classification of the aims of achievement and of endowments no longer suffices for not a few minds of the 19th century, and among them some of the strongest, and — with the thinning out of the time-honored types, among which the surely dying “genius” is also to be counted! — calls for supplementation by basic forms yet to be found for the very first time, the question of Nietzsche’s chief endowment as well as of his cultural significance would nevertheless concern us directly hardly at all. For however that may stand, it can at any rate not

p. 10

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

be denied that the favorite object of his reflections and proclamations was the essence of man, and that he believed himself to be continually giving us disclosures about the constitution of the soul, partly of historical individuals, partly of peoples, races, cultures. If, moreover, he lets no opportunity slip to call himself a psychologist or, by preference, also a “diviner of souls,” indeed, without circumlocution puts forward the demand “that psychology be recognized once again as the mistress of the sciences, for whose service and preparation the other sciences exist” (J), then, as it seems to us, it should surely be the obvious course first of all to examine whether as an explorer of the soul he discovered anything that increased the stock of knowledge that cannot be lost. We even think that nothing of consequence at all could be decided about his type, his creative intentions, his conditioning by cultural history, etc., before that question has been decided, and we wish at the very least to have it settled with all emphasis that what occupies us here first is solely the weighing of the truth-content of his findings in the science of the soul, whether for the rest, by the testimony of his work, he was chiefly a thinker or a poet or a founder of religion, etc.

We wish now, to say it in advance, to advocate the view that with Nietzsche psychological research (= psychology) in the most proper sense of all only began at all, but do not neglect to add at once how this is meant, and in particular in what respect it is compatible with any degree whatever of esteem for the discoveries in the science of the soul made by earlier thinkers. We consider comparisons of greatness in the domain of endowments a chief concern of run-of-the-mill people, and are of the opinion that there has never yet been a thinker and poet of rank whose works could not bestow upon the genuine lover the deepest insights in abundance into the essence of man. Who would undertake to exhaust even approximately what treasures of this kind are harbored by the legacies of a Lao-tzu, Chuang-tzu, Plato, Cervantes, Shakespeare, Goethe, Novalis, to pick out only a few world-famous names from the immeasurable series! Such men are always also “diviners of souls” and have together made possible an intensification of self-reflection in relation to which the contributions of academic psychology strike one as a curiously pedantic plaything of average intelligence. That inceptive element, however, which in contrast to this we believe we hear in

p. 11

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Nietzsche’s direction of inquiry lies in the particularity of an aim of knowledge, to which must be added the particularity of the means to the end (method): two intimately connected states of affairs whose fitness for laying the foundation above all of a science of characters could in the end be made known in a single sentence. But only if we prepare that sentence step by step do we avoid the danger of appearing ambiguous. Before we begin to step, however, may it be permitted, by linking up with something half personal, to let the same task light up a little more colorfully, more enticingly, more promisingly.

The younger generation of today, insofar as it has not yet passed its thirtieth year, can, even with the most receptive immersion in Nietzsche’s works, never again feel its way into the surges of the soul to which, from the same occasion, a youth of roughly the same age, only far more thinly sown, was exposed in the nineties. The author of these lines at least looked on at this, because at that time his path in life crossed the careers of some personalities for whom the breath of the Nietzschean spirit meant an event that could at most be compared only to a typhoon robbing one of one’s senses, or an earthquake, or a volcanic eruption. If one lived through something of the sort, one finds it easier, at any rate, to record a peculiar experience, which, however, every receptive reader of the present will also confirm as soon as he has merely begun to pay attention to it. For if someone reads any writing of Nietzsche’s for the first time and at one stretch, he will find — whether what he heard attracted or repelled him — its psychic after-effect more similar to that of a painting by Böcklin, or of an Espagnola during a sirocco on the Gulf of Naples, than to the psychic after-effect of reading Plato’s Republic or Spinoza’s Ethics or Schopenhauer’s Fourfold Root of the Principle of Sufficient Reason. He is likely to be overcome by the feeling of a slight dizziness or of a kind of breathlessness, as if he had climbed the steepest heights too quickly, or as if he had walked through a cloudburst of rain. Nietzsche, scarcely have we begun to read, compels us to mount a magic chariot that flies with tearing speed through region after region, exchanging caverns of the earth’s depths for glaciated mountain ridges, all of it, however, bound together by an essentially estranging, often frightening, menacing, always sudden and surprising light.

p. 12

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Precisely because one seldom gives oneself a clear account of it, the reflection in consciousness of the mood briefly outlined here has given rise to the view, still quite predominantly widespread, that Nietzsche lacks all consistency in his trains of thought, turns capriciously and, as it were, merely sampling, now to this problem, now to that, or even takes a cruel pleasure in reviling today what he glorified yesterday. The reverse side of such censure would admittedly, beyond dispute, be the acknowledgment of a wealth of far-reaching and deep-reaching intellectual insights such as had never existed before, in the face of which it would seem rash to speak of a chief finding of his inquiry, let alone to want to capture it in a single sentence! There has been no one until now, nor could there be, who might presume to know all the veins of ore in the labyrinth of tunnels and galleries of this mine of thought, and no one could even guess for how many generations some of Nietzsche’s problems must renew themselves before answers to them came forth behind whose different problematic the generating one would vanish. Not dozens, no, hundreds of sentences could be taken up, each of which could furnish the theme for an investigation the size of a book, if one followed it into all its corners and secret places. “The thought is one thing, the deed another, the image of the deed another still. The wheel of reason does not roll between them” (Z). Into what depths, breadths, heights would the work grow that undertook to interpret and examine completely even the foregoing “thesis” alone, and to walk to their end the roads which it points out like a signpost with many arms!

But the immeasurable abundance of the thoughts, together with a changeability of the “views,” flaring up as it were as in sheet lightning, which entitled their bearer to believe of himself: “only he who changes remains akin to me,” signifies so little chaos, confusion, or self-negation that at least we have come to know of no thinker whose spiritual flight shot along more arrow-straight than Nietzsche’s! There are two pieces of evidence for this whose probative value can hardly be shaken. — First, it is always the same questions that run through Nietzsche’s entire output, and these are decided in the most diverse places either simply in the same way, albeit of course in language that varies and alters the lighting, or one aphorism takes up the reflection again at the very point where another had

p. 13

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

dropped it, even though ten or fifteen or twenty years had elapsed in between! Only a few pieces of evidence for this!

The GdT in the year 1872 offers, among other things, an unmasking of Socratism whose essentials might be paraphrased with the following sentences from it: “With astonishment he (Socrates) recognized that all those celebrities themselves (namely statesmen, orators, poets, artists) were without . . sure insight into their calling and practiced it only from instinct. ‘Only from instinct’: with this expression we touch the heart and center of the Socratic tendency. With it Socratism condemns existing art just as much as existing ethics: wherever it directs its examining glances, it sees the lack of insight and the power of delusion, and infers from this lack the inner perversity and objectionableness of what exists. From this one point Socrates believed he had to correct existence: he, the individual, steps with the air of disregard and superiority . . into a world whose hem we would count it our greatest good fortune to catch hold of with reverence.” No less than sixteen years later, namely in 88, the “Problem of Socrates” in the G is tuned to exactly the same key, but at the same time carried further, to the point of conveying insight into the causes of the overgrowth of the theoretical drive: Socrates, it says, had invented for an anarchy of the instincts that had advanced hopelessly far the appearance, deceptive though it was, of a remedy, by endeavoring to establish the daylight of reason in permanence. — The same book treats, partly with dazzling irony under the title “How the ‘True World’ Finally Became a Fable,” partly with objective rigor, the problem of the relations between appearance, reality, being, and in doing so offers among other things the following especially pertinent utterances: “‘Reason’ is the cause of our falsifying the testimony of the senses. Insofar as the senses show becoming, passing away, change, they do not lie. . . . But Heraclitus will be eternally right in this, that being is an empty fiction. The ‘apparent’ world is the only one: the ‘true world’ is merely a lie added on.” Further: “The characteristics that have been given to the ‘true being’ of things are the characteristics of non-being, of nothingness: the ‘true world’ has been constructed out of the contradiction to the actual world.” But in MA, thus ten years earlier, we read (16th maxim): “The human intellect has let appearance appear and has carried its erroneous

p. 14

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

basic conceptions into things. . . What we now call the world is the result of a multitude of errors and fantasies which . . have gradually arisen, grown into one another, and are now bequeathed to us as the accumulated treasure of the entire past. . .” Who could fail to see that, in relation to the earlier words, the later ones signify their continuation and closer elaboration!

Nietzsche is supposed to have changed his views about Wagner from the ground up. But in fact only his valuation of Wagner changed from the ground up, whereas with regard to his conception of Wagner the strongest preludes to the latest utterances can be shown already in the earliest. If we try to draw the “sum” from FW and NcW (88), it could best be done with the help of the following sentences: “With Wagner, at the beginning stands the hallucination: not of tones, but of gestures. Only for these does he then seek the tonal semiotics.” “Was Wagner a musician at all? At any rate he was something more: namely, an incomparable histrio . . the most astonishing genius of the theater that the Germans have had. . . Even as a musician he was only what he was in general: he became a musician, he became a poet, because the tyrant in him, his actor’s genius, compelled him to.” “. . . If it was Wagner’s theory that ‘the drama is the end, the music is always only the means,’ his practice, on the other hand, from beginning to end was ‘the attitude is the end, the drama, and the music too, is always only its means.’ Music as a means of making clear . . the dramatic gesture and the actor’s sensuous palpability; and the Wagnerian drama only an occasion for many interesting attitudes.” But what do we read in 1876 in “Richard Wagner in Bayreuth” (U)? “If one has tried to derive great developments from inner inhibitions . . , if, for instance, for Goethe poetry was a kind of expedient for a failed vocation as a painter, if one can speak of Schiller’s dramas as of a displaced popular eloquence, if Wagner himself seeks to interpret the advancement of music by the Germans, among other things, in this way too, that, lacking the seductive impulse of a naturally melodious gift of voice, they were compelled to take the art of tones with the same profound seriousness as their reformers took Christianity —: if one wished in similar fashion to bring Wagner’s development into connection with such an inner inhibition,

p. 15

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

then one might well assume in him a primordial endowment for acting*, which . . found its expedient and its salvation in drawing on all the arts for one great histrionic revelation.” A “But” that then follows, objectively no more weakening than the conditional form of the foregoing utterance, betrays to us only that Nietzsche’s accurate conception was already at the outset at feud with a veneration which, as we do not doubt, had predominantly personal grounds.

Or let one compare the astonishing psychology of the scholar and of scholarship in “Schopenhauer as Educator” of 74 (U) with the sixth main part of J (85), entitled “We Scholars,” or finally with chapters 23 and 25 of the essay on the meaning of ascetic ideals in ZG (87), and one will not deny that here too the author, across the intervening years, continues in content, so to speak, without a gap. — The same holds for his numerous pronouncements on democracy, freedom, the state, socialism, asceticism, likewise for his psychology of the sexes, his critique of endowments and of so-called genius, and so on without end. So extraordinary, truly, is the consistency and concord of the leading ideas in the most diverse writings of this explorer of the soul that his opponents would have rather more prospect of success than with the usual mode of contestation if they reproached him with too frequent repetitions! The self-contradiction which, to be sure, we too shall bring to bear — it is of the most fundamental, not to say the most terrible, kind, and perhaps precisely for that reason has, it seems, been overlooked by all the talkers — does not concern the sequence, change, and development of his thoughts, but more or less every main thought itself, and splits all his works, the earliest no less than the latest, indeed often the single page already, and not seldom even the single sentence!

Weightier still is the almost unexampled uniformity of the Nietzschean style! Goethe was certainly a significant stylist of prose. But if one compares, say, a passionate scene between Werther and Lotte with one between Eduard and Ottilie, one comes upon such great differences in the handling of language that one would hardly assign the two works to the same author if one did not already know of their origin. Exactly the opposite holds of Nietzsche. He did indeed pass from the broadly expansive and eloquently expository periodic style

p. 16

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of his didactic works of the early period more and more over to the passionately flickering style of a judicial rhetoric, in which foil-thrusts of hatred alternate with hammer-blows of wrath (not without gradually bending back again toward the beginning); but even if we open the Zarathustra, striding along in biblical solemnity, we always find the same peculiarities of cadence, the same syncopated meter, the same character of turns of phrase and images, the same accumulation of the plural, never before seen in anything like it, the same predilection for similes, which despite their superabundant influx never mingle with one another, the same many-colored transparency — in short, a uniformity in the handling of language whose idiosyncrasy, perhaps “all too Nietzschean” in Nietzsche’s sense, makes it utterly impossible not to recognize any page whatever by him! This, however, is infallibly the expression of a psychic-spiritual uniformity of the most unusual kind. And so, speaking in the simile of the magic chariot, we may trust that the wild ride has been set a goal which could be reached only with such a vehicle. And here we now close with the remark: we know one essential side of the goal once we have recognized the basic idea of Nietzsche’s science of the soul, and we take possession of the magic of the chariot by uncovering his psychological method.

PDF 21

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

KNOWLEDGE OF OTHERS

AND SELF -EXAMIN ATION

We shall make it easier to mark off Nietzsche’s utterly different science of essence from the by no means youthful academic psychology, which in the 19th century could only record a flowering hitherto without precedent, by applying results of our own inquiries which would not in every particular have met with Nietzsche’s approval. In this way we shall have sketched, as it were in woodcut fashion, the outline of his explorations as well as of his findings, and its lines remain unaffected by the fact that to produce them we made use of an instrument which Nietzsche — to his detriment, as we shall see — disdained.

Apollo’s world-famous threshold saying “Know thyself” had and has, besides its manifest meaning, a secret one, which was known to the esoterics of old. Inasmuch as the manifest meaning, which we consider first, addresses to everyone the summons to explore his own nature, it has helped to foster a prejudice whose refutation should lead us step by step over to its hidden significance: the prejudice, namely, that it lies nearer to us to take cognizance of one’s own ego than of another’s ego. We first show the truth of the opposite.

To begin with, the most fleeting glance at the spiritual history of all peoples, but especially of the European ones, reveals to us that the outer world formed the earliest, and inwardness a very much later, object of questioning, seeking, and finding, regardless of whether, at the word inwardness, one thinks more of the spirit or more of the soul. Consciousness of the ego is in general preceded by consciousness of things. The Greeks, for example, the true inventors of philosophy, had long possessed comprehensive systems concerning the origin and constitution of the universe before they began to reflect on the soul and the thinking spirit. The same holds in another way of the small child, whose initial perceptual achievements are oriented toward outer impressions Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 2

p. 18

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and which learns to touch and later even to fix its gaze on its own foot far sooner than it becomes aware that this foot belongs to itself and to personal stirrings of the will. — And so likewise it is objects of perception with which its grasp of psychic facts is first bound up. The infant child has long noticed anger or tenderness in the features of the persons who care for it and has answered the former with fear, the latter with affection, before it begins to know that it can itself fear and love, and is thus able to think what we mean by words such as fear or love. As surely as the thing-reflective judgment “there stands a tree” must have been passed for the self-reflective judgment “I see that a tree stands there” to be possible, so surely does the grasp of the states of others precede reflection on one’s own states, and this not least with regard to those demonic occurrences which, with countless turns of speech, we still bring to light unsuspectingly even today, as when we say of a thunderstorm, for instance, that it is rising “menacingly” in the sky2)!

For if all thinking objectification rests on the lived experience of reality, and if its character of being alien to the soul belongs to the content of experienceable reality, then what is the soul’s own (as likewise what is consciousness’s own) is found only by the detour through the thing of perception and in opposition to it, and it plainly requires a more than ordinary exertion of the spirit to move it, in its turn, into the place of the object. With the laconic turn of phrase: “The You is older than the I” (Z), Nietzsche casts a shadow-throwing light upon a fact whose opposite it had been good form to regard as an axiom at least since Descartes, and this not only among the ideologues but even among the “sensualists”! The other confronts me in the form of appearance, hence as primary reality; one’s own ego must first estrange itself from itself in order to become graspable; the other lies in the outward-going direction of the gaze that is natural to primitive man as well as to the child; one’s own ego is apprehended only through a reversal of the natural direction, or, more briefly, through “reflection.” But should anyone, in the face of a truth that afterward strikes one as something self-evident, doubt that the legend of the “immediate self-certainty” of consciousness and of inwardness was for a millennium and a half unshakably

p. 19

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

believed, let him simply be referred to the still ever-popular school psychologist Wundt, who believes he can mark off the science of the soul from natural science with the stipulation that the former rests on immediate, the latter on merely mediate experience! If one sets beside this a single sentence fragment of Nietzsche’s of the following kind: “Instead of that ‘immediate certainty’ in which the people (!) may believe in the given case, the philosopher . . is handed a series of questions of metaphysics, properly speaking questions of conscience of the intellect . . .” (J), then one has at the same time grasped once and for all what the guest appearance of a creative thinker of Nietzsche’s ruthlessly aggressive independence might mean for “science,” and why there they have not fully recovered from the fright of it even today. — If, then, the “Know thyself” in fact imposes on us a task of particular difficulty, the question arises all the more urgently how the appearance of the opposite could gain ground and owing to what misunderstanding self-examination has presumably taken a wrong direction. Meanwhile we wish to learn beforehand why self-examination in particular should be required at all for a science of character, and we introduce the answer with a preliminary consideration to the effect that an enhancement of personal “knowledge of human nature” may not be hoped for from it.

The ability for that can indeed, like every ability, be exercised and neglected, but not transmitted by teaching or acquired by learning, and it by no means coincides with the aptitude for the investigation of essence, notwithstanding that both gifts sometimes come together tolerably well in the same character. Goethe was, more or less, both, a significant judge of men and a significant explorer of the soul; Nietzsche the latter in the highest degree, the former at most a little on the side; but even the surely virtuoso knowledge of human nature of so brilliant a writer as the Abbé Galiani has scarcely anything more to do with a science of the soul and may help to convey to us the not exactly far-fetched insight that the greatest masteries in knowledge of human nature are found among political natures (= men of deeds), hence within the practicing professions rather than the shaping and writing ones — professions which today, summarily speaking, range from the hotel porter through the lawyer to the high financier, and formerly from the lackey through the prince to the governing minister and finally to the statesman of the grand style,

2*

p. 20

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

including, as goes without saying, the rulers of the Papal States. There can hardly ever have been a writer (by profession) who could have measured up in knowledge of human nature to the Wallensteins, Richelieus, Fredericks, Napoleons, Bismarcks; yet, however many entertaining sayings and exceedingly shrewd maxims we may owe to such men, no one will seriously wish to maintain that they enlarged the science of the soul in any way worth mentioning; and more than that, we must stress that not one of them would have grown richer in knowledge of human nature through the study of the most excellent works of psychology.

True, the “Renaissance” in particular presents personalities in whom strongly intellectual mainsprings are contradictorily coupled with strongly political ones; yet even here closer investigation would reveal to us the predominance, rooted in disposition, now of the impulse toward reflection on things over that toward self-reflection, now of the impulse toward self-reflection over that toward reflection on things; and in less transitional times above all, characters may be at least to some extent halved, among other ways, into men of practice and men of theory. As a variety of the theorist who looks predominantly inward, the explorer of the soul is drawn away from the direction natural to him by the compulsion to look outward; as a variety of the practitioner who looks predominantly outward, the man of deeds is conversely drawn away in just the same way by the compulsion to look inward; for which reason each of the two would have to expect from exercises in the other’s procedure not only no furtherance for his own but even a hindrance3). — Add to this that so-called knowledge of human nature (that is why we wrote “knowledge of human nature” above) is not so much a knowledge as an attitude, which could not be overloaded with knowledge and insights without danger of degeneration. A prominent politician may now and then bestow rules of wisdom upon us: his politics does not consist in their application, nor does he have his knowledge of human nature from them, but rather thanks to those pre-intellectual recognitions by virtue of which the animal avoids at least many of its natural enemies without yet having experienced their hostility, and likewise needs no experience in order to join in communities of life with some of its natural helpers and friends; on which matter we may be instructed in small things by the ability of the tender-hearted woman to adapt her behavior to the moods of a beloved person with a precision that exceeds not only

p. 21

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

her own power of judgment, at times considerably, but even the range of self-reflection of the other person in question!

Even if we had not adopted a standpoint which forbids ascribing to the insights of the science of essence an immediate fitness for influencing human action, we would nonetheless not have to concern ourselves for the present with the educational, indeed legislative, aims to which Nietzsche subordinated his psychological disclosures, because, as we have emphasized, our first duty is the weighing of those disclosures. But with regard to the question, recognized as worth examining, whether and to what end self-examination is needed at all in the service of a science of character, let us be permitted to point to a parting of the ways which lured some thinkers to the right, others to the left. Personal characters, so the one party reasons, can be nothing other than varieties of the character of mankind, for which reason we, in order to fathom characters, would have to fathom first and foremost the character of mankind; the essence of mankind, so the others reason, can only be found inductively on the basis of the most exact acquaintance with its varieties, for which reason we, in order to fathom it, would first have to explore the individual characters. It could be shown without difficulty that for millennia both directions have crossed each other and more or less feuded in all the “human sciences”; here, however, we wish to set forth that the representatives of both practice self-examination, only in opposite ways, and that it was precisely the secret meaning of the temple saying that misled both into taking the matter too lightly.

It was Aristotle who, with his νόησις νοήσεως, named the distinguishing mark of thinking consciousness and thereby of consciousness itself, in that meaning, namely, which ought to be conferred on the word exclusively. Whoever apprehends a process of the outer world with consciousness will sooner or later also grasp his apprehending and accordingly his capacity for it; and if the first step of reflection infallibly refers to the thing, then in principle already the second could refer back to the referring self. If someone can judge at all, then at some point he will also judge that he sees, hears, loves, feels, wills, and so forth, and in the end it will no longer escape him that he — passes judgments! But does he not thereby practice self-examination, and does he not in this way head straight

p. 22

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

for the essence of man, inasmuch as it is evidently given to man alone to pay homage to this mode of reflection?

Not only the opinion that one is in this way practicing the most proper and most essential self-examination, but even a variant of world-view based upon it begins in Greece at the latest with Protagoras, whose high significance in the history of the spirit was obscured for posterity by the more successful authorship of Plato, who would be quite unthinkable without his forerunnership. Scholars have not yet come to agreement on whether his famous proposition: “Man is the measure of all things, of those that are, that they are, of those that are not, that they are not,” has in view man as a species or the individual man. We content ourselves with a mere statement of our own conviction, since a justification would lead us too far from our subject: intent on protecting prerogatives, if alas no longer of blood, then at least of the superior personality, Protagoras meant the individual man (about which, incidentally, the few other words of his that have been preserved leave no doubt at all); but there lies in his proposition already the counter-opinion with which — according to Plato — a Socrates made war on him. For it by no means wishes to express that Protagoras is the measure of all things, but on the contrary, that any person whatever is! If he thereby unmistakably turns into the universal concept, then the further question arises what it is that guarantees this privilege to man in particular; to which the answer can only be: his share in the ability to “measure,” to weigh, to count, in a word to — judge. It took a little while yet before the spirit (Nous, Ratio) set itself up as the lawgiver of the world, and the Christian turn was required for it to set itself up straightway as the creator of the world as well; yet even the germ cell of the “cogito ergo sum,” no less than of the merely apparently opposed “esse est percipi,” would be easy to find and to demonstrate already in the dangerous double meaning of the Protagorean proposition, notwithstanding that its father was, in the theory of knowledge, a Heraclitean!

The following formula would make this recognizable: because man, and only man, can judge, he decides about being and not-being; for being and not-being exist only in the judgment about them. We point out at once the twofold error that such a view would harbor, provided it believed it had thereby circumscribed, of its object, thinking man, the

p. 23

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

essence: it takes him as the bearer exclusively of the power of judgment, and for that very reason it exchanges reality for being. The first error has had the consequence that for more than two millennia the, if one may say so, official self-examination was confused with the investigation of the cognitive faculty. “Philosophy reduced to theory of knowledge, in fact no more than a timid epochism and doctrine of abstinence . ., that is philosophy in its last throes . .,” thus Nietzsche in Beyond Good and Evil. Well, timid this so-called theory of knowledge has not been, which in the course of time swallowed up the entire science of the soul, so that the “psychologists” in all innocence speak of feelings, affects, drives, dreams, impressions, etc., as of “facts of consciousness,” and divide up the life of the soul, in method as in content, among concepts which they borrowed from the doctrine of the conditions of origin of the — knowledge of it. Indeed, does not Nietzsche himself betray an after-effect of the Protagorean error when, with sentences such as: “Only at the end of the knowledge of all things will man have come to know himself” (M), he grants man a kind of top position which a spirit pursuing the “divine animal” in human inwardness could understand only from the praiseworthiness of the gift of judgment! None the less for that, the striking power of his critique proves itself nowhere more irresistibly than in shattering the hybrid fiction that there could be a cogitare without the vivere, and contents of knowledge without contents of lived experience!

As regards the second error, it suffices to recall the truly world-historical struggle out of which Greek thought gave birth to itself, indeed all European thought down to the present day, the struggle of Heracliteanism with Eleaticism, and the sham victory of the latter, already sealed by Plato’s philosophy, in order to dispel every doubt that even then reality had been exchanged for being. But that is not enough: deeply entangled in the “unsteady errancy of men’s lives” (Euripides), the Greek also divined in his way the occasioning ground, in that, without of course seeing through the role of the ego, he in fact deified the ego by projecting it outward into the most spiritual of his Olympians: Apollo. “For at bottom we can lay no claim at all to existence, but every mortal nature hovers always between coming-into-being and passing away

p. 24

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and gives merely . . a dark and uncertain opinion of itself. If one now strains one’s understanding in order to grasp it wholly and completely, it fares as with water strongly compressed. For just as the latter, through pressing and squeezing, runs away and is entirely lost, so reflection goes astray when it chases after an all too distinct representation of any being subject to accidents and changes, now toward its origin, now toward its passing away, without being able to snatch anything . . truly existent . . . But of God one must say: He is; and he is not according to any determinate time, but according to an immovable, timeless, and unchangeable eternity, in which the past, the future, and the new have no place at all, which is a single one and fills out everlasting being with a single Now.” (From Plutarch’s On the ‘ei’ in the Temple at Delphi.) If, accordingly, we thought the secret meaning of the “Know thyself” to be uncovered as follows: know in the self the archetype and the source of all being, then we would see foreshadowed in it the trajectory of metaphysics from Protagoras to Fichte, nay, to the beginnings of Nietzsche; for even this most vehement adversary of the belief in “existences” escaped the consequences of it far less than the consequences of the error outlined just before. — Again, however, it holds that the force of his attack was by no means weakened on that account. “Man has projected his three ‘inner facts,’ that in which he believed most firmly, the will, the spirit, the ego, out of himself; he first took the concept of being out of the concept of the ego, he posited the ‘things’ as being according to his . . concept of the ego as cause: no wonder that later he always found again in things only what he had put into them!” With such words (from G), to which an almost interminable series of others akin in content could be added, Nietzsche not only unmasks, in accordance with truth, a most ancient fiction, but he also, “as the enemy and challenger of God” (MA), takes sides for the reality of becoming and passing away, and hence for that very multiplicity of the inner, so hard to grasp, whose all-too-human character Plutarch believed he had to lament in view of the unity and sameness of the Olympian.

If we had thus at first established only that self-examination had come upon two fundamentally different sides of inwardness, which according to their manifestations to consciousness would deserve to be called the cognitive faculty and

p. 25

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the faculty of lived experience, or, metaphysically speaking, spirit and soul, then the fleeting look back at the peculiar development of the investigation of essence has, if we are not mistaken, incidentally procured us the conviction that of the two sides of inwardness it was only the one, namely the spirit, that prompted the temple saying itself, and that it has accordingly repeated itself countless times that the bearer of the thinking head, after he had without particular effort discovered in himself the — thinking head, and in it, to anticipate a little, the ground of the superiority in power of man over the animals as well as of himself over other men, felt compelled to regard precisely this thinking head as the “true” essence of his own person too and to see the chief task of psychological investigation in its glorification, in relation to which the exploration of souls seemed from the outset to concern only the remainder that is still left over after the detachment of man’s “higher” and ennobling part. And we already suspect, too, that the opponents striving for knowledge of particular characters could hardly avoid understanding the “traits” moralistically as modes of variation of a pattern which had surreptitiously gained its claim to “normality” only thanks to that one-sided narrowing of the general character of mankind. But it is time to recall that the fact of the history of the spirit has exemplified for us in the grandest style what we shall now make vivid in a few of the very simplest cases: the dependence, not indeed of knowledge of human nature, but truly of the science of character on the findings of self-examination.

If we stay for a moment with judging, or better with apprehending, the ground that makes judgment possible, it is evident first of all that it is only a co-experiencing that enables us, say, in that setting of the eyes which we significantly call “fixing,” to perceive not merely the coming to rest of a circling movement but a gaze, and to see in it, without the least interpretation or inference, the degree of tension of the one who is attending. Now as surely as no self-reflection whatever is needed in order, with the apprehension of a face, to apprehend at the same time the state of attention or distraction and even the trait of more than ordinary attentiveness or inattentiveness of its bearer, just as surely would the most finely shaded acquaintance not obtain the least insight into the traits in question! We set foot on the path toward it

p. 26

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

only with the question: what happens to me myself when I gaze, attend, am attentive? And supposing that we were granted only the slight finding that there is a voluntary and an involuntary attention, we would nevertheless have found something lying entirely outside all connoisseurship, which could lead us further toward the ascertaining of connections that fell to the lot of no penetration of others, however comprehensive.

Another example! Even the animals (as later man) know how to make themselves fearsome to one another by enlarging their volumes and, besides, by producing loud noises. Loud noises have something frightening about them, and to every more than ordinary volume, but especially to every more than ordinary extension in height, there clings the quality of the imposing. Now it is one thing, by virtue of such and other expressive features, to assess correctly the means of power of a character and thus to know whom one is dealing with; another again, to ask oneself the question: why does the powerful man actually “impress” me? But should the self-reflection kindled by it press through to the insight that one puts one’s own person in spirit in the place of the powerful man and experiences as peculiarly gladdening the self-feeling thereby heightened, then once again a gain in knowledge would have been achieved which no receptivity directed merely outward could have conveyed.

If finally we turn back to our example from the history of the spirit, then the distinction between spirit and soul, fully worked out since Plato, could have been the answer to the question: does my reflective state coincide with my being alive, and if perhaps not, what is it by which the two stand off from each other? If instead it gives information far more on the question of how one might gain mastery over the (suffering) entanglement in a world of ceaseless change, then it cannot escape us that precisely self-examination, to which we too have granted indispensability, was not protected, despite the utmost acuity of its bearers, from hatching the most momentous errors, and this with a kind of inevitability that must cause us not to believe ourselves immune to them before we have succeeded in finding their place of origin. We need only hold side by side two such mighty dialecticians as Socrates and Descartes in order to see grippingly before our eyes how a self-contemplation that fancies itself sovereign, on the part of two thinkers extremely different in character and by millennia

p. 27

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

removed from one another falls victim, in the labyrinth of inwardness, to the Minotaur of one and the same negation of the soul. For if the former taught that vices, weaknesses, and defects consisted in the absence of insight, from which it would follow that even the most excellent action, if performed without insight and “only from instinct,” is at the very least worthless, and if the latter taught in turn that the emotions are disturbances which befall the (thinking) spirit through “confused” representations, then both, however great the difference of their methods of self-examination, are astonishingly at one in regarding the whole world of feelings as a clouding of inwardness that is to be cleared away, and accordingly in regarding the uncountable wealth of varieties of living characters as, so to speak, a ladder whose topmost rung would be the pure res cogitans, to which the concept of manifoldness, indeed even the concept of plurality, would henceforth find no application at all. And the Sophists, who did indeed seemingly accord the highest esteem to the drives and passions, had secretly fallen prey to the same delusion, in that, for the sake of his living and suffering, they believed they had to deny to man the — faculty of finding truth.

We leave it open for now to what extent even Nietzsche was unable to slip entirely out of the net of these errors, and above all we have not yet got far enough to point out his aim of inquiry and his road toward it; but we can now make recognizable that side of his spiritual attitude which enabled him to do both. He was the first to practice, with an accuracy that nowhere strayed, what even the greatest among the more recent sages were able to exercise only by chance: a self-examination which receives its problems as well as the pointers toward their solution from the sign language of the characters of other egos. Nietzsche discovers his own inwardness insofar as it was able to become for him a mirror of the inwardness of others, and he illuminates the characters of other egos with the help of the self-knowledge gained in this way.

If one takes the word physiognomics in a widest sense, according to which it would mean the doctrine of the appearances of human nature, in particular including all the utterances and precipitates of the human urge to give form, then one might call Nietzsche the greatest physiognomist so far and would then have to add that characterology is possible at all only on a physiognomic

p. 28

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

foundation. Why in doing so he took, with a momentum never before seen in anything like it, the direction toward the psychological investigation of cultures will emerge in what follows. On the other hand, it would exceed the task we set ourselves were we to attempt an answer to the very far-reaching question why what is nowadays called the psychology of culture could never succeed except by leaning on the psychology of significant contemporaries. For this, in the extreme case, a single one suffices, and this one for Nietzsche was undoubtedly Wagner. But this one suffices, after all, to discover one’s own nature in the counter-light of his nature and therewith at the same time its entire content of past; whereas without the one with whose real or merely supposed genius we must at least once have felt inwardly compelled to wrestle and to measure ourselves in a wrestling-match of spirits, one’s own nature would remain undiscovered, and accordingly there could not be discovered either what it, by virtue of its hidden connections with them, might be able to disclose about the nature of peoples, cultures, moralities. Let that now rest. — Before we now demonstrate Nietzsche’s aim of inquiry, we wish to make sure that, and for what reasons, more than one of the true sages, far from paying homage to the belief in some experientia evidentissima of inwardness, was inclined to regard self-knowledge as the most difficult task of human inquiry, if not indeed as something chimerical.

PDF 33

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

BASIC THEME:

SELF-DECEPTION

When Goethe, one of the greatest knowers of themselves who ever lived, meets the possibility of self-knowledge with the most decided mistrust, this will give us more to think about than know-it-all revelations in the mouths of those who still indulge the popular belief in the immediate certainty of consciousness, and it will make us eager for his reasons for it. “Herewith I confess,” he writes in 1823, “that the great and so significant-sounding task: Know thyself, has always seemed suspicious to me, as a ruse of secretly allied priests who wished to confuse man with unattainable demands and . . to lead him astray into a false inward contemplativeness. Man knows himself only insofar as he knows the world . . . I have therefore in riper years paid great attention to how far others might perhaps come to know me, so that in and by them, as in so many mirrors, I might become . . clearer about myself.” A treatise would be required to hold fast all the thoughts that the above sentences could awaken, and a new light would probably fall from them on the almost passionate interest of most people in the judgments of others about their own nature. Here we content ourselves with pointing to the authoritative confirmation which the physiognomic manner of psychological investigation, just circumscribed by us and exemplarily practiced by Nietzsche, has thereby received. Goethe’s well-known remark to Chancellor Müller from the year 1824 is pitched in exactly the same key: “I maintain that man can never come to know himself, never regard himself purely as an object. Others know me better than I know myself.” And he repeats the same thing yet again to Eckermann in 1829. Were one to search his collected work for all the utterances that concern themselves with the problem of self-knowledge, one would hardly find even a single one in contradiction with the conviction of his old age. But if one thinks that

p. 30

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the reflection on things which naturally predominated in Goethe, the man of the eye, is to blame for such skepticism, together with his preference for the active over the contemplative conduct of life, then three verses of Tasso, addressed by the man of the world Antonio to the self-absorbed title hero, teach us that Goethe the thinker had seen through the reasons for the futility of “introspection”:

Inwardly no man learns to know

His innermost, for by his own measure

He gauges himself now too small, and often, alas, too great.

Schopenhauer, who was probably even a better judge of men than a metaphysician, but surely a better psychologist, scattered, especially in his shorter writings, numerous methodologically apt remarks, of which the following deserves our attention: “Why, in spite of all mirrors, does one not really know what one looks like, and therefore cannot call up one’s own person to the imagination as one can that of every acquaintance? a difficulty which already at the first step stands in the way of the γνωθι σαυτον . . . One cannot cast upon one’s own image in the mirror the gaze of estrangement which is the condition of the objectivity of its apprehension; because this gaze, namely, rests ultimately on moral egoism with its deeply felt not-I, which are required in order to perceive all defects . . without deduction, whereby alone the image presents itself faithfully and truly. Instead of this, namely, at the sight of one’s own person in the mirror that very egoism always whispers to us a forestalling ‘this is no not-I, but I,’ which acts as a noli me tangere and prevents the purely objective apprehension . . .” (From Psychologische Bemerkungen § 331.) — Here we find it plainly stated that the false self-estimation which, according to the preceding Tasso verses as well, formed the chief hindrance to self-judgment is something metaphysically unavoidable and threatens to color over even the content of meaning of the objects of perception. Supposing this were so, would not then the character of others too, and in the end the image of mankind itself, be endangered by distortion in the mirror of self-reflection? Not that we incline to mistaken self-valuation, which would evidently happen more on the part of the one than on the part of the other, but only a kind of inescapability of the occurrence would make the science of

p. 31

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

character extremely difficult, if not call its possibility into question.

The immediate object of guild psychology, at least since Aristotle, are, according to the conception of antiquity, which was still correct in this respect, certain processes of life, and according to the erroneous one of modern times, so-called processes of consciousness, but in every case processes, whose distinction, grouping, and connection depends on the extent and the significance of their contributions toward making possible a supposedly highest process, namely the process of the production of knowledge; in accordance with which their foundations, which can perhaps be circumvented verbally but are materially indispensable, appear in the light of abilities, “faculties,” gifts, and all other traits of character form a series ascending from the lower to the higher according to the measure of their differing usefulness for the exercise of the understanding. The immediate object of practical as well as of theoretical wisdom of life, and thus also of academic science insofar as its thinking oversteps the artificially fenced-off special domain by so much as a foot’s breadth4), are the traits of character prefigured by general linguistic usage, whose number is legion, but of which so few take account of intellectualistic needs of classification that science early found occasion to revel in technical terminologies of the science of the soul. Yet, although they are free of the obligation to indicate the degree of their fitness for the increase of knowledge, all names of traits of character without exception — and we may unhesitatingly add: of all languages — carry an accent of value, now only faintly audible, now loudly drowning out the literal sense, but never to be silenced entirely, so that every depiction even of the outer world, as soon as it passes from mere description into characterization, infallibly takes on a value-grading form.

Let one attribute to someone capacity for love, enthusiasm, capacity for devotion, or benevolence, kindness, tact, or tolerance, indulgence, conciliatoriness, or resoluteness, energy, courage, or loyalty, sense of justice, strength of conviction, or self-control, moderation, conscientiousness, or perseverance, endurance, steadfastness, or love of truth, openness, sincerity, or dignity, chivalry, seriousness, or even only clarity, acuity of mind, imagination, and he seems thereby to have been praised; let one attribute to him selfishness, unreceptiveness, envy

p. 32

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

or grudging, malice, gloating or harshness, dullness, cruelty or irresolution, weakness of will, timidity or faithlessness, partiality, unreliability or immoderation, licentiousness, instability or fickleness, lack of independence, seducibility or slyness, suspicion, deceit or arrogance, conceit, shallowness or even only confusion, extravagance of fancy, forgetfulness, and he seems thereby to have been censured. We are wholly unable to speak of human characters without thereby, even against our intention, straightway issuing them a “sort of moral censorship” as well, as Nietzsche once humorously complains in W. It is astonishing with what far-reaching agreement even the very smallest differences of value are caught by everyone versed in the language, and how, in the apprehension of characterizing statements about one’s own nature, the limits of sensitivity to shadings of value seem to widen! Driven by an upsurge that would like to vent itself indignantly or ironically, even intellectual narrowness chooses its words effortlessly and surely according to their content of wounding sharpness and pointedness, and conversely no one without a morbid lack of tact manages, in a state of equanimity, to communicate a statement of a trait having even a merely hinted aftertaste of disparagement to the person concerned in exactly the same words as to a third person; hence many an unconsidered but harmless remark is moved, through tale-bearing, into the lighting-color of falseness.

The peculiarity of the matter stands out still more strikingly when we call to mind that names of personality traits burdened with the strongest emphasis of value lose that emphasis when applied to the mere description of things. For we speak not only of hard rock and soft butter, but also of hard and soft temperaments, not only of warm and cold stoves, but also of warm and cold hearts; likewise of bitter grief, bright joy, hot love, clear mind, gloomy mood, dull torment, etc. But before we go further, we wish to explain the double usage, because the fantastic opinion still widely circulates about it that properties of things could be “transferred” to characters.

Transferences, of which there are plenty and to spare, rest on intuitive similarity of the facts compared through them. If I call the support of a tabletop its foot or leg, I have, by virtue of a vivid

p. 33

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

similarity of function, compared it with the support of a human or animal trunk. The warmth of a stove, on the other hand, has no intuitive similarity whatever with the “warm” red of a peony, the roughness of a pelt none with the “raw” climate of the highlands of Tibet, the sharpness of the knife none with the “sharp” whistle of the locomotive. No one imagines that he could ascertain the warmth of a color, the roughness of a climate, the sharpness of a whistle by touch, as he really has ascertained by touch the properties of things bearing the same names. If, notwithstanding, all names of properties of things are in principle also used in an allegedly transferred meaning, this in truth comes from the fact that all the qualities on which the finding of the properties of things rests can be experienced only and entirely as characters! If I now dip my hand into warm and then into cold water, I experience, together with the difference of the warm and the cold, at the same time the difference of the character of warmth from the character of cold. So little is it any properties of things that would be transferred to characters by some inconceivable process of comparison, that, on the contrary, the activity of the understanding in looking away from and detaching (= abstracting) from the character of the qualities is required in order to find in them the properties of things in the first place! While, therefore, the warmth of sunlight cannot be objectively compared with the warmth of the tints of a painting by Rubens, there does indeed lie in the character of, say, the “warm red” the character of the warmth of the sun, and this character in turn is no other than the one we mean by the warmth of a human heart5).

The more we sever from the words, by the manner of sentence construction, the feeling-colors, which of course can never be extinguished entirely, the more we arrive at that almost expressionless, merely communicating language which hovers before the natural sciences as the model of utterance of the strictest “objectivity.” If, for example, I explain the properties of ice physically, or if I make to someone the remark that the lake is frozen over and tomorrow skating begins, the feeling-arousing content of meaning of the words ice and icy remains below the thresholds of noticeability. But let one speak, however neutrally, merely of “icy winter weather,” and something has already crept in unnoticed of that character of ice which it is the chief task of the expressive use of words in all poetry to bring to bear with suggestive insistence. Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 3

p. 34

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

But if I go so far as to report that the crowd persisted in “icy silence,” then there lies upon the “icy” an accent of value according to which the silence in question passes condemnation on the one at whom it is aimed. — We shall see below that the feeling-colors furnish the foundations for the accents of value. In order, however, not to make the exposition needlessly difficult for ourselves, we distinguish for the present from value-characters in general the particular value-characters which mingle in every designation of the nature of human beings, and we wish to call the genus of lived experience in which the former manifest themselves that of the feeling-colors or feeling-tones, and the genus of lived experience in which the latter manifest themselves that of the clearly pronounced feelings of value. We may regard it as established: if our words in sentence construction lack, as far as at all possible, emphasis of feeling, then they mean — in their meaning lifeless — things; if on the contrary the feeling-colors come forward to stamp the sense, then they mean — in their meaning living — beings. To the question that at once presents itself: what do our words mean in the case of the preponderance of their accents of value, the answer runs: the personal ego.

To banish every doubt about this, let one hold side by side the following double judgments: a chief property of eiderdown is softness, but a chief property of granite is hardness; and: the chief trait of Peter is softness, but the chief trait of Paul is hardness. One convinces oneself at once that the difference of value which was thereby undoubtedly ascribed, among other things, to the two persons Peter and Paul does not already lie, say, in the opposition of meaning of the words softness and hardness; for it is not objective hardness, nor yet the character of hardness, that is detrimental to value, but rather the hardness of character of a personal ego6). — Since the view we hold and which underlies the whole exposition, that the personal ego is a real being and moreover a being differing in principle and metaphysically from all ego-less beings, is not shared theoretically by Nietzsche, we shall at once set forth of the evidence for it at least as much as is required to gain insight into the distinguishing nature of the ego, and in connection with that, presently, to point out the aim toward which Nietzsche’s psychological investigation strove in the most direct line despite his contrary conception of the ego. Before that, however, we must provisionally bring to notice his divergent conception of the ego.

“If I analyze the process that is expressed in the sentence ‘I think,’ I obtain a series of rash assertions,

p. 35

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the grounding of which is difficult, perhaps impossible, for example, that it is I who think, that there must be something at all that thinks, that thinking is an activity and operation on the part of a being that is thought of as a cause, that there is an ‘I,’ finally, that it is already settled what is to be designated by thinking . . . What gives me the right to speak of an I, and even of an I as cause, and finally of an I as cause of thoughts? . . .” It is a falsification to say: “the subject ‘I’ is the condition of the predicate ‘think.’ It thinks: but that this ‘it’ should be precisely that old famous ‘I’ is . . only an assumption . . In the end, even with this ‘it thinks’ too much has been done: even this ‘it’ contains an interpretation of the process and does not belong to the process itself. Here one infers according to grammatical habit: ‘thinking is an activity, to every activity belongs one who is active, consequently —.’ Roughly according to the same schema the older atomism sought, in addition to the ‘force’ that acts, that little lump of matter in which it sits . . the atom . . .” (J). “Language belongs in its origin to the time of the most rudimentary form of psychology: we enter into a crude fetishism when we bring to consciousness the basic presuppositions of the metaphysics of language, in plain German: of reason. It sees everywhere doer and doing, it believes in will as cause in general; it believes in the ‘I,’ in the I as being, in the I as substance . . .” “And the I above all, that has become a fable, a fiction, a play on words: it has entirely ceased to think, to feel, and to will!” (G). It would go badly with his “psychological achievements” had Nietzsche been in earnest about the opinion indicated above, which he expresses in similar fashion in many places. But although it too sprang from the central error which divided his thinking as such by an irremovable self-contradiction, and in particular helped not a little to strengthen the dose of genuine skepticism in his work, he nonetheless conducts himself as a thinker throughout in accordance with the exactly opposite way of seeing, which we are now preparing to support with the ground that alone is already able to bear it, and in particular he unceasingly gave the lie to the mistrust of grammar and language here displayed, in that he allowed himself to be most happily fertilized, with a receptivity scarcely ever seen before, by language above all.

We leave it undecided whether, and with what means of thought, Heracliteanism could be perfected into the metaphysical central concept

3*

p. 36

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of a substrate-less happening; for there is no doubt that the process of the formation of judgment, and all the more that of the declaration of judgment, which is moreover inseparable from it, springs from a being of its own that is capable of judgment. Nietzsche too does not mean, and cannot mean, that the universe, say, or space or time thinks, and still less does he mean that thinking lacks a place of origin.
