When he speaks of the soul “as a social structure of the drives and affects” or favors a hundredfold turns of phrase such as this one: “A man who wills commands something in himself that obeys . .” (J), then he has not only readmitted the outlawed “subject” but has even conceived it as an analogue of the ego; to say nothing at all of the fact that his favorite formula of the “will to power” would naturally lose all meaning without the assumption of actively rivaling centers. — But the boundness of all formation of judgment to a place in space and to a point in time, or more briefly to a Now and Here, would hold no less of every process of life and would therefore not yet be suited at all to prove the existence of the ego. Since what matters is of course not the name, which we could indeed replace by other names, but the thing the name is meant to signify, we fittingly designate the something capable of judgment by X and would accordingly have to state what must be added to the living individual being in order that it may deserve to be called an individual being capable of thought. Now the concept of the living individual being in any case exhibits three relational marks, none of which could be lacking without its dissolving into vapor: the living individual being has a living body; it lives, as long as it lives, without interruption; and it is a temporal reality, in accordance with which every following moment of its being alive is co-determined by all the preceding moments and thus also differs in kind from each of them. To the general concept of the continuity of localized processes of life, the particular conditions of sensibility and self-movement, of the recognitions, re-recognitions, and modes of behavior which the animal shares with us, are related as the weft to the warp of the fabric; but no merely temporal vitality would of itself arrive at the production of the act of judgment, as the following simple consideration teaches.

If someone has judged at moment a, reflecting on things: there stands a tree, then at moment b he can judge, reflecting on himself: I remember that a tree stood there. The continuity of life processes makes possible animal recognitions and re-recognitions; it makes possible

p. 37

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

not the thought of sameness. If the bearer of life, because embedded in temporal reality, is at moment b in any case no longer the same as he was at moment a, how then could he relate the later judgment to the earlier judgment, and how, still more, even if the miraculous intervention of a god were to help him to this as well, could he imagine that he, the one now relating, is the same as the one relating at moment a, when it is rather the otherness of his vitality that would become objective to him! To Nietzsche’s question: what gives me the right to speak of an ego, there follows the counter-question: supposing that were a delusion, how do you propose to make the possibility of such a delusion intelligible from a thoroughly transient being? Nietzsche was right to regard the concept of being and of the thing as a self-projection of the ego; but it can be explained only by a tendency if, in his effort to dissolve that which projects as well, he seems not to notice at all the question of how, out of nothing but processes, the delusion could brew in man that he is, and how in this way a being of an entirely processual kind was enabled by self-projection to invent an entirely being-like world! Here, but also only here, the ontological proof holds good7). The possibility of existence of the thought of sameness compellingly requires that there really inhere in its bearer an X that remains identical with itself in the stream of time; and precisely this we call its egohood.

The objections of the whole and half “sensualists” present themselves as misunderstandings that would be peculiarly foolish were it not for the prejudices underlying them: thus, for example, the reference to the indubitable interruptedness of ego-consciousness, since ego-consciousness no more coincides with the ego (as substrate) than reflection upon a thing coincides with the thing itself; or the remark, correct in and of itself, that the feeling of self is subject to constant fluctuations, reporting now elation, now dejection, which we shall have to treat in great detail. But however differently someone may feel himself constituted in his states, and however much he may change in judging his traits, he always and necessarily refers the states as well as the traits to one selfsame something, namely his ego, and thus to something that persists, constant in time, through the change of states. Just as we, faced with the object of perception, a tree for instance, must

p. 38

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

abstract from it not only the color of the lighting, the momentary movement of the leaves and branches, the condition corresponding to the season, but also our particular “view” of the moment, in order to arrive at the tree-thing itself, so we grasp the egohood of our own personality only by removing all states and traits, and finally egohood “in itself” by de-localizing the remaining residue. Such abstract reflection upon the ego is, however, unmistakably one and the same with reflection upon existence or reflection upon being-there, and reflection upon being-there is in turn one and the same with reflection upon identical persistence or upon duration of existence. I cannot think of myself as an ego without thinking that I am (= exist), and I cannot think that I exist without lending my existing a (longer or shorter) duration. — Since a deepening of this proposition in terms of the science of consciousness is no longer planned at this point, let it merely be remarked here, without proof, that duration, constancy, persistence, regardless of whether they are thought of as referring to the really constant egohood of a person or to the seemingly constant thinghood of an occasion of perception, must not be confused with continuity. Continuous are, without exception, processes, whereas with “constancy,” “persistence,” “duration” the extratemporal condition of what really or supposedly endures is referred to what is temporally changeable8). —

With the demonstration of the difference of the ego from the vitality or the soul of the individual being, its difference from the body of the individual being has already been demonstrated as well. If a man of forty declares: I was in South America at the age of twenty, he certainly cannot mean his present body, in which not one atom more would be found of the body that served his ego, if you will, as a lodging twenty years ago; and just as little, if he considers it rightly, can he mean his soul, which is surely no longer the soul of the youth and which at bottom no one doubts accompanies, with growing, blossoming, and withering, the growth and withering of the body, exactly as Plutarch movingly described it above of man himself. If he nonetheless means by that ego which was in South America twenty years ago something identical with his ego of today, something that he — sanity presupposed — would run no risk of confusing with anyone or anything in the world, then he has, of course, involuntarily posited in his ceaselessly changing vitality a real point of

p. 39

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

reference that through all the transformations of the latter remained one and the same and past which time literally runs by. Here we have the archetype of being-there. If we strip from it the fetter of the here and now, there remains abstract being, which Parmenides discovered and with whose help late mysticism interpreted the essence of Apollo. — If, accordingly, a changing vitality and the unchangeable ego (or, metaphysically speaking, a soul appearing in space and time and the spirit beyond space and time) are coupled together in conflict in the personal individual being, then the person experiences, in relation to the unceasing passing-away of psychic reality, its ego or self as something that resists the stream of perishing. Or: the feeling of existence is inescapably attended by the impulse toward assertion of existence. From reflection upon the latter Nietzsche won his “will to power,” which he handles feature by feature like a subject striving for power, but then, out of deficient insight into the nature of the ego and out of — other reasons, regrettably carries over into the extra-human world.

Since we shall have ample opportunity to show what is misleading in the formula of the will to power, we do not neglect to urge in its praise that in the hand of its master it rendered excellent service toward the shattering of the Darwinian and almost universally biological legend of a world-pervading “drive for self-preservation,” to which we must, for other reasons, give a sidelong glance. Supposing our foregoing derivation carries weight, then there are of course self-preservations only where there are selves, thus only in personal beings, and the smuggling of self-preservations into the animals, to say nothing of the plants, would have misunderstood their stirrings one and all in the sense of calculating intentionality and thus poisoned the innocence of nature to the bone. Conversely, however, he would fall into an error no less pernicious who wished to equate the assertion of existence inseparable from the ego with the animal drives of provision, which, from lack of space or superabundance of germs — take it as you will — occasion countless struggles on this planet already in the animal kingdom (a state of affairs that belongs among the “contingent,” i.e. metaphysically underivable, ones), or even merely with the personal egoisms, of which, strictly speaking, the will to power, namely in the meaning of the drive to dominate, would be one variety. — To facilitate

p. 40

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the further exposition we insert a few definitions of concepts.

Animals have merely drives, and these are vital causes of movement; human beings likewise have drives, but chiefly mainsprings, a name by which we Germanize the foreign word interest, and these are causes of will. More precisely, the mainspring is the dispositional precondition of a generically delimitable direction of will. In the main mainsprings of a given person we have to seek what we just called the assertion of existence; for a main mainspring deserves its name precisely insofar as the ego of its bearer identifies itself with it. Since the main mainspring of, say, a passionate researcher is his interest in knowledge, his self-assertion manifests itself in the tenacity with which he holds fast to his theoretical convictions, possibly sacrificing to them his bodily well-being and his economic existence. A single example of this kind, to which a hundred others could be added, reveals to us the fundamental difference not only of drive from interest in general, but above all also of the animal drive of provision from the tendency toward assertion of existence of an individual being whose soul revolves, as it were, around a spiritual axis, and at the same time discloses to us in what respect something correct hovered before our philosopher when he, who started entirely from the essence of man, saw fit to interpret the assertion of existence in the sense of a will to power. Inasmuch as the researcher of whom we spoke identifies himself with his theoretical convictions, he must seek to refute and to that extent to eliminate all convictions incompatible with them; and inasmuch as nothing in any given state of affairs stimulates him so powerfully as to penetrate it spiritually, he strives for a kind of dominion over the whole world, which he unquestionably wants to make his spiritual property. He has, as Nietzsche unsurpassably puts it, the “will to the thinkability” of all that is real.

Once one has grasped that ego-consciousness requires the existence of an ego, that in relation to the temporally changeable soul of its bearer the ego is something that persists constant in time, and finally that the personal individual being accordingly must meet its lived experiences (= its undergoings) with acts of assertion of existence, then it takes only a few steps of reflection to see why every character trait of the

p. 41

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

person bears an accent of value and why consciousness of the ego can by no means be separated from consciousness of the ego’s worth. For the sake of clarity we shall divide the path thither, short though it is, into two stages. — Everyone is accustomed to keep willing and being able apart, or the intention and its realization. One is wont to stress this in regard to the struggles and contests of men among one another; one transfers it with special fondness to the technical and sporting violation of the landscape (cf. the “assault” on Mount Everest, the “conquest” of Mont Blanc, the “conquest” of the ocean of air, and whatever other such self-important displays of the human will to prevail there may be); but one is not always wont to take it correspondingly into account for the fact of self-activity in general.

If I will to solve a problem, I shall succeed or not succeed; if I will to master myself, I shall succeed or not succeed; if I will to humble myself, I shall be able to or not be able to; if I will, after the model of Buddhist monks, to deaden my soul against the stimulus value of all sensory impressions, I shall find that it depends on other powers, as foreign and unsuited to my willing as the headwind to a sailor, whether and how far it succeeds; if I will to forget or to fall asleep or in some other way to slip out of ego-consciousness as out of a prison, it will prove no different! Even if, then, the mortification of willing or the renunciation of assertion of existence were my appointed goal: my willing directed toward it as well cannot help proving itself powerful or powerless. Since the entire external world is mediated to consciousness only through life processes belonging to the ego, and since it is again life processes belonging to the ego with whose help every effect of consciousness upon the external world takes place, we have to seek the opposition and conflict in the personal soul itself and see ourselves compelled first of all to the decision: if the ego, in relation to the foundation of ever-flowing experience, more briefly in relation to the soul, is a tendency toward assertion, then in the reflection of consciousness its being-there will become for it a constant alternation of victory and defeat, of ability and inability, of power and powerlessness, indeed, to put it more boldly, of being and not-being; more briefly, a constant alternation of experienced heightening or diminution of its being-there itself.

What we have thereby characterized is the feeling of self or, more correctly,

p. 42

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the ego-side of all feelings; for logically, of course, mere being-there can be neither heightened nor diminished! There is scarcely a more vivid proof of the extraordinary significance of the feelings of self within the vital range of the personality than the memorable fact that the opposition of pleasure and displeasure peculiar to them was confused with the polarities of the feelings of life, or rather of the life-side of all feelings, and that it was imagined that the experiences of the soul antagonistic to the ego could be resolved into satisfactions and dissatisfactions of a drive for self-preservation or else of a tendency toward power. (Compare, say, Spinoza, Hume, Herbart, Schopenhauer, Lipps!) To repel such nihilism, it is advisable to determine, by means of a slight extension of the meaning of the words success and failure: feelings of pleasure are feelings of success, feelings of displeasure feelings of failure; for then no one would any longer be likely to run the risk of equating pleasure and displeasure henceforth with the sensory polarity of voluptuousness and dread, the psychic polarity of rapture and horror! If I miss the train that matters to me, there follows a feeling of displeasure, even if a quickly fading one; if I catch it after all, contrary to expectation, say because it was late, there follows a feeling of pleasure, even if a quickly fading one. But he would be a pitiable starveling at the table of life who could seriously persuade himself that the pleasure in question is one and the same with sensory voluptuousness or psychic exuberance! So much, however, remains true: the personal individual being can feel pleasure but also displeasure at the thought, for example, of the experience of sensory voluptuousness, and indeed one or the other actually occurs, according as the bearer of consciousness counts the capacity for experiencing sensory voluptuousness among the valuable or the disvaluable traits of his character! With this we have reached the first stage of our path and pause for a moment to look around.

Little as there is an individual being that would be ego or pure spirit through and through, insight into the ego-nature of the person nonetheless suffices to explain that generalization and grading of its conditions of life without which the essentially measuring concept of value could not have arisen. Because mere assertion of existence is without quality, it seems to adhere indiscriminately to any bearer of an ego whatever, and because its conditions, exactly like the physical forces modeled after them, are comparable with one another only from the standpoint of a more or less of capacity for assertion,

p. 43

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

then the stronger capacity for assertion would impress the bearer of the weaker even if mankind were de-vitalized to the point of no longer knowing anything of qualitative values! Even a Richard III, of whom we hear that he is determined to prove a villain, and who approves each of his villainies, would find himself prompted, thanks to his egohood, either to admire or to envy the more successful criminal, and would thereby attest belief in the worth of certain traits with whose help one emerged unchallenged from the most gruesome misdeeds. For him, too, there would still be a worthiness of existence without which he could not manage, even though it consisted only in his certainty of being able also to accomplish the infamies he had in mind. Should criminality, to be sure, in time become not merely general but even legitimate, then a recurring Nietzsche would hardly a second time burn incense to the Cesare Borgias and might instead revise his doctrine of the will to power in a few points. —

One follows, to be sure, a correct feeling when one ordinarily suspects the place of origin of concepts of value to be precisely not in selfishness, and it might be disputed whether the esteeming merely of ability, of capacity, of power, thus with complete indifference to the kind of ends, still falls under valuations proper. But what lends these their special stamp, in consequence of which they are wont to quarrel with the personal egoisms, lies not so much in their universality independent of the person as rather in the fact that the claim to validity is occasioned by qualities and signifies realities that stem not from the assertion of existence (of the spirit) but from the life-side of the personality; with which we have entered upon the second part of our path. — We need only recall the life-side of the person, which is never entirely absent, in order to grasp that only the capacity for life can secure to the will to assertion the capacity for existence. From this there results a translation, fluctuating according to times, cultures, and individual beings, of the compulsions of experience into counter-demands of the spirit, which signify a barrier for the naked will to assertion of the personal ego and whose capacity for fulfillment (within the limits of their audibility) with the help of the traits of the personal character henceforth forms for the latter the indispensable condition of its worth, now understood in the narrowest

p. 44

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

sense of the word. The oppositions of value thus indicated could accordingly not have arisen without the polarized qualities of life of brightness and darkness, warmth and cold, fullness and emptiness, etc., which make themselves known in corresponding colorings of feeling; and they could again not have arisen without a translation of such qualities into the language of assertion of the ego that strives for their spiritualization, which makes itself known in claims to universal validity that are in turn charged with feeling. If the inner conflict of the personal individual being thus unmistakably splits even its ego-side, then we may surmise in advance that the will to assertion, riven within itself, will seek ways out and try to forestall the threats to its equilibrium by falsifications of values. —

The personal individual being must be able to believe in something in itself, or it would perish. The person’s knowledge of traits of its own that undermine its belief in the real or supposed capacity for life of its ego takes from it some of the capacity through which it exists as an ego, the capacity to assert itself. Reflection upon the personal ego is therefore inseparable from reflection upon its worth or worthlessness, which the German language confirms in that by Selbstbewußtsein (self-consciousness) it in fact means Selbstwertbewußtsein (consciousness of one’s own worth). Since there was and is no mortal whose willing, as deemed permissible or obligatory by him, would at all times stand in equilibrium with his ability, and since there are even countless mortals whose accomplishment habitually falls short of the willing they deem permissible or obligatory, the self-esteem of every human being is exposed to attacks now stronger, now weaker, now only occasional, now very often repeated, from which it emerges now victorious, now defeated. Were it to succumb completely for a time, so that its bearer believed he saw in himself no traits of worth at all and instead exclusively disvaluable traits, he would fall into the state of despair, a word that we must look at more closely for a moment. In verzweifeln (to despair), formed by a prefix from zweifeln (to doubt) (which for its part belongs together with zwei, “two,” and Zwist, “discord,” and leads back to the Indo-Germanic root dwo), the negative side of doubt is so overemphasized that one may well say that one who despairs doubts everything and everyone, or that he no longer “believes” in anything at all. Is this now supposed to mean, say, that he no longer believes in the

p. 45

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

existence of the earth or of the sun or of his own body, or else that he doubts as a Protagoras, Aristippus, Pyrrho, Timon, Carneades, Aenesidemus, Sextus Empiricus, Montaigne, Charron, Hume doubted? Certainly not! He doubts none of all this; he merely, but to the very ground, doubts his own worth. If on that account utter despair now takes hold of him, we see it thereby convincingly confirmed that after the loss of self-esteem the assertion of existence of the personality dwindles away and can be preserved from collapse by no reference to objects of the external world and their values. The state of feeling called “despair” can therefore also be experienced only by a spiritual individual being, thus by man alone, and not at all by the animal, whose extreme fear of death bears not the slightest resemblance to it. Indeed, every despair that lasts to any degree leads inescapably to suicide, which is in every case actually murder of the ego and, merely for the purpose of extinguishing consciousness, destroys its vital foundations along with it; by which, however, it should be remarked in passing, it is by no means meant to be said that suicide must always spring from despair. One can depart from life voluntarily with unbroken self-esteem, be it that one is weary of life, be it that one wants to forestall worse evils. Type, say: the Stoic of antiquity, who for these or similar reasons calmly opens his veins.

If, accordingly, every doubt of one’s own worth is on the way to a goal that, if reached, would be unbearable, then we understand that by virtue of its tendency toward assertion of existence the ego must be intent on blocking this path. This is now accomplished above all in two ways: through the use of narcotics and through self-deception. Of the drive for narcosis we have to remark that it aims at a change in the objects of consciousness and, all things considered, strives for flight from the reality of things into the reality of dreams. This flight, however, is ambiguous. Either it takes place for its own sake and then has as little to do with the question of self-esteem as, say, rapturous enthusiasm for nature; or it really takes place for the purpose of banishing gnawing doubts; but then it is an underivable means to this only in those cases where its purpose is known to the narcotic user, whereas in all other cases it is merely a special manifestation of the main means

p. 46

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which alone we shall keep in view henceforth, namely self-deception.

If a Goethe, a Schopenhauer believed they had to understand the difficulty, if not impossibility, of self-knowledge in terms of errors of self-esteem, we have now ascertained from the nature of the ego why such a thing could even appear unavoidable; and if, in turn, self-examination cannot be dispensed with for the interpretation of all the data that we gained through the apprehension of the characters of the other ego, indeed of the characters of beings without an ego, then characterology itself is called into question by the propensity toward falsification of one’s own worth. If, for example, I overestimate myself, I thereby underestimate the characters of other persons, and if man as such overestimates himself, he thereby underestimates all extra-human beings and falsifies the total picture of the world. Far more pernicious, finally, than both, and beyond all conception in its effects, is the falsification of measures and weights, without the presupposition of which the falsification of one’s own worth, whether of the individual or of mankind as a whole, would generally not succeed at all! — The train of thought has, however, also revealed to us that we are capable of becoming aware of the fact of self-deception and of its motives; and if we can become aware of them, we can also make them the object of inquiry and try to trace the secret paths on which it succeeded and succeeds in whitewashing the images of human nature, the images of the nature of reality, with illusory images that flatter the spirit’s hunger for power. Nietzsche was the first (one exception excepted) to grasp fundamentally and thoroughly that the science of essential nature is possible only to the extent of the decomposition of all fictions of the drive for recognition and the craving for significance, and he thereby became, in the sense mentioned at the outset, the “first psychologist.” (We shall come later to the single exception and also to the respect in which it is not one.) He recognized that there can be neither a personal nor a general self-knowledge, and then also no science of character, unless one has first seen through and thereby annihilated all self-deceptions.

Before we characterize his aim of inquiry more precisely, we add a few explanatory remarks to what has been said so far. All self-deceptions are deceptions about one’s own worth, and every deception about one’s own worth consists in this, that one attributes to oneself a trait of supposed worth and thereby

p. 47

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

denies oneself an opposite trait of supposed worthlessness. It is not a matter of indifference for the science of the soul whether it was more the craving to possess a value or more the flight from possessing a disvalue that brought about the deception: the result, however, remains the same. If a timid man holds himself to be courageous out of esteem for courage, he believes at the same time in the absence of timidity; if he holds himself to be not timid out of aversion to timidity, he believes at the same time in the presence of some degree of courage. For reasons that we shall touch on later, Nietzsche’s reflections take their departure overwhelmingly from man’s experience of inadequacy and seek the chief source of all deceptions in the avoidance of knowledge of his deficiencies and supposed basenesses.

The following is further to be noted. If someone deliberately speaks untruth, he deceives another, but not himself. If, on the other hand, he deceives himself, then it cannot be he, and so it must have been an It, a drive, a need, or, to speak with Nietzsche, a “state of distress,” that instilled in him the belief in something not so present, much as he could instil it in another by lying. The process of deception thus always runs its course on this side of consciousness, and only its result is a “fact of consciousness.” Thanks to his passionate hatred of the motives of self-deception, which he churned up down to their undercurrents, Nietzsche calls the same thing that he elsewhere designates as self-deception, self-delusion, self-blinding, etc., with special fondness also “mendaciousness,” which would at any rate be compatible with the current sense of the word, but at times even “lie,” which in linguistic usage would mean the deliberate distortion of truth; but he means without exception the “unconscious” and by no means the conscious and genuine lie. “For let there be no deception on this point,” it says in GM, “what constitutes the most proper mark of modern souls . . is not the lie, but the innocence ingrained in moralistic mendaciousness . . . Our educated people of today, our ‘good men,’ do not lie, that is true; but it does them no honor! The real lie, the genuine, resolute, ‘honest’ lie . . would be something far too severe, too strong for them; it would demand what one may not demand of them, that they open their eyes to themselves, that they know how to distinguish between ‘true’ and ‘false’ in themselves. What alone befits them is the dishonest lie. Everything that today feels itself to be

p. 48

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

a ‘good man’ is completely incapable of taking any stand on any matter other than dishonestly mendacious . . innocently mendacious . . virtuously mendacious.” Or: “I call it a lie: to not want to see something that one sees, to not want to see something as one sees it: whether the lie takes place before witnesses or without witnesses is of no account. The most common lie is the one with which one lies to oneself; lying to others is relatively the exceptional case” (A). The same is found in many passages of his works.

But, one might ask: can one not also underestimate oneself? Certainly! But we shall learn that self-underestimation may, according to Nietzsche, be understood only as a — indeed quite frequent — means of overestimation. Here the following hints may suffice. One underestimates oneself out of an overstrained need for significance, and with this, too, one compensates for unacknowledged feelings of insufficiency, even though this does not always come to light as glaringly as on the part of the fanatics of confession, who spread out their basenesses in order to parade their courage of truthfulness. Indeed, self-abasement, and in particular that before one’s own consciousness, thus at first without a sidelong glance at applause, infallibly forms the reverse side of a self-exaltation that would not otherwise have been for sale and with which an experienced insufficiency without exception conceals itself: for he who abases himself before a phantasm, for example that of his god, thereby partakes precisely of a glory that is literally “above all criticism.” Let us recall already here the saying: “He that humbleth himself shall be exalted!” All humility of “holiness” is, according to Nietzsche, unconscious sanctimony. “For when a belief is more useful, more effective, more convincing than conscious hypocrisy, hypocrisy instinctively and forthwith becomes innocence: first principle for the understanding of great saints” (G).

Turning thus to a sharper characterization, we raise three questions in succession: whether Nietzsche developed a theory of self-deceptions — whether predecessors are to be named and in what respect he offered something fundamentally new in relation to them — whether his goal, the uncovering of all self-deceptions, does not perhaps signify something merely negative. — On the first question: He developed no theory of self-deceptions; but he applied the aids he found for tracking down self-deception in every form so far-reachingly, successfully, and penetratingly that for the researchers inheriting from him

p. 49

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

not much more would remain to be done in that respect than to draw out the fundamental findings and to link them with one another in thematic unity. But should one award the highest prize even to a coming theorist of self-deception who found an exemplarily simple formula that would permit all conceivable self-deceptions to be derived from one principle (and we hold such a formula to be capable of being set up), then let it not be forgotten that the Newtons are always possible only after the precedence of the Copernicuses, Galileis, and Keplers, whose discoveries do not cease to be the more decisive ones because they were made without knowledge of the “law” that was to be abstracted from them.

The second question we have of course not raised in order, in the manner popular of late, to trace Nietzsche’s relations to all possible thinkers of the previous history, with which a few volumes could be filled in the turn of a hand, but it is to serve us as a springboard to the peculiarities of his aim of inquiry and of the methods developed in its service. We therefore take up some things seemingly related and also really opposed, not in chronological order, but according to their capacity to illuminate Nietzsche’s spiritual attitude feature by feature. — Here we interpose: supposing the value-accent of certain traits proves to be a falsification by a need for power of the spirit not otherwise to be satisfied, then the uncovering of this would coincide without further ado with a revaluation of the traits in question, and the result would nonetheless bear the character merely of a piece of knowledge. Now although Nietzsche is fond of using such disclosures for demands on a better-instructed mankind, with which, however, we have no immediate concern, still no one will on that account place him beside those schools of philosophers whose investigations of the soul, worthy of recognition as they are, were avowedly meant to serve a kind of practical wisdom of life. If we nonetheless compare him, say, with Stoics or Epicureans, namely in consideration of their many acute pronouncements about man, then by contrast characteristic novelties of Nietzschean thought at once stand out.

Both groups, whose mutual gibes are to be understood more from similarity than from dissimilarity, teach in agreement: Learn to endure life and let nothing rob you of your composure Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 4

p. 50

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

; for with the Stoics, to be sure, the mask of equanimity worn for show is wont to conceal only poorly the resolute will to subjugate life. “Si vis tibi omnia subjicere (!) te subice rationi,” Seneca declares, treacherously enough; to “true freedom,” according to Epictetus, leads “contempt for everything that is not in our power” (!), and even the seemingly so resigned Marcus Aurelius confesses: “To my soul everything is indifferent that is not an effect of it!” Far from lingering even a minute with the hitherto customary consideration of what might be praised, what blamed in such principles, and what value belongs to them for the apprehension of the characters of man, Nietzsche asks: What, so you find life unbearable? You consider yourselves entitled to criticize it and to recommend to us means of thought by which one might more or less — be rid of it? Should that not indicate that something was not in order with you! “These wisest men of all ages, one ought first to look at them from close by! Were they perhaps all of them no longer steady on their legs? . . . Did wisdom perhaps appear on earth as a raven, enthused by a faint smell of carrion?” (G).

Only in the second part can we uncover what is really the matter with this “life,” of which we hear that its value “cannot be estimated . ., not by one who is alive, because such a one is a party, indeed even the object of contention . ., not by one who is dead, for another reason” (G), and whose unconditional value is nevertheless evidently presupposed by Nietzsche. But we can nonetheless already state here the guiding thought that, however it may be grounded in that estimation of life, prompts our thinker to assume from the outset that all rules of wisdom are ultimately rooted in a self-deception of their inventors. “Nothing goes more against a philosopher’s taste than man insofar as he wishes. If he sees man only in his doing, if he sees this bravest, most cunning, most enduring animal, astray even in labyrinthine predicaments, how admirable man appears to him! . . . But the philosopher despises wishing man, also the ‘desirable’ man, and in general all desirabilities, all ideals of man. If a philosopher could be a nihilist, he would be one because he finds nothingness behind all the ideals of man. Or not even nothingness, but only the worthless . . all kinds of dregs from the drained cup of life” (G).

p. 51

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

If we wished to give grounds, in Nietzsche’s sense, for the tendency here indicated toward the decomposition of all (reality-fleeing) desirabilities or ideals, it might be said, say: insofar as there is merely wishing, the wish-image in question bears witness to an unfulfillable need, a state of distress of the will to assertion, or in Nietzsche’s language of the will to power; and insofar as positings, opinions, valuations proceed from such wishes, they are fictions with which a semblance of fulfillment passes itself off as fulfillment itself. In this way, however, a systematic falsification of essential nature takes place; or: judged values of the ego are one and all illusory values with which the consciousness of the will to power cheats itself over its experienced disvalues (= incapacities for life). From this there results a heuristic principle. We may not ask, as hitherto: what feeling expresses itself in a desirability, even if it should enjoy the most general approval; but we must ask: what lack hides behind this feeling’s need for ideals? Or, with a slight variation of a winged word, spoken for once as flatly as possible: what wish was here the father of holding-for-true? — Some pronouncements of Nietzsche and applications to be named in anticipation will let us surmise the quite extraordinary scope of the principle.

“If I have anything over all psychologists, it is this, that my eye is sharper for that most difficult and most insidious kind of backward inference, in which most mistakes are made, the backward inference from the work to the author, from the deed to the doer, from the ideal to the one who needs it, from every way of thinking and valuing to the need that commands behind it” (NcW). “Success has always been the greatest liar, and the work, too, the deed is a success. The great statesman, the conqueror, the discoverer is disguised in his creations, hidden to the point of unrecognizability; the work, that of the artist, of the philosopher, invents* only the one who created it . .” (NcW). “A Homer would not have created an Achilles, a Goethe no Faust, if Homer had been an Achilles, if Goethe had been a Faust” (GM). Let it be noted already here that Nietzsche either forgets or, what is more probable, deliberately avoids adding the converse: a Faust would not have “languished in enjoyment for desire” and stormed onward “unsatisfied every moment,” provided that he, like Goethe, had

4*

p. 52

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

been able to — write a Faust! “Our belief in others betrays in what we would like to believe in ourselves” (Z). “There where our deficiencies lie, our enthusiasm indulges itself. The enthusiastic maxim ‘Love your enemies’ had to be invented by Jews, the best haters there have ever been; and the most beautiful glorification of chastity has been written by those who in their youth lived dissolutely and abominably” (M). Or with the whole pathos of Nietzschean eloquence: “Have you ever asked yourselves sufficiently how dearly the erection of every ideal on earth has been paid for? How much reality had to be slandered and misjudged for it each time, how much conscience disturbed, how much ‘God’ sacrificed each time?” (GdT). The sum: “ideals” are “lies pronounced holy” (M). To this a few samples of applications in the narrower sense.

Rousseauian cult of nature: “Oppressed and half crushed by haughty castes . . corrupted by bad education and ashamed before himself through ridiculous customs, man in his distress calls upon ‘holy nature’ and suddenly feels that it is as far from him as any Epicurean god . . . And when he cries: ‘. . only natural man is human,’ he despises himself and longs to go beyond himself . .” (U). Will to science: “Your knowledge does not complete nature, but only kills your own. Just measure once your height as a knower against your depth as one who is able” (U). “The will to a system is a lack of integrity” (G). Belief in a beyond and “better life”: “To make up fables about a world other than this one has no sense at all, provided that an instinct of slander, belittlement, suspicion of life is not powerful in us: in the latter case we avenge ourselves on life with the phantasmagoria of an ‘other,’ a ‘better life’” (G). “What is a beyond for, if not as the means to besmirch this side?” (G). Concept of guilt: “Guilt is sought everywhere where there is a failure; for this brings with it a dejection against which the only remedy is involuntarily applied: a new excitation of the feeling of power; and this is found in the condemnation of the ‘guilty one.’ This guilty one is . . the victim of the weak, the humiliated, the dispirited, who want to prove to themselves by something that they still have strength. Condemning oneself, too, can be a means of helping oneself to the feeling of strength after a defeat” (M). We

p. 53

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

shall examine only in the second part Nietzsche’s most peculiar and illuminating relation to the will to truth or to knowledge; but we offer already here some evidence of the audacious doubts to which his method of disillusionment led him with respect to the will to truth.

It lies in the essence of what we call truth or knowledge that no truth can be the all-embracing truth, no knowledge the very last knowledge. If, nevertheless, something like a definitive fulfillment and satisfaction now hovers before our drive for knowledge, beyond which nothing could lure us any longer to further efforts at knowledge, then “truth” too has become a mere desirability, and the will to knowledge works in the service of an ideal. But then we should have to ask: what incapacity of the will to power has disguised itself as the belief in truth? “This unconditional will to truth: what is it? Is it the will not to let oneself be deceived? Is it the will not to deceive?” Nietzsche proves that it is not the will not to let oneself be deceived, and then continues: “Thus the belief in science, which after all indisputably exists, cannot have taken its origin from such a calculus of utility, but rather in spite of the fact that the . . dangerousness . . of ‘truth at any price’ is continually proven to it . . Consequently ‘will to truth’ means . . ‘I will not deceive, not even myself,’ and with this we stand on the ground of morality. For one need only ask oneself thoroughly: ‘why do you not want to deceive?’, especially if it should seem — and it does seem! — as if life were designed for semblance, I mean for error, fraud, dissimulation, blinding, self-blinding . . Such a resolve might perhaps be . . a quixotism, a little enthusiastic madness; but it might also be something worse, namely a principle hostile to life, destructive. ‘Will to truth’ — that could be a concealed will to death . . . There is no doubt, the truthful man, in that audacious and ultimate sense which the belief in science presupposes, affirms thereby another world than that of life . . and . . must he not by that very fact deny this world, our world?” (F). And so that one may gauge how dangerous the crowbar that Nietzsche wields becomes even to the tempered-steel safe compartments of professional philosophy,

p. 54

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we offer the following passage as well: “How are synthetic judgments a priori possible, Kant asked himself; and what did he really answer? By virtue of a faculty . . . But how does opium make one sleep? ‘By virtue of a faculty,’ namely the virtus dormitiva, answers that doctor in Molière . . But such answers belong in comedy, and it is finally time to replace the Kantian question . . by another question: ‘why is the belief in such judgments necessary?’ . . . Or, to speak plainly and coarsely and thoroughly: synthetic judgments a priori should not ‘be possible’ at all: we have no right to them, in our mouths they are nothing but false judgments. Only, to be sure, the belief in their truth is necessary, as a foreground belief . . which belongs to the perspective optics of life” (J).

We shall set out later to what extent Nietzsche actually partakes of genuine skepticism, to what extent not, and wherein lies the sense but also the nonsense of a fundamentally doubting disposition of the spirit. The latter, be it remarked in passing, logicians and epistemologists have long since brought to light; the former, so far as we see, has never been sufficiently disclosed, and least of all by the — skeptics themselves. In view of the utterances just heard, however, we are occupied here with the question of how, in means and goal, Nietzsche’s spiritual attitude differs from the spiritual attitude of the skeptics, to whom he surely stands somewhat closer than to the most positive among the wise of life. In doing so, however, we leave aside the chiefly epistemological skepticism of antiquity and, all the more, the merely epistemological skepticism of a Hume, whose relation to Nietzsche’s kind of doubting of truth can be clarified only after a thorough illumination of the latter, and keep to the more recent Frenchmen such as Montaigne, Charron, De la Rochefoucauld, whom Nietzsche himself in part mentions, now dismissively, but now also appreciatively. He has, let it be said in advance, probably overestimated his kinship with them.

As regards first the typical Renaissance skeptics such as Montaigne or Charron, they are separated from Nietzsche by an unbridgeable gulf already because — in Kant’s famous formula — they “wanted to abolish knowledge in order to make room for faith.” If one objected that behind Nietzsche’s destruction of pretensions to knowledge there also stands a faith, it would have to be replied that in relation

p. 55

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

to a thinker who believes he finds the source of the most pernicious of all self-deceptions in the ideals of faith of Christianity, including their unchurchly moralistic forms of fulfillment, authors could not possibly be invested with the character of precursorship whose doubts, of whatever kind, do not prevent them from speaking in favor of the unrestricted approval of precisely those wish-images. Furthermore, however, the doubting attitude of all Renaissance thinkers, insofar as they doubt, is rooted in the disposition of so-called individualism, in which Nietzsche partakes only in modest measure and for essentially different reasons, and the pièce de résistance of their questionings is formed by the reference to differences of opinion, be it of peoples, be it of individuals; whereas Nietzsche declares war precisely on the most general and best-believed values. He was thus fully entitled to put this kind of skepsis likewise under the magnifying glass, as happens, among others, with the following sentences: “The skeptic, namely . . is all too easily frightened; his conscience is trained to flinch at every No, indeed already at a resolute . . Yes . . . Yes and No — that goes against his morality; conversely, he loves to make a festival for his virtue with noble abstention, say by speaking with Montaigne: ‘what do I know?’ Or with Socrates: ‘I know that I know nothing’ . . . Thus a skeptic consoles himself; and it is true that he has need of some consolation; skepsis, namely, . . arises every time races or classes long separated from one another cross in a . . sudden manner. In the new generation, which, as it were, receives different measures . . as an inheritance in its blood, everything is unrest, disturbance, doubt, experiment . . But what becomes most deeply sick in such hybrids . . is the will” (J).

We can be still briefer about a De la Rochefoucauld, who, moreover, belongs rather among the moral pessimists. He hunts for the small and petty mainsprings of man and wants to win us with witty maxims for the conviction that, if not all, then most actions proceed from traits such as self-love, indolence, vanity, apprehension, envy, jealousy, self-satisfaction, ambition, mistrust, timidity. Against this, Nietzsche: “To deny morality — that can mean, first: to deny that the moral motives which men allege have really driven them to their actions; it is thus the assertion that morality consists in words and belongs to the gross and subtle deceit (especially

p. 56

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

self-deceit) of men . . Secondly it can mean: to deny that moral judgments rest on truths. Here it is granted that they really are motives of action, but that in this way it is errors . . that drive men to their moral actions. This is my point of view: yet I should least of all wish to fail to recognize that in very many cases a subtle mistrust after the manner of the first point of view, thus in the spirit of La Rochefoucauld, is also in the right and in any case of the highest general utility” (M).

Closer, unquestionably, is Nietzsche’s kinship with the Greek Sophists. In Plato’s Theaetetus it is set forth as Protagorean: although one may not simply distinguish the right and the false, since for each that is right which he holds to be so, there are nonetheless better and worse opinions, and the wise man therefore does right to transform the unwise man through persuasive speeches in such a way that he exchanges his own worse opinion for the better opinion of the wise man. Were we here to replace “better” and “worse” by “stronger” and “weaker,” there would at any rate be a relation to Nietzsche’s doctrine, to be sure not consistently maintained: that there are degrees of error and approximations to truth, and that the stronger personality manifests itself in being able to bear the truth that contains less deception. When we even hear the Sophist Callicles declare in Plato’s Gorgias: “Law and custom are always established by the weak men and the multitude. For themselves and in their own interest they make law and dispense praise and blame. Thereby they want to intimidate the stronger men, who would have the power to procure more advantages for themselves than they, so that these do not do it, as if it were bad or unjust to have more advantages than others; and this is what is understood by doing wrong, when someone seeks to gain more advantages than others; for they, the inferior, are indeed content when they have equal rights . .” (and similarly Thrasymachus, also according to sayings of his that have come down), then we are involuntarily reminded of Nietzsche’s master and slave morality, indeed to some extent even of his doctrine of “ressentiment.” But even apart from the fact that the Sophists in question in turn call a halt individualistically before the mere pleasure of the “stronger” and do not tell us in what his strength really consists, since he must so often give way before the weak, Nietzsche’s main task would nonetheless only begin with the

p. 57

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

question: how could values weak in life become master over values strong in life? The answer, to be sketched with the greatest brevity, yields what is decisive in his method.

The will to power, landing — under conditions which we must still pass over — in the most extreme difficulties of life, induces its bearers, more precisely a group of them, to take their inadequacies of life for supreme values, but the unattainable values of life — roughly after the sour-grapes recipe — for contrary to value. This may happen involuntarily from the outset, it may also happen deliberately: in either case the origin of the value-concepts in question is soon forgotten. Consequence: the sham values are henceforth the object of any and every fervor of faith. “In the son, what in the father was still a lie becomes conviction . .” (A). Nietzsche’s work of unmasking, as one observes, is directed at the general self-deceptions and is consummated in the unrolling of their history: he is the theorist of the development of the value character of general concepts.

Here the root of the opposition between his method and that of the school psychologies since Aristotle comes to light. Whether they turn their attention more to lived experiences or more to the facts of consciousness, they take their object as “given,” break it down into partial objects, in order to come to rest, wherever possible, in elements or “simple processes” (together with the dispositions for them). Against this Nietzsche objects: the simplicity of a psychic process, even if it were established, proves nothing for its originality; for in all lived experiences incorporated convictions have a share, and thus vitalized judgments of a centuries-old human past!

“Trust your feeling!” — But feelings are nothing ultimate, original; behind feelings stand judgments and valuations which are inherited by us in the form of feelings (inclinations, aversions). The inspiration that stems from feeling is the grandchild of a judgment, and often of a false one, and in any case not of your own!” (M). “We live in the remnants of the sensations of our forefathers . .” (SuE). “Not only the reason of millennia, their madness too breaks out in us” (Z).

We refrain here from any criticism of the foregoing propositions; for our only concern is to bring to light the astonishing novelty of Nietzsche’s method. In passing we can at once decide the question

p. 58

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of his relation to those thinkers commonly called theorists of development, such as Darwin, Rée, Spencer, etc. The answer runs: he is their most dangerous opponent. For they believe they must investigate the animal, “natural man,” the child, before they had even investigated themselves, and so they interpret the prehistory of mankind on the basis of the moral value-concepts of today. What they read out of the soul of the animal, of primitive man, of the child, they themselves had first carried into it! Just as the geologist ascertains from strata lying one above another a temporal sequence of formative processes, so the explorer of the soul can hope to get on the track of the genetic history of the sham values only insofar as he learns to ascertain it from “ideals” still effective at present, toward whose reflex in lived experience within his own soul he has gained, through the most extreme self-estrangement, that distance which is required in order to make it an object for himself without the interference of the will to power’s claims to validity.

We could conclude our weighing of Nietzsche’s predecessors, were we not still obliged to remember the once perhaps overestimated, today considerably underestimated, and in any case hitherto greatest theorist of anarchism, who wrote in the first half of the last century under the name Max Stirner! If someone, holding in his hands the compass needle that we have offered for traversing Nietzsche’s works, were to read Stirner for the first time, he would at first take it that it fits him exactly; and if he then copied out all the passages of Der Einzige which, if not in form, then in sense and substance, would also find a place in Nietzsche and for which parallel passages could actually be shown there, they might well comprise in their totality half of Stirner’s book. Stirner, as already remarked, a theoretical anarchist, dissolves the norms of the “liberal” citizen, of the “bourgeois,” in all its forms (such as humanitarianism, socialism, communism, the state, etc.), and in doing so not only makes use of the technique of disillusionment now familiar to us, but also pursues the preconditions in the history of the spirit deep into the essential features of Christianity. If we compare with this what, say, Nietzsche’s Untimely Meditations undertake for the devaluation of the “cultural philistine” (in the widest sense of the word), we must even concede that Stirner often proceeds more radically, less circuitously, with more exact vivisection, and not seldom offers the very last results in briefer words.

p. 59

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Here a fleeting florilegium! What is one’s own is displaced by what is “instilled”; for education aims “to produce feelings in us . . instead of leaving their production to us.” “That there is an absolute . . stood firm as a faith among those who devoted all the strength of their spirit to knowing it . .” “One does not venture to approach morality itself with the question whether it is not itself a phantom.” “The Christian way of seeing has . . gradually restamped honest words into dishonest ones.” “God, who is spirit, alone lives. Nothing lives but the specter.” “The Catholic is . . only a layman, the Protestant is himself a cleric. That precisely is . . the curse of the period of the Reformation, that the clerical became complete.” “The spy and eavesdropper ‘conscience’ (now) watches over every stirring . .” “Did not Christianity concede right at the beginning that it only wished to tighten more sharply the rule of law of Judaism?” The bourgeoisie “has revealed the Lord . . ‘without whose law nothing is justified.’” “Right is the spirit of society. If society has a will, then this will is precisely right . .” “The human religion is only the last metamorphosis of the Christian religion.” “One believes one cannot be more than man; rather, one cannot be less.” “The state’s conduct is violence, and it calls its violence ‘right,’ that of the individual ‘crime.’” “The weak, that we have long known, are the . . unselfish.” From now on we are to carry on the “degradation and desecration of thinking” and “to regard the voice of God and of conscience as the devil’s work.”9)

One cannot disillusion more completely, cannot clear away more thoroughly, cannot dissect out more sharply the connections of the humanitarian value-concepts, of the bourgeois as well as of the communist citizen, with the value-concepts of Christianity; and Nietzsche, insofar as he sails in the same waters, gets not one ship’s length further. Here we have really come upon a forerunner, the one exception foreshadowed above: upon the forerunner of Nietzsche’s method. But he aims with it at something entirely different! We consider it extremely improbable that Nietzsche knew Der Einzige. Had he known it, he would have attacked it passionately, and not with arrow shots and sword thrusts such as he granted a Dühring, a von Hartmann, but with those heaviest axes and hammers which he fetches out of his armory as often as it is a matter of Socrates or

p. 60

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

even a Saul-Paul. For in Stirner he has the antipode to be taken seriously, almost still among his contemporaries!

At first, so we said above, our compass would seem to fit Stirner’s ship of thought as well; but soon, we now add, one would notice that the voyage goes in — the opposite direction! We state at the outset: Stirner is nearly always, and in any case far more often than Nietzsche, right where he denies; he is without qualification always wrong where he affirms. Der Einzige is and remains a monument. By revealing to us, beyond the boldest conjecture, to what degree the spirit can dissolve its own products, it furnishes an invaluable piece of evidence for the spirit’s essentially negating nature. Stirner, and not, say, the bungler Fichte, strips the ego of the last dross of adhering vitality (of the last “rag,” as he calls it); Stirner, and not the philosopher of life Nietzsche, represents with “fear-inspiring” consistency the naked will to power. But now it turns out that the mere tendency to self-assertion of nothingness, that the pure — will to destruction remains! While Nietzsche smashes the Christian ideals by undermining, Stirner smashes them by the last possible outbidding. He knew this and — wants it no other way! “I am no longer a ragamuffin, but have been one” (E. 165). “Let us therefore accept the directive . . to feel at ease only in — dissolving” (168). “. . Not the ego is everything, but the ego destroys everything” (223). But to be sure: after this almost demonic dialectician has crushed all sham values and become free of all self-deceptions, he exchanges them all for one general self-deception: for the self-deception that the abstraction named will to existence is viable! The day on which Stirner’s program became even only the conviction of everyone’s will (a state of affairs still vital) would be the “Last Day” of mankind and — will perhaps be so. —

If one returns from Stirner to Nietzsche (as from the polar ice to Greece), one only then becomes aware, with joyful alarm, how much in his will to power is precisely not will to power but will to — something far different! But of this only later, and here just one thing more: his will to power, in reality even an essence, is at least a principle; Stirner’s unique ego, on the other hand, is no principle, let alone an essence, but merely the substratum mentally added to the actuality of self-asserting. It exists only in self-assertion,

p. 61

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and it ceases to exist with the cessation of self-assertion. It is the famous causa sui, and with this the deepest thing so far was disclosed concerning the essence of egohood. “I do not presuppose Myself, because I am at every moment only just . . creating Myself . . I am creator and creature in one” (178). “I am I only by making Myself . .” (270). Whoever finds these sentences difficult or obscure has not yet understood Stirner’s unique ego and is all the less informed about the ego. An ego-being (= personal ego), we add, is an ego-being only in that there exists in it the permanent disposition to perform, on and on, instantaneous acts of the positing of existence. But: separated from the vitality in which it occurs, the capacity for producing existence is no longer met with in the spatio-temporal world! Over against this, Nietzsche’s will to power appears as the will of life of a peculiar drive to dominate, in such a way that it is not at all the will to power that measures and regulates, but rather it itself that is regulated — and not gently! — by demands whose discovery was owed to unheard-of insights into the nature of egoless life. No doubt, Nietzsche would have criticized Stirner root and branch as the last, but also the very last, cry of rage of Christian nihilism! For Der Einzige no longer denies merely without admitting it, but with the open visor of the declared anarchist, all the foundations of Nietzsche’s strongest affirmations, such as: descent, blood, race, rank, responsibility! If we might borrow Nietzsche’s splendid turn of phrase about Catiline as the “preexistent form of every Caesar” (G) for a daring simile, we would say: Nietzsche stands to Stirner as the Caesar to the preceding Catiline; and the Caesar, as is well known, makes short work of the Catilinarians. Only, Stirner was no Catiline, but merely his consistent theorist, and Nietzsche was no Caesar, but merely his not consistent theorist: for “friend Zarathustra came” and with him the feast of — overcomings! Let it finally not be concealed: Nietzsche too partakes of Stirnerism, as he partakes of skepticism, as he partakes of the theologians’ disbelief in reality! Yet one would misunderstand all this before one had understood his positions and their significance for his technique of dissolution. That is why we bring the proof of his self-contradictions only in the coming parts of our work.

A few words suffice for our third question, whether his aim has only

p. 62

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

a negative significance. We answer with two opinions, which to be sure are certainties for us ourselves. The first: if one were to strip the civilized average man, this “abortion of falsity” (A), of all self-deceptions, there would remain not even a skeleton, but at most a residue of ash; the rest would have gone up in vapor! The second: what makes a science of the soul so difficult is not the — soul, but it is the masquerade of the soul which the will to power has pushed between it and the observer. Therefore, whoever, lifting all the masks, had come even only as far as the soul, would have by far the greater stretch of the path of inquiry of characterology behind him.

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

A P P L I C AT I O N S A N D R E S U LT S

PDF 69

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

TRANSITION

For two reasons we have called Nietzsche the “first psychologist”: first, because his main task, namely to shed light into the developmental history of the value character of general concepts, forms the propaedeutic to every possible science of the soul; secondly, in view of his method of examining every value-concept as to whether it does not conceal an emergency of the will to power and thus bear witness to a self-deception. In our attempt to set forth the applications of his technique of disillusionment, we must restrict ourselves to results that are either especially novel or especially rich in consequences; since otherwise, given his almost incredible wealth of thought, we would run the risk of copying out the whole of Nietzsche. We shall not take into account, or at most touch upon, what he believes he has found out about the nature of love and of pity, because in this he remained dependent on Schopenhauer to the end — thanks, it goes without saying, to his aversion to the latter’s morality of pity, already outlived today; further, his psychology of the characters of the sexes, which, notwithstanding astonishingly clairvoyant details, on the whole betrays more of the personal peculiarities of taste of its creator than of the matter itself; finally, to some extent also his uncommonly sharp-sighted contributions to the science of expression, because in relation to the tree of knowledge of his main results they represent a kind of side shoot, which would demand and deserve a special treatment.

Since we subject all individual findings to the sharpest criticism and in conclusion split Nietzsche’s total view into a half that in our judgment is lasting and one that is transitory, a few words in justification of so radical a procedure may not be superfluous. — To wish merely to repeat a great mass of thought pure and simple, as many “histories of philosophy” at least aspire to do, is hopeless. Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 5

p. 66

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Through selection, distribution, emphasis, differences in detail, grouping, manner of connection, and mode of presentation one infallibly exercises criticism, even if one has intended the opposite ten times over, and one therefore does better to bring this to consciousness at once than to conceal it from oneself and thereby from the reader. But one will even have to lay the strongest stress on it if, like us, one has set oneself the task not so much of retelling a thinker’s view as of drawing out of it that with which it has inalienably enriched a particular science. Should there in the meantime really still be paltry schoolmaster souls somewhere who imagine they have surpassed a work on which they were able to exercise somewhat justified criticism, let them be told that the activity of checking could never presume to step onto the same level as poetic creation, construction, finding, inventing, discovering, even if it proceeded from a worldview of its own. For it was not able to create the work which it judges and to which it mostly owes, for its thoughts about it, even the standard and indeed the demand of that higher perfection which helps it to know the work’s shortcomings!

One would moreover err in assuming that the longest effects proceed precisely from what is most successful. There are shallow truths and profound errors (as, to be sure, also the reverse). The false doctrine of the Eleatics has fertilized European thought more lastingly than the best achievements of all mathematicians since Pythagoras taken together. — This gives us at once the opportunity to oppose a still widespread manner of appraisal which on principle seeks the values of creative spirits in the wrong place, namely in their most comprehensive but also emptiest general concepts, and which, to speak with Nietzsche, sees in the history of the spirit in the end only a series of refuted systems. To be sure, there has been no thinker, and there will be none, who would not, in consequence of an irresistible compulsion, strive for a systematic summation of his assumptions and insights, and Nietzsche in particular, the unequaled pyrotechnist of the aphorism and declared enemy of systems, refutes with his ever more loftily surging late works (Z, J, ZG, G, A, WzM) the very possibility of a philosophizing that remains open. Nor is it by any means merely tectonic stimuli, let alone merely spiritual reasons, but vital compulsions of the strongest

p. 67

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

caliber that command one to draft a ground plan for the inner universe; but let no one imagine that with the proof of its unfitness — which can be furnished without exception and, after the fact, mostly without difficulty — one could ever refute a source-spirit!

Against the favorite view of the spiritual reporters let it be said: to weave webs out of boneless generalities and to offer them as “worldviews” is the business of the everyday philosophical minds; whereas the discovering mind is always occupied by few special questions, rather at bottom by only a single one, which he first of all found, not to say which chose him, and to which he remains bound, unblessedly blessed, all his life long. In this sense the philosophical discoverers, like the scientific ones, are — specialists, however vehemently one may dispute it. They come from the particular to the general, never and nowhere the reverse, and those formulas expanded into world schemes, to which they too do not deny themselves aspiring, sometimes stand to their most immortal finds as a bad casket to the jewels in it, or even as to the contents of important books stand their tasteless bindings from the eighties! The development of Western thought looks somewhat different from what can be read in the history books. It is, for example, a trifle to prove the inferiority of Leibniz’s “Monadology” and Theodicy; but with this one would, so to speak, only have criticized the periwig of his spirit and not yet even touched his real merits, which must be sought entirely in his efforts to clarify the concept of infinity. While one is accustomed to neglect, over the philosopher Leibniz, the mathematical logician Leibniz, so conversely, over the mathematical physicist Newton, the philosopher Newton, whose hitherto, as far as we can see, unperceived and downright despotic influence on all thinkers up to Romanticism, including the fantastically overestimated Kant, goes back in turn to the one and only achievement of forming a concept of mass which proved capable of bearing the invention-piling technology of two centuries. Is not, for example, the whole so-called association psychology merely a botched imitation of Newtonian machinalism! Precisely for the “great spirits” it holds inviolably true that their greatness at the same time signifies their limits, as also the reverse!

The task — already undoubtedly a particular one, although necessarily leading through the whole

5*

p. 68

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

science of essences, of questioning the value character of general concepts as to origin and development, took shape in Nietzsche only gradually out of a still far more special inclination. For every philosopher predestined to his problems it is a conflict of the soul, peculiar in such strength to him alone, that sets his thinking in motion without his willing it and gives it the direction chosen by no man before, along which the surging waves of thought of contemporary as well as past spirits come to view only in the form of a faint trembling and wavering. One would understand him falsely, and often most of all on the basis of the perhaps invented goals for which he himself professes to struggle, before one had ascertained that inner discord and with it the vis a tergo of his trajectory. Our presentation aims to uncover it step by step and will culminate in the proof that the unexampled vehemence of Nietzsche’s spirit corresponded to the unexampled terribleness of an inner struggle on which its bearer would have had to break in one way or another, even if it had not — from whatever secondary causes — happened as it happened. We begin with a reference to the outer side of the matter, and for the time being merely for the purpose of establishing a fact of the motives of thought, whose nature as a conflict may in the process just begin to dawn.

PDF 73

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON NIETZSCHE’S MOTIF OF THE “NEAREST THINGS”

Through all of Nietzsche’s writings there runs — often in the form of a somber admonition — the assertion that for millennia mankind, over all manner of castles in the air and cloud-cuckoo-lands, has misjudged the importance of the “nearest things,” indeed has denied them, partly deliberately and according to plan, partly at least involuntarily. Bound up with this, at first still hesitantly but with the years increasingly decisively, is the conviction that man’s most demonstrable reality is not, say, the still halfway admitted “spirit,” still less the “soul” merely invented for a “higher” purpose, but the living body; the psychologist, then, ought rather to be a physiologist and will get all the further the more he studies the body.

“‘Since I have come to know the body better,’ said Zarathustra to one of his disciples, ‘the spirit is to me only, as it were, spirit.’” “Body am I through and through, and nothing besides; and soul is only a word for something about the body . . There is more reason in your body than in your best wisdom . . The creative body created the spirit for itself as a hand of its will” (Z). “Our greed for knowledge of nature is a means by which the body wishes to perfect itself.” “The human body, in which the . . most distant . . past of all organic becoming becomes . . alive again . .: the body is a more astonishing thought than the old ‘soul’ . . No one ever hit upon the idea of understanding his stomach as a foreign, say a divine, stomach: but to take his thoughts as ‘inspired’ . . for this inclination and taste of men there is testimony from all the ages of mankind.” Should the body, however, so it goes on, be a fallacy, that would be a grave “question mark against the trustworthiness of the spirit”; which is why whoever wishes to undermine faith in the body “thereby most thoroughly undermines faith in the authority of the spirit as well” (WzM).

p. 70

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Nietzsche’s immoralism thus not seldom becomes a “morality for physicians” (J, G), and at the very least the intention exists of clothing the ultimate decisions of metaphysics in the language of physiology and physiopathology. Carlyle’s work is for him an expression of “dyspepsia,” Buddha a “profound physiologist,” his teaching a “hygiene” (Eh), the skeptic as well as the scientist a “consumptive of the spirit” (G), the gospel of Jesus of Nazareth the outcome of a case “of retarded puberty, undeveloped in the organism” (A). Similes from the sphere of the abdomen, of digestion, of the entrails are preferred at the most decisive places, and it is now deliberately left open whether it is merely a matter of similes, now the opposite emphatically underlined. “A strong and well-constituted man digests his experiences (deeds and misdeeds included) as he digests his meals, even when he has hard morsels to swallow. If he ‘cannot have done’ with his experiences, this kind of indigestion is just as physiological as the other — and in fact often only one of the consequences of the other.” “‘Someone or other must be to blame for my feeling unwell,’ this kind of reasoning is peculiar to all the sickly, and the more so the more the true cause of their feeling unwell, the physiological one, remains hidden from them (it may lie, say, in a disease of the nervus sympathicus, or in an excessive secretion of bile, or in a poverty of the blood in . . potassium phosphate, or in states of pressure in the abdomen which obstruct the circulation of the blood, or in degeneration of the ovaries and the like)” (ZG). Nietzsche “smells,” as he announces in spaced type (Eh), the constitution of another’s soul (to be understood entirely without a side glance at Jäger!) and cannot do enough in body-analogical depictions of spiritual capacities and in emphasizing the decisive role which the body and its treatment play in the supposedly highest achievements of man.
