If one were to copy out all the passages that refer to cooking and nutrition, vegetarianism and the enjoyment of beefsteak, to alcoholic drinks and tobacco smoking, to the excellence of drinking water, the inadvisability of coffee and tea, to the duration and manner of bodily recreations, the beneficial and hindering effects of place of residence and climate, to sexual intercourse and marriage, sensuality and chastity, debauchery and “innocence,” the mixing of races and of classes, to dietetics in man’s relation to the landscape, movement in the open air and

p. 71

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

sitting indoors, to malaria, syphilis, and epilepsy: it would make a stately volume!10)

It would of course be natural to find in this something of the psychological taste of the eighties, which moreover might have received an influx from the circumstance that Nietzsche was a gravely suffering man in body, who for that very reason had occasion like no other to gather experiences about the bodily conditions of the darkenings and brightenings of the spirit. When in his disconsolate book Ecce Homo, which may be used only with the greatest caution, he even rises to the astonishing remark that since the writing of MA he had “in fact pursued nothing more than physiology, medicine, and natural sciences,” one has understandably been tempted to see in such displays of his familiarity with the sciences surely most foreign to him one of those coquetries such as, for example, his stylistic fondness for French scraps indisputably betrays. Yet, even if something of all this played a part, one would have missed the mark from the outset with regard to the type of this thinker if one thereby considered the matter settled! What hitherto was credible only with regard to the works of artists, especially of lyric poets and composers, emerges with Nietzsche in a thinker, and one of the greatest at that: namely, that to an extent not previously attestable, at least not equally strongly, his “worldview,” down to seemingly the most incidental individual features, must count as a self-interpretation of his personality, including its range of changing moods. “Gradually,” he says in J, “it has become clear to me what every great philosophy has been until now: namely, the self-confession of its author and a kind of involuntary * and unnoticed * mémoires.” We shall consider presently how far this may indeed be supposed of every system of thought, but wish here to remark that Nietzsche had to feel compelled to bring precisely these to light among the causes of origin of comprehensive judgments of others, because with his own judgments of worldview he had consciously and deliberately never striven for anything other than the unriddling of himself. Thanks to the penetrating brightness of his sleepless consciousness he had already sensed this in the days of his youth, but in manhood he saw through it with that sharpness and clarity which no longer permits any evasion and hiding.

p. 72

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Whither, it says in M of his own philosophy, “whither does this whole philosophy want to go with all its detours? Does it do more than translate, as it were, into reason a steady and strong drive, a drive for mild sun, bright and moving air, southern plants, the breath of the sea, light meals of meat, eggs, and fruit, hot water to drink, days-long quiet walks, little talking, rare and cautious reading, solitary living, cleanly, simple, and almost soldierly habits, in short for all the things that taste best precisely to me, are most beneficial precisely to me? A philosophy which at bottom is the instinct for a personal diet? An instinct which seeks my air, my altitude, my weather, my kind of health by the detour of my head?” “We, however, want to be the poets of our life, and in the smallest and most everyday things first!” (F). — Insofar as Nietzsche, as already indicated earlier, also partook of individualism, indeed of personalism (of a Stirner), he was consequently bound to become a partisan of that skepticism for which antiquity had essentially already established the canon of the most persuasive arguments. Insofar, on the other hand, as he was something far different, namely the first thoroughgoing vitalist of modern times, he held with such knowledge in his hands the Faustian key that promised to lead to the “Mothers.” For the present we have to do only with the vitalist turn, and before characterizing the methodological innovation which — inseparably sister to it — promises to open up the most immeasurable field of inquiry, we interpose an intermediate consideration for the purpose of fending off on principle all meddlesome desires of the spirit of doubt.

If of two persons who enter a room one after the other, the first makes the accurate statement that the carpet in it is narrow and long, the second the likewise accurate statement that it shows a discreet pattern on a uniformly brick-red tone of ground, it would be possible for the owner to base on this a few cautious conjectures about differences in the state of mind, indeed in the character, of the two, notwithstanding that he held their judgments to be correct without qualification. If the first person had even added that, owing to the excessive length of the carpet in relation to its breadth, the room appeared unfavorably elongated, and the second, that pattern and tone stood in excellent harmony with the color of the chairs, the table, and the paneling of the

p. 73

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

walls, then from the difference between these value judgments the owner could already draw some fairly definite conclusions about the natures of the two, even if he agreed with them to such an extent that he felt prompted to remark that only because it was a “hand-knotted Beshir” had he shrunk from having the carpet shortened, and had put up with the unpleasing length in view of the wonderfully harmonizing colors. We have deliberately chosen an example as everyday as possible in order to confirm at one stroke the proposition: no judgment, however apt, about anything whatever could be passed that would not contain something or other of the character of the one who holds the judgment. But if, beyond this, a whole system of judgments depends in addition upon the peculiar nature of its creator and accordingly counts, among other things, among his “confessions,” then nothing whatever has thereby been decided about its content of truth!

One feels involuntarily driven to hunt for the “subjective” grounds of origin of a judgment that one holds to be erroneous; but one might with no less right hunt for the subjective origin of the judgment that one holds to be correct; and one still has a very inadequate concept of the nature of “objectivity” so long as one imagines it to be lacking in subjective grounds of origin! As surely as only “subjects” pass judgments, so surely is there not a single judgment in which the personality of the judge, indeed his momentary state, would not also have a share. Instead of therefore surrendering ourselves to a bottomless skepticism, we would do better to consider that not even the simplest content of an impression could ever be exhausted by judgments. And were it merely an ink blot: with regard to it not thousands, not hundreds of thousands, no, millions of truths can be stated, all of which would accordingly at the same time indicate to us something of millions of differences in the state of the speakers. — So much, for the moment, to fend off the addiction to doubt.

Only with the exhibiting of vital foundations of a disposition according to which Nietzsche consciously strove for that by which other thinkers were led by the nose more unconsciously, would we have sketched the provisionally required features of that typical thing which we have in mind. — For the legend which has the Bodhisattva flee the palace of his father at Kapilavastu and seek out the grove of the penitents, because on three outings he had encountered a decrepit old man, then a sick man, finally a corpse

p. 74

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

— for this legend, the source of the Buddha’s whole teaching lies in three experiences of a youth coming to maturity. With the genial art of simplification which is peculiar to it elsewhere too — think of Galileo’s swinging chandelier, of Newton’s falling apple, of Boehme’s pewter vessel of his very own invention — it knows how to convince us that the germ of the world-thoughts that move mankind must be seen in a particular receptivity and sensitivity of their founders, and the condition of their unfolding in the early excitation of these by elective-affine events. But if, following such a directive, we wished to trace the hearth of the Platonic theory of Ideas, of the Aristotelian entelechy, or even of Kant’s critique of reason, we would soon have to admit to ourselves that our yield would turn out slight and doubtful, because the works in question would in any case have incomparably less to tell of the — in short — qualities of heart of their authors than of the relation of their heads to learned tradition, and thereby, to be sure, something of the constitution of those heads, and thus indirectly, finally, of a most modest dose of the vitality attached to them. At once, however, the guild of thinkers might well seem to us to fall apart into two guilds, those — once again, in short — with mere head-thoughts, and those chiefly with heart-thoughts, among the latter of which we might be inclined to count, of the kinds, for example, the so-called mystics, further the Gnostics, further and especially the Romantics, and, to pick out a few individual names too, say a Schopenhauer, a Bruno, Paracelsus, Plotinus, and above all an Empedocles, Heraclitus, Pythagoras (him at least by reputation).

Superfluous to say on which side Nietzsche would find himself. For he, if anyone, had the right to the saying: “Write with blood, and you will learn that blood is spirit” (Z). Were his teaching to penetrate into the broad masses of the people, which it never will do, the rise of legends similar to those about the Buddha would be most highly probable. Yet with all this we would still stand rather far from what is distinctively particular in this side of his nature. A further necessary step now leads, to be sure, into darkness and permits us for the present only to draw on certain analogies, important though they are. It has hardly yet been asked, let alone an answer attempted to the question, why in the one group of men the spiritual personality is able to eke out a kind of separate existence, whereas in the other group it appears directly in the

p. 75

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

light of the presentation of a peculiarly constituted corporeality. For we do not even know on what the quite extraordinary difference in degree of the suggestibility of different persons rests, and the no less extraordinary difference in degree of the capacity for exercising suggestions; and only the general fact can be established with full certainty, that the intimacy of the connection between the vital and the spiritual sphere of personalities displays an uncommonly large scale of degrees. All religious geniuses such as Lao-tse, Manu, Buddha, Zarathustra, Pythagoras, Paul, Muhammad, etc., knew this; all turned and turn to the life-dependent, never to the intellectual spirits, and all have devised precepts, and hallowed them with no matter what means of religious fantasy, which amount to a training of the body: think of the yogis, fakirs, dervishes, of the early Christian anchorites, of medieval saints, of the Hesychasts, finally of the monastic orders of all times and peoples. We shall not go astray if we look for Nietzsche in the neighborhood of this kind of being, and we shall convince ourselves that the downright crushing criticism which he practiced precisely upon them by no means stands in contradiction to this. On the other hand, the consideration whether and how far he himself partakes in the image-making of the religious occupies us at all only incidentally, and for the present quite exclusively the circumstance that it was precisely the life-dependence, indeed beyond that, in the narrowest sense, the body-dependence of his spirit that enabled him to make his most consequential finds.

His incessantly repeated invocation of physiology, far from being a play on words, means a doctrine of the body which — immeasurably different, to be sure, from medical doctrine of the body — would be founded on a listening to the lived experiences of the body, or else of lived experiences as such, with salient attention to their dependences on habitual as well as transitory states of the body. It is one thing to ask about the criteria of the correctness of a judgment, another to raise the question: how does my feeling of the compelling relate to them, and what do I gain from it for the understanding of reality? If we did the latter, we would have taken as the starting point of our inquiry a lived experience or, if you like, a genus of lived experience, and if we were even to direct our attention to the fact that, in the face of judgment contents of the same kind, our readiness to assent

p. 76

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

could fluctuate with the fluctuation of the mood of life, then we would already be on the way to the investigation of the body as Nietzsche understands it. Suppose now that, thanks to an especially close clamping of his spirit to his corporeality, he possessed a far more than ordinary fine sense for the most delicate oscillations, surges, fluctuations of the latter, which enabled him to draw out of every attitude, every thought, every approval and rejection, every conviction and certainty of faith, ultimately out of every content of consciousness, what in it stemmed from participation in the states of the organism and to that extent from prejudice; then it will be understood if we give his biologism, praised above, the essentially narrower formulation that, renouncing the ever hypothetical explanations which need the substructure of a world of being, he took his direction with unexampled exclusiveness toward the science of appearance, which, to be sure, has yet to be opened up; and supposing finally that his own vitality reached down with even only one root into the groundwater of life itself, then we would accordingly have to recognize in him a methodically conscious explorer of reality. For here the proposition would hold: “Truth deepens according to the power of sight of the truth-seeking spirit”11).

The faith in reason (and still more the faith in will!) of the present, veiled in many opposites of itself, has also seized upon the name of the science of appearance (or, more sonorously, “phenomenology”) for the advertising of logical juggling tricks and pettifogging hair-splitting; but one may predict for it a mayfly’s life in view of the mighty model that Nietzsche gave. Research into appearance is either the way back from the facts of consciousness to lived experiences (processes of life), or it is an exercise in peculiarly airy shadow-boxing! Let one understand the word “physiology” in Nietzsche’s mouth each time as the doctrine of the lived experiences of the body, which are partly directly, partly indirectly capable of consciousness, and one will convince oneself that it not only has a justified sense, but even quite aptly designates the goal to which he owed the possibility of his most fruitful deep soundings; since, to be sure, it could then without exception also be replaced by a correctly understood “psychology.” But what such a mode of consideration undertakes and what in principle it is capable of achieving, a single example instead of many shall teach us in advance, from whose fabric of thought one may pluck out only the most colorful threads

p. 77

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

(as the average mind, incapable of surveying the whole, has done today and always), in order to find certain highly admired theories of the present contained in it, only without the distorting perspective that unfailingly accompanies isolation, which by its nature exaggerates.

“However far a man may carry his self-knowledge, nothing can be more incomplete than the picture of the totality of drives that constitute his being. He can scarcely call the cruder ones by name: their number and strength, their ebb and flood . . and above all the laws of their nourishment remain entirely unknown to him . . . Suppose a drive finds itself at the point where it craves satisfaction . ., then it looks at every occurrence of the day to see how it can use it for its purpose . . . Perhaps this cruelty of chance would strike the eye still more glaringly if all drives took the matter as seriously as hunger, which does not content itself with dreamt food; but most drives, especially the so-called moral ones, do precisely this: if I may be permitted the conjecture that our dreams have just this value and meaning, to compensate to a certain degree for that chance absence of ‘nourishment’ during the day. Why was yesterday’s dream full of tenderness and tears, the one of the day before playful and high-spirited, an earlier one adventurous . . ? Why do I enjoy indescribable beauties of music in one, why do I soar and fly in another . . ? These fictions, which give . . discharge to our drives of tenderness or of playfulness or of adventurousness . ., are interpretations of our nervous stimuli during sleep, very free, very arbitrary interpretations of movements of the blood and the bowels, of the pressure of the arm and of the bedclothes, of the sounds of the church bells . . and other things of that kind. That this text . . is commented on so variously, that the poeticizing reason today and yesterday imagines such different causes for the same nervous stimuli: the reason for this is that the prompter of this reason was a different one today from what it was yesterday, — a different drive wanted to satisfy itself . . Waking life does not have this freedom of interpretation that dreaming life has, it is less poetic and unbridled; but need I point out that our drives in waking likewise do nothing but interpret the nervous stimuli and posit their ‘causes’ according to their needs? . . . That all our so-called consciousness is a more or less fantastic commentary

p. 78

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

on an unknown, perhaps unknowable, but felt text? — Take a small lived experience. Suppose we notice one day that someone in the market-place laughs at us as we pass by: depending on whether this or that drive in us happens to be at its height, this event will mean this or that to us . . One man takes it like a raindrop, another shakes it off like an insect, one seeks to pick a quarrel over it, one examines his clothing to see whether it gives occasion for laughter, one reflects in consequence on the ridiculous as such, one is gladdened to have given . . a ray to the cheerfulness and sunshine of the world; and in every case some drive finds its satisfaction in it, be it the drive of vexation or of combativeness or of reflection or of benevolence. This drive seized upon the occurrence as its prey: why precisely this one? Because it lay in wait, thirsty and hungry . .” (M).

Let us for a moment picture to ourselves the probable genesis of this train of thought! It begins with reflection on the difference between a few dreams or, what is more likely, on the various effects of being smiled at, arrives at once at the insight that it is impossible to locate the cause of the differences in the occasions, interprets the bodily impulses that might underlie them, which cannot be directly found, as the coming into effect of “drives” that precisely require stilling, discovers — the correctness of the interpretation presupposed — and emphatically brings out the surprising discrepancy between the impulse in question (“text”) and the objectivations of the understanding (“commentary”), and culminates in the unmistakably emotionally charged assertion that reason has rather invented what it supposed it had found, namely in accordance with an unrecognized whispering of the body. The greatness as well as the limits of Nietzsche’s manner of proceeding in investigation could be fully demonstrated by this one example (as, incidentally, by any other one always in the same way). Were we to dissect it sharply, we should find that his method is admissible, if not exemplary, from the standpoint of the science of phenomena, that he really possesses the unusually keen hearing for states of the body with which we have credited him, but that in his interpretation he has neglected to keep drives and interests (= mainsprings) apart, which points back to some error in the calculation and, consistently carried further, would inevitably end in skepticism. In fact the quoted

p. 79

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

passage ends as follows: “What, then, are our lived experiences? Much more what we put into them than what lies in them! Or must we even say: in themselves nothing lies in them? To experience is to invent?” (Let it be noted already here how thin, how phantasmagoric, how strangely bodiless the “drive” suddenly appears — think of raging hunger —, when placed on the same level with a mere invention!)

For the present, however, it is not any possible shortcomings that concern us, but precisely the merits of the procedure and its inner preconditions. If, rightly illuminated, the valuing objectification really leads back to life-processes of so utterly different a kind, then by means of the obvious generalization that the testimony of consciousness by no means yet attests the compelling lived experience, and that in relation to it it is commonly to be taken rather for a reinterpretation — from whatever interest —, something of the peculiar conflict would already have become conjecturable which, comparable to a tensed spring, might have formed the chief source of energy for Nietzsche’s thinking. But a brief consideration teaches us that the real focus of forces must be sought in a far more specific discord. — Were the starting point of my philosophizing the considerable difference between the smell of onions and the scent of violets, and had I reflected on it in an appropriate manner, I could hardly help becoming aware at once of the still far greater differences that my total state would undergo through the change of mood brought about by the two smells, as well as of the readiness for the arising of correspondingly opposed phantasms, now with, now without the character of memory. In this way I might perhaps arrive at a concept of the onion-like and of the violet-like, might find out similarities between certain sequences of tones, combinations of colors, lines and the onion-like, and between other sequences of tones, combinations of colors, lines and the violet-like, could make experiments to see whether the smell of onions, for instance, occasions in different persons dreams similar in some respect, and the scent of violets dreams similar in another, could proceed more metaphysically by pursuing the question: what does the onion-like mean, what the violet-like, at least for man; or, more strictly in the sense of the science of the soul: on what, in the organism as in the spirit of man, does the difference in character of the two smells rest? I should not avoid turning off into the domain of investigations of value, and should thereby, according to the customary scheme of classification, probably be moving essentially in the field of

p. 80

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

so-called aesthetics. In this way I should no doubt have treated some “nearest things,” and again by the method of the science of phenomena; but everyone feels at once that nothing in the least comparable to it would be found in the whole of Nietzsche! Let us betray it in a word: it is moral, and solely and exclusively moral, values whose relation to the constitution of life Nietzsche labors to determine! Into the essay “Experience and Invention,” too, reproduced above in fragments, the sentence is interspersed: “Need I point out . . that our moral * judgments and valuations, too, are only images and fantasies about a physiological process unknown to us, a kind of acquired language for designating certain nervous stimuli?” And we venture the assumption that only with this have we discovered the motive of the whole train of thought, at least in the sense that our thinker, for the sake of confirming this proposition, which for him was already established on other grounds, was bent on demonstrating the supposed relation between lived experience and objectification in spheres of fact as well that are morally indifferent and as remote as possible from his main question. Someone will perhaps reply that Nietzsche was a moral philosopher, which has long been known. We ask, however, that the context be observed.

We had assumed, and have in part already made probable, that Nietzsche’s spirituality was dependent on life to an unusual degree, that his constantly repeated reference to the decisive importance of the “nearest things” and to the almost solely still credible reality of the living body attests this, and that he therefore found in listening for lived experience (which he liked to call “self-interrogation”) not only his most particular strength, but was also empowered by it to inaugurate a science of phenomena: in relation to this, however, his passionate, not to say monomaniacal, preference for moral and ever again moral problems must arouse astonishment. Thanks to that first precondition of his creative work he would have had the makings of an “aesthetician” such as had never yet existed, however reluctantly we take the word into our mouths12), and indeed there sparkle in the giant fireworks of his writings colored balls and stars enough from a psychology of the artist, but rather like high-spirited disturbers of the peace than as systematically fitted into an overall picture that permits not the slightest doubt about his thoroughly moral-philosophical vocation. If, then, to the first of the conditions of his creative work

p. 81

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

a second of unexpectedly contradictory character is added, then the generating conflict of the soul is sharpened and particularized for us still beyond the measure at first conjectured, and we think we see the chasm in Nietzsche running not between lived experience in general and consciousness in general, but, more volcanically, between bodily feeling and moral pathos. He becomes the judge of the claims to value of rationality out of a rebellion, suffered by him in torment, against the claims to value of morality, and he becomes the grim enemy of the values of morality because against the rock of morality, to which he himself is chained, the spirit of his body rears up! Only with this have we arrived at the unique ground of his problematic of values.

Rebellions against morality, “conventions,” laws we found and find in the age of the Sophists among the Greeks, in the individualism of the “Renaissance,” among the initiators of the “Storm and Stress” from the following of Rousseau, in most of the pessimists of mood (as especially Byron), most radically in Stirner and since then in a whole series of more or less significant authors of the nineteenth century (we name, say, Hebbel, Ibsen and Tolstoy), but always on the basis of other, or at any rate predominantly other, traits of character. Without already seeking here to “fathom” a conflict that was, after all, meant for the time being only to help us characterize Nietzsche’s direction of research as sharply as possible, we nevertheless believe, both for the sake of clarifying the matter and in order to ease our later exposition, that we may justify the insertion of a general finding, with which we tie on to our definition of drive and mainspring in the first part of this work.

In principle every mainspring stands in opposition to every other of the same person, and above all every personal mainspring to every general one13). Thus someone’s acquisitiveness may contend with his ambition, both with his lust for domination, all three with his urge for independence, and each of the four may on occasion fall into the fiercest feud with demands of general dispositions of the will such as “conscience,” love of truth, sense of justice, etc. What used to be called by preference the conflict of “duty and inclination” was and is, nine times out of ten, a conflict of general dispositions of the will, say the feeling of duty, with a personal interest, e.g. with craving for distinction, greed, addiction to pleasure, and at the very most once — with which Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 6

p. 82

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it is invariably confused — the conflict between the former and a (sub-spiritual) drive, e.g. hunger. Of the ten Mosaic commandments, more correctly prohibitions, all therefore, with the exception of a single one, aim at the bridling of personal mainsprings, and it could be shown without difficulty that movements of social as well as spiritual liberation, with a fairly exactly corresponding distribution of forces, were each time directed at the tearing of such reins. If now, as was set forth in the first part, mainsprings are causes of the will, and if will is the same as “will to power,” then such struggles have always been played out on the spiritual plane, hence in favor of the will, and have all in all served to consolidate step by step ever further the dominion of the spirit. In contrast to this it is in Nietzsche not the sense of independence or whatever other mutinous insubordination, but a — if not mighty in its drives, then at any rate, as it were magnetically, sensitive to disturbance — bodiliness that declares war on the demanding power of the spirit and thereby, to be sure, on the will itself! (That in doing so he succumbed and in the end reinterpreted the body as a will to power must here still remain out of consideration.) Once again we feel ourselves reminded of the religious geniuses. For what in them is struck by the scourge-blows of the will, the instincts and drives, precisely that rises up demonically in Nietzsche against the wielder of the scourge and kindles in his spirit a light in whose mask-consuming reflection the supposed God changes into the life-strangling Satan. It is the body that here demands its right “by the detour of the head,” and it is the counterattack of the instincts of the body — and by no means of high and highest interests — against what most threatens precisely them, against the moral statute, that feeds the decomposition of all values. “The physiological confusions are the cause of all evil” (WzM).

The human spirit, not the living organism, knows anarchy: thus Nietzsche, the greatest breaker of fetters mankind has yet had, consistently rejects all revolutions that have ever been. Not the body, this eternal here and now of sorrowful and joyful happenings, is capable of wishing; whereas the spirit, restlessly oscillating between the once and the one day, participates in livingness only through the mediation of wishes: thus Nietzsche consistently rages against wishing man and his vampiric “ideals,” and has disclosed as no one before him the paradoxical correspondence that subsists between the delusion of purpose and the mummification of the past.

p. 83

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

The protest of life against the arrogance of consciousness grows out of the protest of the body against the “holy spirit”! “A few hours of mountain-climbing make of a scoundrel and a saint two fairly equal creatures. Fatigue is the shortest way to equality and fraternity . .” (W): with such a sentence, one of hundreds of a kindred sort, the strongest and deepest tendency of Nietzschean thinking, and in it the belief in the judicial authority of the body, comes to light as it were naked. From this, and indeed only so, do we understand it when he sees the attitude of the researcher in the light of a Promethean sacrilege, likes best to designate his immediate motives with words such as mistrust, suspicion, daring, hardness, cunning, and calls the “philosopher of the future” a “tempter” who has the duty “of squinting most maliciously out of every abyss of suspicion” (J)14).

Were it not foolish to want to have anything about a source-spirit otherwise than it once and for all has been, one might easily talk oneself into the sentimental wish: if only Nietzsche, as the unique vitalist that he was, had systematically, and as impartially as possible, taken up all concepts of value, in order to ascertain step by step from what discords as well as agreements of the spirit with life they acquired their character, instead of always interrogating only the values of morality, inquisitorially, as to their origin! Yet the wish would not merely be absolutely unfulfillable, but above all foolish as well. Never yet has a philosophical discovery come about without the passionate for or against of the discoverer, and without exception, we repeat it, it began with a highly particular formulation of the question, which only gradually drew wider circles, in order finally perhaps even to become applicable to the totality of the objects of thought and, beyond all conjecture, to prove itself fit for the uncovering of a side of reality whose share in every movement of the spirit had never before been appreciated.

Born of innermost distress and, so to speak, of mortification of the soul, Nietzsche’s work, we do not doubt it, is rich in dangerously misleading partialities. But only the striking power of the spirit, compulsively gathered upon one point, could succeed in tracking down the moral essence and mischief in the most innocent disguises and in following the forgers’ work of the will to power into domains of meaning

6*

p. 84

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of whose kinship with morality no one had hitherto had an inkling. But if, thanks to a resonator that wrests from every concept, even from the one that seemingly does not value at all, its moralistic overtones, Nietzsche thus indisputably becomes the critic of values as such (and especially of the traditionally moral ones, more ruthless than ever an immoralist out of a cult of beauty), we shall nevertheless, after the premises of his art of separation already disclosed in principle, no longer forget for a moment that only a single value-side of all units of meaning was within reach of his unmasking grasp.

PDF 89

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON THE PERSUASIVE POWER

OF SUCCESS

Thus equipped, we may venture to simplify greatly the formulas for what we have just called Nietzsche’s resonator, without still having to fear misunderstandings. Little as there is to object to if one posits a faculty of judgment or, following general linguistic usage, the “understanding,” in order to characterize the indisputable fact that human beings pass judgments, one nevertheless runs a great risk with such an abbreviation of overlooking that in every judgment, however apt it may be, there is also involved an interest and, with it, that for which one prefers an underlying concept of seemingly opposite stamp: namely the will. If we were to form a series according to degrees of its participation and, with the help of the ends of the series, to develop two types, the type of those who are wont to judge with the understanding and the type of those who are wont to judge with the will, then even this would only be meant in the sense of the pronounced predominance in each case of one of the two elements, and would not relieve us of the obligation to trace the share of the will even in the judgment of the most matter-of-fact minds. With the remark that Nietzsche seeks, fathoms, adduces proofs in the service of the doctrine that man thinks with the will — understood, of course, without noticing it and having words for it — the most general characteristic of his direction of research, or better still of his bent for research, would surely be designated with all the brevity one could wish.

After the expositions of the first part we hardly need to add that the will in question, which would of course be an altogether unconscious state of affairs, is circumscribed only a little more concretely by the phrase “will to power,” and indeed very expediently with regard to that trait which is common to all mainsprings without exception. At the same time this would foreshadow in what respect the will must generate wishes, strengthen the readiness to believe of its

p. 86

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

bearer, and finally press toward the falsification of judgment precisely insofar as the truth would be bought only with the admission of its impotence. This we had already settled earlier in broad outline, but we now recognize somewhat more clearly the vitalistic standard that is employed in the process, and we shall hold provisionally to this: that the head-thoughts and supposed truths of the understanding are summoned by Nietzsche before the judgment seat only of truths of the body that are to be lived. — The examples by which we shall now illustrate the most general artifice which the will employs to influence, in the first place, the forming of value judgments, but then, in the second, also the forming of judgments of fact, are so chosen that they lead us step by step deeper into the structure of the technique of deception.

“Success crowns the master,” said the proverb; “success has always been the greatest liar,” says Nietzsche (NcW). Since we have known that by far the greater part of proverbs are renderings of sentences of ancient authors, merely adopted and varied by the “people,” their prestige has diminished somewhat and has sunk at least to the level of validity of “familiar quotations.” Nietzsche is right, and the proverb errs; but that does not prevent it from voicing in classical brevity a view almost undisputedly held as valid. Machiavelli, who in his book of the “Prince” does something similar to what, according to Nietzsche, Shakespeare’s heroes and villains do, namely to blurt out what is indeed so but is usually carefully kept silent, and most carefully by those who involuntarily act accordingly, Machiavelli expresses himself, among other things, as follows: “It is not necessary that a prince should actually possess all the virtues I named above, but it is quite sufficient if he merely seems to possess them. Indeed, I dare to assert that it would even be dangerous for him if he really possessed them all and always practiced them, since on the contrary it is useful to him if he merely seems to possess them. A prince must seem merciful, upright, affable, sincere, God-fearing, and nevertheless be so entirely master of himself that in case of need he can do just the opposite of all this.” A prince who conducts himself thus, Machiavelli holds, will achieve success, and that, in the eyes of the crowd, as the reward for his virtues, since he would seem to owe it precisely to the qualities

p. 87

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which he had in fact only feigned; for “the rabble always holds to outward appearance and judges things solely by their success.”15)

Such things happen daily. Politics great and small of all times and peoples, but especially of the present, gives us evidence enough of lied-up and trumped-up successes and no less of the most purblind worship of success on the part of the deceived. Swindlers of every kind know this instinctively, the moral skeptics know it consciously; but what only Nietzsche brings irrefutably to light with his method is the incomparably more far-reaching circumstance that the mode of inference in question belongs to the technique of deception of the will as such, hence influences the self-assessment of all, of the swindlers as well as of the honest, and afflicts the human picture of the world with an almost monstrous distortion.

The considerably more outspoken proverb, “Every man is the smith of his own fortune,” may serve us still better to illuminate the downright wicked frivolity which, by assenting, we should be advocating in all innocence. Since it admits only the interpretation that everyone is to blame for his misfortune, it ought to strike us as the insolent scorn of the sated Pharisee toward all who are not, like him, “sitting in the fat.” For even if we disregarded the fact that even in private life baseness has at least never yet fared worse than nobility of mind, and in public life has fared consistently better, a single glance at the host of chance events, be it accident, earthquake, hurricane, famine, drought, flood, fire, be it plague, revolution and war, would still suffice to recognize in the willing and ability of the individual only a partial cause of his “fortune,” and in his deficiencies, all the more, merely a subsidiary condition of his “misfortune.” This is so obvious that in abstracto anyone can in the end be convinced of it who does not happen to belong to the most obdurate pietists and incorrigible priests of pride.16) But if we pass over to applications, we shall lose approval step by step, until finally we stand alone with a few of the rarest exceptional minds. We could not make this more vivid than by presenting a series of extremely widespread commonplaces, each in the form of a pointed assertion (and partly tailored to topics of the day), in which, from case to case, there is attested in a particular way

p. 88

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the belief in the deservedness of success and in the culpability of failure, and appending in turn the denials that correspond to the truth.

Assertion: “‘Genius’ always prevails, even under the most adverse circumstances, though with struggles and difficulties.” — Rejoinder: “Countless ‘geniuses’ have perished prematurely, of whose having existed we consequently know nothing at all (how many, e.g., as youths in this so-called world war or, in its day, in the Thirty Years’ War, cannot even be guessed); others have suffered incalculable loss in work and life through the recalcitrance of their contemporaries, and only the very fewest reached the goal unscathed.”

Assertion: “That the war took place speaks for the political incompetence and the false ‘system’ of the defeated party before the war.” — Rejoinder: “Owing to the economic community of interest of the enemies in the face of Germany’s commercial upsurge, the war would have been unavoidable whatever policy, according to whatever system, the defeated party had followed. All contrary views are beside the point, and attest only the irresistible vehemence with which the wounded honor of the will calls for — the scapegoat.” “Guilt is sought everywhere where there is failure” (M). “Misfortune and guilt, these two things have been placed by Christianity on one scale: so that, when the misfortune that follows upon a guilt is great, the magnitude of the guilt itself is even now still involuntarily measured back by it” (M). “All lamenting is accusing, all rejoicing is praising” (MA). In his ‘French Revolution’ Carlyle remarks on the condemnation of General Custine as tersely as aptly: “He was accused of harshness, injustice, faithlessness, accused of many things, found guilty, we may say, of only one thing, the want of success.”

Here we interpose: in the course of the whole of “world history” no battle has been fought, no war waged, in which at decisive moments and at important turning points chance did not help the victor, and no most famous general, most celebrated statesman, most enduring lawgiver, founder of a religion, shaper of an economy has ever achieved what he achieved without the “favor of circumstances.” It was not due to Luther alone that he prevailed

p. 89

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and remained unharmed, nor to Hus alone that he was burned, but to incalculable incidental circumstances which, in relation to success or failure, deserve to be called chance events. No one will ever unriddle beyond question why Hannibal undertook the attack on the capital neither after his victory at Lake Trasimene nor after the terrible defeat of the Romans at Cannae, and Frederick the Great, for all his genius, would have been lost after the battle of Kunersdorf had not Soltikov and Daun, thanks to the “miracle of the House of Brandenburg,” incomprehensibly renounced exploiting their annihilating victory!

Assertion: “I have proved the correctness of my principles; for it is to them that I owe it that I prevailed.” — Rejoinder: “You have proved the usefulness of your principles, and moreover only under certain circumstances and merely for yourself, by no means their correctness. Of every hundred convictions with which their representatives won successes, on average at least ninety-nine were false! And finally: might it not rather have been the manner of acting suited precisely to you that also found expression in your principles?!” Nietzsche remarks on this: Cornaro saw in a meager diet the recipe for a long and happy life; “the reason for it: the confusion of consequence with cause. The worthy Italian saw in his diet the cause of his long life, whereas the precondition for long life . . the slow metabolism, the low consumption, was the cause of his meager diet . . . The most general formula . . of every religion . . runs: ‘Do this and this, refrain from this and this — and you will be happy! Otherwise . .’ Every morality, every religion is this imperative — I call it the great original sin of reason . . Long life, plentiful offspring is not the reward of virtue, virtue . . is rather itself that slowing down of the metabolism which, among other things, . . has as its consequence a long life, plentiful offspring, in short Cornarism. — The church and morality say: ‘. . a people is ruined by vice and luxury’. My restored reason says: when a people is perishing . ., vice and luxury follow from it . . This young man grows pale and withered early. His friends say: such and such an illness is to blame. I say: that he became ill . . was already the consequence . . of an hereditary exhaustion. The newspaper reader says: this

p. 90

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

party is ruining itself with such a mistake. My higher politics says: a party that makes such mistakes is finished; it no longer has its sureness of instinct” (G).

Assertion: “A people that perishes was ripe for its downfall.” — Rejoinder: “That a people has ever perished from the weakness of old age, of this ‘world history’ offers not one even tolerably probable example, and whoever would have us believe it is an advocate of the devil; but countless examples of peoples having perished because they were exterminated. The Incas, Aztecs, Tasmanians, Fuegians and all the best of the so-called primitive peoples succumbed and are succumbing partly to cannonballs, partly to other gifts of ‘civilization,’ such as brandy and syphilis. The Russian people, e.g., is at this very moment being slaughtered. The Indians, left to themselves, would still be hunting their buffalo herds today as three hundred years ago, had not the ‘white man’ exterminated hunter and game alike. The African elephant is dying of the ivory trade, the albatross, bird of paradise, egret of the fashion in ladies’ hats, blue fox and sable of the demand for furs, the primeval forest of the newspaper plague, and so on beyond sight. ‘Died out’ is the infamous hypocritical phrase for what has been ruthlessly murdered. Of all the slaughtered peoples and species of beings, not one was extinguished from ripeness for downfall, any more than a man who was struck down by the wayside in the bloom of his years.”

Assertion: “Right is above might. Though the latter may triumph for a time, in the end justice triumphs; as Schiller already said, world history is the world’s court of judgment.” — Rejoinder: “Yes, afterward, namely when the manner of valuation of those who ‘laughed last’ has become good form and habit! The victorious party calls its claims law, calls what it commands justice: to this rule the millennia-long struggles of shifting power groups with which ‘world history’ is filled show no exception to this hour.” Here Nietzsche’s utterance may still find a place: “‘Great’ is the name given . . to everything that has moved a . . mass for a longish time and has been, as they say, a ‘historical power.’ But does that not mean quite deliberately confusing quantity and quality? . . The noblest and highest has no effect at all on the masses; the historical success of Christianity (e.g.) . . fortunately proves nothing with regard to the greatness of its founder, since at bottom (it) would prove against him: but between him and that historical

p. 91

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

success there lies a very earthly . . layer of passion, error, greed for power and honor . ., which as it were gave (to Christianity) its durability . . . Expressed in Christian terms, the devil is the regent of the world and the master of successes and of progress” (U).

Assertion: “Granted, things look bad; but soon the lowest point should be reached, and then things must go upward again.” — Rejoinder: “Do you conclude, from someone’s being dangerously ill, that improvement is imminent; and would you not conclude with greater right that he will soon come to his end? Well, just as little does an extreme decline otherwise harbor the precondition of recovery. Hope never and nowhere offers a guarantee that what is hoped for will in fact come to pass.”

Assertion: “But all in all, progress is surely unmistakable: we stand higher than the apes, and our descendants will stand higher than we.” — Nietzsche replies: “Nothing doing there either! At the end of this road stands the funeral urn of the last man and gravedigger . . However high mankind may have developed — and perhaps in the end it will even stand lower than at the beginning! — there is for it no transition into a higher order . . Becoming drags having-been along behind it . .*17) (Therefore) away with such sentimentalities!” (M).

However different our examples may be, and however far some seem to stray from the proverb set at the head, one may nevertheless say that whoever took offense at the principle of even any one of the rejoinders would not yet have overcome the propensity to believe in the probative force of success. According to our concluding examples, every doctrine of a purpose of the world is rooted in it, as is the thought, put forward in some form by almost all Western philosophers, that the universe is to be regarded from the point of view of an order, of a planned course; indeed, there is rooted in it the dogma, taken over by the natural sciences from the Stoa, of the existence of so-called laws of nature, and it speaks for Nietzsche’s profundity, and gives one of the strongest testimonies to the capability of his method in the science of the soul, that he rejects theories of purpose and mechanism together, by uncovering the source of origin of the regulative concepts of both, instead of seeking it in prior decisions of the understanding, in the vital need of the will. For if extra-human nature refutes a thousandfold the will to success inseparable from historical man, most

p. 92

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

emphatically with a transience which not only no thing, but not even any property of things, and thus no conceivable order, can withstand, then in relation to it the will to success turns into absurdity, and every interpretation of events turns utterly into hopeless error that, whether openly or covertly, construes a sequence of processes after the manner of the linkage of end and means, even though our striving to take possession of the world may be unable to dispense with such deception and may reach its goal only with the help of the “adjustments” that it makes possible. Once one has seen through this, one need not on that account renounce the desirability of success, but one will henceforth neither consider anything valuable because it was in some respect successful (surviving, durable, resistant), let alone worthless and contrary to value because in some respect it succumbed to more brutal forces or to a superior number of forces, nor ascribe the least explanatory value to the purposiveness that can be poetically attributed to many facts of nature. “We have invented the concept ‘purpose’: in reality purpose is lacking” (G). “Those iron hands of necessity which shake the dice-box of chance play their game for an infinite time: so there must be throws that look perfectly like purposiveness and rationality of every degree” (M).

For reasons to be discussed later, Nietzsche did not give to the unavoidable question of whence, then, the purpose-positing will in man derives, the answer that he could have given, indeed should have given, in accordance with his doctrine of fate: that this happens by virtue of an extramundane power that wages war on life. Since we ourselves hold this view, which has never yet existed speculatively, and hold it without reservation, let us be permitted to recall that it is solely the man of Christian “world history” who feels it to be strange to the point of bewilderment, whereas East Asian Taoism, however far removed by its own presuppositions from such a thought, would nevertheless have so little occasion to contradict it that in part it rather resembles an application of it to everyday occurrences, and might appear as its translation into the idiom of Asia, i.e. into the language of the wisdom of life! A single example of the opposite of Christian worship of success, there wholly purified, will make us more sensitive to the turbidities and mixed colors of Nietzschean

p. 93

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

thought and strengthen in us the conviction that to the oldest and in some respects most perfect culture of which we know, the European glorification of wishing, hoping and willing and the degradation of the world to a pedestal for human “ideals” were entirely foreign.

The son of Gi Liang, against his father’s wish, summoned three physicians to have his illness treated. The first holds it to have arisen by chance and to be curable: he is chased out by Gi Liang; the second holds it to have arisen gradually and to be incurable: Gi Liang praises him and gives him to eat; the third says: “When with life you received your bodily form, then it took shape . . What use can medicines and powders be to you!” Gi Liang dismisses him richly rewarded. “Suddenly, then, Gi Liang’s illness got well again of itself.” The “moral”: “Not by careful nursing can one prolong life, not by love of life can one gain fullness. Just as little can one bring about an early end by contempt of the body, or make life poorer by neglecting it. — Therefore it happens that he who values his life loses it; he who despises it does not die after all; he who loves it does not gain its fullness; he who takes it as unimportant does not make it poorer after all. That seems perverse, but it is not perverse; rather it comes from this, that life and death, fullness and want rest upon themselves. It also happens that one values his life and keeps it; that one despises it and dies; that one loves it and gains its fullness; that one takes it as unimportant and falls into want. That seems to be the straight course, but it is not the straight course; rather this too comes from this, that life and death, fullness and want rest upon themselves”18). In boldness of speculation Asia cannot measure itself against the Greeks, nor against the Germans; but compared with its wisdom, that of all Christendom becomes a wretched, if not poisonous, quackery! We turn back to Nietzsche.

The belief in the probative force of success, and then also of the “experiment” (!), rests on a self-deception prompted by the will’s need for a justification of its existence within a world whose real constitution evidently negates existence itself. We need only shift the susceptibility by which a Machiavelli hoped to characterize the rabble, the susceptibility to the persuasive power

p. 94

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of any filth whatever, provided only that success has “crowned” it, into the mainspring-layer of human vitality, and we have uncovered the strongest as well as most general motive of all holding-for-true on the part of the bearer of “world history,” and with it the most general cause from which he deceives himself. With the barest outlay of quotations as epigrammatic as possible, let us call to mind the applications, important in principle, that Nietzsche makes of this, and we ask the reader to note that the passages devoted to the same subject could always belong to a single treatise, although they are spread over periods of more than ten years.

“The most usual false inferences of men are these: a thing exists, therefore it has a right . . an opinion makes happy, therefore it is the true one . . a thing cannot prevail . ., therefore it is in the wrong; an opinion torments, agitates, therefore it is false” (MA). Of this broadest formulation, the first particularization that requires mention is the rejection of the probative value of the beatitudes that can accompany the holding-for-true of a judgment. Let the reader not skim lightly over the following disclosures, but call to mind their subversive consequences.

“The blessings and beatitudes of a philosophy, of a religion prove . . just as little for its truth as the happiness which the madman enjoys from his fixed idea proves anything for the rationality of this idea” (MA). “They (the philosophers) take ‘beautiful feelings’ for arguments already, the ‘heaving bosom’ for a bellows of the godhead, conviction for a criterion of truth” (A). But the philosophers of the future “will be harder . . than humane people might wish, they will not have dealings with ‘truth’ so that it may ‘please’ them or ‘elevate’ and ‘inspire’ them: their belief will rather be slight that precisely truth brings such amusements for the feeling along with it” (J). “A strong faith that blesses is a suspicion against that in which it believes; it does not establish ‘truth,’ it establishes a certain probability of deception” (ZG). “‘Faith makes blessed: therefore it is true’ . . . Whence in all the world would it be established that precisely true judgments give more pleasure than false ones . . ? . . Faith makes blessed: consequently it lies” (A).

Dangerous for everyone are the cunning and mendacity of others, pleasant and beneficial, on the other hand, their honesty, sincerity,

p. 95

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

truthfulness; which is why, according to Machiavelli, that prince may count on successes who knows how to wear the clothes of such virtues. According to Nietzsche, however, with regard to knowledge genuine honesty, sincerity, truthfulness are far more dangerous still, because by the same lawfulness of effect they prejudice us in favor of the correctness of whatever they stand up for! By the spirited apparent antithesis “Convictions are more dangerous enemies of truth than lies” (MA) we are to be reminded not only of the well-known greater persuasive power of real conviction as against even the most skillful dissimulation, but still more of the fact that among the causes of the immediate “evidence” of a judgment there belongs, among other things, the entirely extra-logical pathos of truthfulness of those who advocate it, in accordance, no doubt, with a securing tendency of the will. “Men believe in the truth of what is evidently strongly believed” and therefore commit the false inference: “Because someone is true and sincere toward us, he speaks the truth” (MA). But: “Learn, then, . . that truth is proved otherwise than truthfulness, and that the latter is by no means an argument for the former” (M). With this, however, there is mercilessly disposed of what had hitherto counted among the most effective grounds of every doctrine of world-view and was wont to secure for it “world-historical” successes more or less everywhere, but quite especially within Christendom: the readiness of the confessors to die for their conviction. “And if someone goes through fire for his teaching, what does that prove! Verily, it is more when one’s own teaching comes out of one’s own burning!” (Z)19).

As the agreeableness of a judgment has no probative value, so the usefulness of a state of affairs has no explanatory value. “When one has proved the highest usefulness of a thing, not a single step has yet been taken toward the explanation of its origin: that is, one can never make the necessity of existence intelligible by usefulness” (M). “However well one has grasped the usefulness of any physiological organ (or also of a legal institution, a social custom, a political usage . .), one has thereby still grasped nothing with regard to its genesis . . Thus punishment, too, has been imagined as invented for punishing. But all purposes, all utilities are only signs that a will to power has become master over something less powerful and has impressed upon it of its own accord the meaning of a function . . . The ‘development’

p. 96

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of a thing, a custom, an organ is accordingly anything but its progressus toward a goal . ., but rather the succession of more or less deep-reaching . . . processes of overpowering that take place in it, with the addition of the resistances each time expended against them . . The form is fluid, but the ‘meaning’ is even more so” (ZG).

But while Nietzsche, on the one hand, sees the ground of convictions in the wishes of the will, on the other he credits conviction, however it may have arisen, with far-reaching influence on the attitude of the will. When, for instance, he occasionally calls Socrates, whom he otherwise by no means treats gently, with unmistakable accents of admiration a “mysterious ironist,” because, though he too followed his instincts, he nevertheless understood how “to persuade reason to help them along with good reasons” (J), he seems after all to hold the “good reasons” to be a thing whose support is worth the trouble; and when in F he even says: “the reasons and the views behind custom are always lied onto it only afterward, when some begin to contest the custom . .,” then, it seems, we are to see in the reasons lied onto it an effective means of defense of threatened inclinations. But Nietzsche goes further. “However important it may be to know the motives by which mankind has really acted hitherto: perhaps the belief in this or that motive, that is, what mankind has hitherto . . imagined to itself to be the real levers of its doing, is something still more essential for the one who seeks knowledge” (F). “Not things, but opinions about things that do not exist at all, have so disturbed man” (M). There needs only one step more, only half a step, and we stand on the ground of Socratism and never find our way back. “This has caused me the greatest trouble . ., to see that unspeakably more depends on what things are called than on what they are . . . It suffices to create new names and valuations and probabilities in order in the long run to create new ‘things’” (F). But we still let pass unheard the painful dissonance between such a profession of the sovereignty of the spirit and the confidence, previously grown familiar to us, in the sovereignty of the body, and we make shift with the assumption that the deceptions of judgment instilled by the will naturally acquire the weight of reality and precisely thereby endanger vitality. “Because

p. 97

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

man takes himself to be free, and not because he is free, does he feel remorse and pangs of conscience” (MA). “Our opinion about ourselves . . henceforth works along at our character and our fate” (M).

If, in the will to success, we stress the flight from failure, or in the striving for happiness the avoidance of unhappiness, or finally in wishing and hoping generally the dread of the opposite, then we have named the particular mainspring to which Nietzsche ascribes perhaps the very greatest significance for the formation of the spirit, namely fearfulness. “One must need spirit in order to acquire spirit” (G). “Fear has furthered the general insight into man more than love has, for fear wants to divine who the other is, what he can do, what he wants: to deceive oneself in this would be danger and disadvantage” (M). Man is the strongest animal “because he is the most cunning: a consequence of this is his spirituality” (A). “The capacity for quick understanding, which thus rests on the capacity to dissemble quickly, diminishes among proud, self-sovereign men and peoples, because they have less fear: on the other hand, all kinds of understanding and of dissembling are at home among the timid peoples; here too is the true home of the imitative arts and of higher intelligence” (M).

But not only spirituality — prejudice too, and habituation to it, is furthered by fear; and so the cultivation of steadfast disposition and loyalty to conviction, or in short of “character,” stands in a dubious light, and the result is easily mistaken for a system of self-deceptions and narrownesses which, though socially useful, are so throughout at the price of a constriction of the body and of its own most proper vitality. “All actions go back to valuations, all valuations are either one’s own or adopted, the latter by far the most numerous. Why do we adopt them? Out of fear; that is: we consider it more advisable to pretend that they were also our own, and we accustom ourselves to this pretense, so that in the end it is our nature . . But at least our valuation of the other, in which lies the motive for our making use, in most cases, of his valuation, must surely proceed from us, must be our own determination? Yes, but we make it as children and seldom relearn it; for the most part we remain all our lives the fools of childish, habituated judgments, in the way we judge our Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 7

p. 98

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

neighbors . . and find it necessary to pay homage to their valuations” (M). “The content of our conscience is everything that was regularly demanded of us without reason in the years of childhood by persons whom we revered or feared” (W). “Habituation to spiritual principles without reasons is called faith” (MA). “Fixedness of opinions, become instinct through habituation, leads to what is called strength of character” (MA). “‘One can rely on him, he stays the same’: — that, in all dangerous situations of society, is the praise that means the most. Society feels with satisfaction that it has a reliable tool, ready at any time, in the virtue of this one, in the ambition of that one, in the reflection and passion of a third; it honors this tool-nature, this remaining true to oneself, this unchangeableness in opinions, endeavors and even in vices, with its highest honors” (F). “Great passion uses, uses up convictions; it does not submit to them, it knows itself sovereign. — Conversely: the need for faith . . is a need of weakness . . The ‘believer’ of every kind . . does not belong to himself, he can only be a means, he must be used up, he needs someone who uses him up. His instinct gives the highest honor to a morality of unselfing . . The man of conviction has his backbone in it” (A).

Thanks to the thoroughness with which we have so far treated Nietzsche’s most abstract instrument of inquiry, its origin in a conflict of his very own soul, and the greatness as well as the limit of its power to achieve, we have to some extent paved the road on which we may now glide along in an easy and increasingly swift ride.

PDF 103

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON

T H E C R AV I N G F O R D I S T I N C T I O N

Under the above heading we gather together the valid findings that Nietzsche set down, on the one hand, about the craving for distinction, ambition, and the need for recognition, and on the other, about vanity and the sense of honor; and we do so once again chiefly for the purpose of completing our knowledge of his methodological basic assumptions. — Since the whole technique of deception plays itself out not in the stratum of drives but in the stratum of interests, and since in every mainspring (= interest) the condition of willing, or more briefly the will, necessarily takes part — which one may always also call the will to success or will to power — it must be possible, despite the diversity of kinds of mainsprings bearing different names, to rediscover in each one, without prejudice to its peculiarity, the will to power, and to peel out precisely the peculiarities of its procedure from their effects. If, further, in accordance with our discussions in Part One, the following belong inseparably together: the feeling of existence, the feeling of being able or of succeeding, the feeling of the right to exist, as well as, conversely: the feeling of impotence, of worthlessness, and of loss of existence, then it might even seem self-evident that every personal ego wishes to surpass other egos, since it would thereby find itself heightened in its feeling of power and consequently in its “faith” in itself. As we shall presently convince ourselves, we would admittedly have skipped over an intermediate link of the thought here, but meanwhile, unconcerned about that, we turn our attention to the noteworthy circumstance that one can test and experience the greater gifts of one’s own self only against the lesser ones of others, one’s greater strength only against their weakness, one’s own superiority only against their inferiority, and that accordingly, with the attitude: I can do more than you, I am worth more and am more than you, indeed, I exist more than you, one unavoidably also asserts: you can do less than I, you are worth less and are less than I, indeed, you exist less than I! In view

7*

p. 100

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of this, Nietzsche sums up the result of his investigations of the craving for recognition — which, be it noted in passing, extend through all his works — most succinctly perhaps in the sentence: “The striving for distinction is the striving for the overpowering of one’s neighbor, even if it be a very indirect one and merely felt or even dreamed” (M). One cannot read such a sentence without at once seeing, as it were, lines of force light up, through which the drive for recognition and ambition enter into connection with traits such as envy, grudging, malice, cruelty, and delight in others’ misfortune.

It would of course mean misunderstanding our thinker from the ground up were one to foist on him the view that whoever wishes to distinguish himself before others consciously strives for their overpowering; since it takes an aqua fortis that dissolves “ideals,” and the most practiced self-reflection, to know of this with Nietzsche! Rather, his opinion is that in the craving for distinction the wish for the overpowering of others is indeed experienced, but ordinarily not also meant, because the personal ego seeks to conceal from its own consciousness the side of its impulses directed toward the increase of power, or more briefly its selfishness, and achieves this through whitewashing renaming. Why, really? Our own answer we have made known: the will does indeed perform the grading of values, but only living qualities, qualities to be experienced, supply the content for it; the former establishes, so to speak, the scale of temperature, but not the caloric, and when this is lacking it must falsify the scale for the purpose of feigning an appearance of it. With regard to the striving for success, which is inseparable from it, it achieved this through a reinterpretation of the entire course of the world, as though that too took place out of a striving for success and proved itself by success-like consequences; with regard to the striving for distinction, which is no longer inevitable in quite the same degree, because it is directed toward a special kind of successes, it achieves it by masking its share in it through a value-laden emphasis on the social usefulness of all who strive for distinction. — Here, moreover, the process is not hard to see through, since its assessment wavers for easily understandable reasons. If, in thinking of an ambitious pupil, one inwardly stresses the skills of common benefit that are acquired only with the help of some ambition, then his craving for distinction appears praiseworthy; if, on the other hand, one stresses his inclination to put himself forward, then one arrives, by way of the two-colored

p. 101

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

shimmer of ambition, in the end at “careerism,” which enjoys general contempt and from which the negative series — envy, grudging, malice, etc. — lies open even to the average understanding; whereas only with difficulty, and often not at all, does one open its eyes to the not inconsiderable dose of the same condition of impulse in the highly extolled — sense of honor!

But that was our answer, and it is not, as we know, Nietzsche’s answer. Anticipating, we insert: if the will is equated with vitality, the question cannot be answered at all, because there would no longer be any conceivable reasons that could induce the will to power, whether general or particularized, to hide itself from the consciousness of its bearer; and we must therefore still close our eyes to the self-contradiction lurking at the end of the road if for the time being we are willing to follow Nietzsche’s lead without misgivings. Here it is to be noted at the outset that he does not, say, revalue and reject the craving for distinction (as we ourselves would do), but only the failure to admit its quota of the will to overpower, together with the euphemistic names for it, and this on the ground that it testifies to a weakness of the will to power, which in and of itself he approves. He thus distinguishes a will to power with the capacity to assert and satisfy itself in accordance with its wishes, and a will to power whose capacity for the realization of power falls more or less, and at times immeasurably, short of its claims to power. We shall later establish the untenability of this distinction, but accept it for the present and remark in addition that, in spite of it, Nietzsche is able to make the most surprising disclosures because he follows it less than he follows deeper impulses, which he merely recasts afterward into the language of his doctrine of the will to power.

An extraordinarily beautiful example, historically instructive as well, and one not to be passed over in view of the further course of our thought, is his interpretation of Hesiod’s “bad and good Eris.” “The envious man feels every rising of the other above the common measure and wants either to press him down to that level or to raise himself up to there: from which two different modes of action result, which Hesiod designated as the bad and the good Eris” (W). “Thus the older Greeks felt differently about envy than we do; Hesiod counts it among the effects of the good, beneficent Eris, and there was nothing offensive

p. 102

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in attributing something envious to the gods: understandable in a state of things whose soul was competition; competition, however, was established and assessed as good” (M).

Nietzsche affirms, as one notices, that envy which, by virtue of an abundance of gifts at its disposal, enables its bearer to heighten his achievement out of ambition, and he rejects the envy that is without accompanying gifts and therefore stands in the light of impotence, the envy that compensates itself only through depreciation of what it envies; and he has thereby brought to consciousness a really existing difference of significance, which would remain a difference in the relations of the two traits to life even if the Eris approved by Nietzsche were likewise hostile to life and therefore, for example, suited as nothing else to explain the tropical swiftness with which the Greek nature blossomed, overblossomed, and fell apart as if through self-consumption. But although in our estimation of the “good Eris,” and thus of the urge for recognition in general, we diverge completely from Nietzsche, we must nonetheless once again admire the great vitalist, as which he proves himself by wanting every condition of inclination, once it is there, to have a field of activity opened to it as well, because he has recognized that inner tensions, robbed of their outlet, threaten man with devastations that can be worse than all those that their release would cause; of which we shall hear more. “Since the will to conquer and to excel is an insuperable trait of nature, older and more original than all respect for and joy in equality, the Greek state sanctioned the gymnastic and musical contest among equals, thus marking off an arena where that drive could discharge itself without endangering the political order” (W).

The objection that with all this Nietzsche does justice to only one side of the urge for recognition, while leaving other sides out of account, would not, even if it were correct, change the least thing about the soundness of the findings presented, in which one should always keep in view that they are called upon to assess the effects of the will to power on the formation of value judgments. If a No must indisputably follow upon the final question of this dialogue: “‘He displeases me.’ Why? ‘I am not a match for him.’ Has any man ever answered thus?” (J), then we do not evade the chilling truth: “Even merit

p. 103

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

offends” (MA), and then no longer evade the admission either that in the urge for recognition a will to overpowering is at work, to which is added the fear of the feeling of being surpassed.20)

“The Greek artists, for example the tragedians, wrote poetry in order to win; their whole art is not to be thought of without contest: Hesiod’s good Eris, ambition, gave their genius its wings . . To strive for honor here means ‘to make oneself superior and to wish that it also appear so publicly.’ If the former is lacking and the latter is nevertheless desired, one speaks of vanity. If the latter is lacking and is not missed, one speaks of pride” (MA). These sharply thought-out sentences supply us with the connecting link of the train of thought skipped over above, in which, more precisely, two components again stand out.
