First, for Nietzsche, who recognizes the reality of the will but not a reality of the ego, it would by no means be self-evident that the will’s efforts at overpowering should be tested precisely on egos; and it speaks equally, then, for the unavoidability of the scorned ego and for the consistency of Nietzsche’s movement of thought when he — almost in Schopenhauer’s manner — grants reality to volitions alone. “One must venture the hypothesis whether, wherever ‘effects’ are recognized, will does not act upon will; and whether all mechanical occurrence, insofar as a force becomes active in it, is not precisely force of will, effect of will” (J). In fact he has thereby illuminated the origin of the mechanical concept of force from the ego’s will to self-preservation, without, strangely enough, noticing that the formula “will acts upon will” says, in only slightly different words: willing egos act upon one another! The ego as ego in fact never experiences anything other than counter-forces analogous to the ego, and precisely for this reason, instead of reality, a world of — “objects.” But to this is added the second question, why it overpowers not merely by destroying and undermining but also by surpassing, and this in turn — since we have, after all, derived the need for self-esteem — comes down in the end to the question how the recognition of others could assist one’s own estimation and thereby heighten one’s self-valuation. It is vanity, so we have just heard, that needs the recognition of others for the production of self-recognition. We give a series of sayings from which the importance of the problem and Nietzsche’s attempt at its solution may become clear.

“That we place more value on the satisfaction of vanity than on all

p. 104

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

other well-being . . is shown to a ridiculous degree in the fact that everyone (apart from political reasons) wishes for the abolition of slavery and abhors in the worst way bringing men into this condition: while everyone must tell himself that slaves live more securely and more happily in all respects than the modern worker . . One protests in the name of ‘human dignity’: but that is, more plainly expressed, that dear vanity which feels not being treated as an equal . . as the hardest lot” (MA). “Your desires are greater than your understanding, and your vanity is greater still than your desires” (M). “Among the things that are perhaps hardest for a noble man to grasp is vanity: he will be tempted to deny it even where another kind of man thinks he grasps it with both hands. The problem for him is to imagine beings who seek to arouse a good opinion of themselves which they do not themselves hold of themselves . . and who nevertheless afterward believe in this good opinion themselves . . . The noble man must first with an effort . . make it plain to himself that since time immemorial, in all somehow dependent strata of the people, the common man was only what he counted as . . . Vanity is an atavism” (J). “The vain man wants not so much to excel as to feel himself excellent; therefore he scorns no means . . of outwitting himself. Not the opinion of others but his opinions about their opinion lie close to his heart” (MA). “Who fathoms in the vain man the whole depth of his modesty!” “You invite a witness when you want to speak well of yourselves; and when you have seduced him into thinking well of you, you yourselves think well of yourselves” (Z).

Nietzsche’s arrows that strike nearest the center of the bull’s-eye are, as one notices, all shot from the bow of the question: how is it possible after all that applause, recognition, flattery elevate a man? Going a little beyond him, we wish to detach the general conceptual aspects from his answers, each time formulated almost pictorially, and, with the help of a few clamps from our own arsenal of thought, to join them into a whole rounded in principle.

1. Values in general, but quite especially values of the will (= moral values), are social values and thus something that, spoken from the standpoint of the person, holds for the — other. “Our duties — these are the rights of others over us” (M).

p. 105

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

2. Words such as honor and sense of honor betray to us that the adaptation of one’s own conduct to the demands and expectations of the social spirit is a source of being honored and consequently of one’s own standing.

3. Even if I were to despise all my fellow men, I nevertheless, in accordance with my invincible drive for self-esteem, still want to count for something before myself. (The extreme formula for this: “Whoever despises himself still respects himself thereby as a despiser” (J)). But with this I have become a fellow man to myself, and from this it follows that there could be real fellow men before whom I would want to count for something. Wanting to count for something before oneself is thus indeed different in its object, but not different in essence, from wanting to count for something before others. I myself am my other whenever I want to count for something before myself, just as, conversely, the actual other is my ego placed outside.

4. As an estranged ego always belongs to one’s own ego, so every self-valuation rests unavoidably on a comparison of one’s own successes with the successes of others, and the need for recognition is by its nature rivalrous. The more the success of others becomes my standard in this, the more ambitious I am.

5. The recognition of the other enriches me insofar as I assent to it and thereby transform it into self-recognition. This could happen through an objective comparison, e.g., of one’s own achievements with those of others: instead, it happens a thousandfold as a result of the recognition one reaps for merely feigned achievements (think of the braggart!). How is that possible? The answer is not hard for us. In playing a role before others in order to appear to them worthy of recognition, or in adopting any attitude whatever that solicits applause, I involuntarily put myself into these others, see and value myself through their eyes, in short, move the valuing alien ego into the place of the valuing ego of my own. Thus I accustom myself to being the spectator of my role, and in step with this I lose the capacity to distinguish between it and my real being. End: I take the role for genuine and in good faith bestow on myself the applause that it earned me from the — other!

If, finally, we consider that the wish to have one’s own worth attested by one’s fellow men grows, under comparable circumstances, at any rate in the degree to which, without its satisfaction, self-recognition

p. 106

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

would fail, then we shall perceive not only in the need for applause and in vanity, but even in competitive ambition and the urge for recognition, the dissatisfaction that can stem only from the lived experience of an inferiority. Our own view — as should at least be intimated already — does not shrink from the most extreme consequence: that merely to experience oneself as an ego at all means to become aware of an inferiority, namely as a result of the exclusion from full being-within life to which the feeling of mere existing condemns its bearer!

PDF 111

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON

“LOVE OF ONE’S NEIGHBOR”

We choose the heading “love of one’s neighbor” because, in Nietzsche’s sense, it allows us to bracket together to some extent: traits such as goodness, gratitude, benevolence, readiness to recognize others, tolerance, indulgence, considerateness, moderation, modesty, diligence, pity, the gift for self-sacrifice — and more or less pronounced directions of disposition such as public spirit, “altruism,” love of the fatherland, etc. — Endeavoring once again to track down the will to power in such character traits, seemingly of so entirely opposite a kind, and to revalue the names that clothe it far more deceptively than in the case of the striving for recognition, Nietzsche in part follows his skeptical predecessors among the examiners of morality, with acute demonstrations of hypocrisies that by no means lie open to view, except that we are to accustom ourselves to see in these, too, predominantly self-deceptions and, as it were, certain “wolves in sheep’s clothing” who are inclined to take themselves for sheep. A few pieces of evidence! Supposed love of one’s neighbor out of actual curiosity: “If curiosity did not exist, little would be done for the welfare of one’s neighbor. But curiosity creeps, under the name of duty or of pity, into the house of the unfortunate and needy” (MA). Out of ambition: “One jumps in after a man who falls into the water twice as gladly if people are present who do not dare to” (MA). Out of fear: “The greatest giver of alms is cowardice” (W). “At bottom they simple-mindedly want one thing most of all: that no one should hurt them. So they anticipate everyone and do him good” (Z). “The trodden worm curls up. That is clever. It thereby reduces the probability of being trodden on anew. In the language of morality: humility” (G). Out of weakness of will: “The fatalism of the weak-willed embellishes

p. 108

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

itself astonishingly when it knows how to introduce itself as ‘la religion de la souffrance humaine’” (J). Out of love of comfort: “Just as all the habitually sick, all dyspeptics, have a bent toward the comfortable, so the German loves ‘openness’ and ‘uprightness’: how comfortable it is to be open and upright!” (J). Out of vanity: “Before whom one shines, him one gladly accepts as a light” (W). Out of impotence: “I often laughed at the weaklings who think themselves good because they have lame paws” (Z).

But such things are, here as always with Nietzsche, merely an extra and an overflow: his very own suspicion digs deeper! The Zarathustra saying already strikes us as peculiarly different: “Your love of your neighbor is your bad love of yourselves”; for this is evidently meant to characterize genuine love of one’s neighbor, if not according to its believed content, then according to its true content and, as it were, its hidden meaning. More precisely, Nietzsche first distinguishes in benevolence “the drive to appropriate and the drive to submit, according to whether the stronger or the weaker feels benevolence. Joy and desire are together in the stronger, who wants to transform something into his function: joy and the wish to be desired in the weaker, who would like to become a function” (F); and from the standpoint of the disposition of the stronger he judges doing good and doing harm as follows, as two mutually complementary forms of expression of the will to power: “With doing good and doing harm one exercises one’s power over others — more one does not want thereby! With doing harm to those to whom we must first make our power palpable, . . with doing good and benevolence to those who already depend on us in some way” (F). The gratitude of the powerful becomes, in this light, a kind of self-evident revenge. “The reason why the powerful man is grateful is this. His benefactor has, through his benefaction, so to speak laid hands on the sphere of the powerful man and intruded into it: now, by way of requital, he in turn lays hands on the sphere of the benefactor through the act of gratitude” (MA).

However much something in most readers may resist such a view — and we too shall show later, with a single turn already intimated below, why all of Nietzsche’s views are incomplete — there is just as little room for the least doubt that it hits upon a really existing state of affairs. One need only think of that, to some extent

p. 109

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

common human “having-to-pay-back” for gifts received, which, especially among peasants and — the Swiss, has developed into the strictest rule of propriety; in which there is voiced the crassly egoistic opinion that one “binds,” indeed that one obligates, by — giving!

Nietzsche refrains from making considerable use of his revaluing critique against this kind of beneficence, for which he would nevertheless have every occasion, insofar as, in relation to the mainspring actually at work, words like benevolence and gratitude would be indubitable falsifications, about whose motive we thus remain in the dark. Instead, he directs the full weight of his attack against that disposition which bestows on the modes of activity of the will to power thus cloaked the rank of supreme values, or rather derives from them the standard for all valuing; and again, according to his analysis, it is weakness of the will that awards the prize to the goodness and helpfulness that seemingly renounce the exercise of power, because the self-esteem of its possessor depends on the “happiness of the smallest superiority,” which alone is still attainable for him. “The most frequent form in which joy is thus prescribed as a remedy is the joy of giving joy (as doing good, giving gifts, relieving, helping, encouraging, consoling, praising, distinguishing) . . . The happiness of the ‘smallest superiority,’ such as all doing good, being useful, helping, distinguishing brings with it, is the most abundant means of consolation which the physiologically inhibited are accustomed to make use of, provided that they are well advised: otherwise they hurt one another, naturally in obedience to the same basic instinct” (ZG). (By whom they are advised in this, we shall hear later.)

If we add, further, the fear of the bearer of strength of will, then we recognize in the canonization of love of one’s neighbor an indispensable component of the morality of the “herd man,” a word that in Nietzsche’s mouth bears the accent of contempt. “On the other hand, the herd man in Europe today gives himself the air of being the only permitted kind of man, and glorifies his qualities, by virtue of which he is tame, peaceable, and useful to the herd, as the truly human virtues: namely public spirit, benevolence, consideration, diligence, moderation, modesty, indulgence, pity.” “How much or how little that is dangerous to the community, dangerous to equality, lies in an opinion, in a state and affect, in a will, in a talent — that is now the moral perspective: fear is

p. 110

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

here too once again the mother of morality. By the highest and strongest drives, when, breaking out passionately, they drive the individual far out and up beyond the average and the lowland of the herd conscience, the self-confidence of the community is destroyed . . consequently it is precisely these drives that one will brand and slander most . . .” “Morality in Europe today is herd-animal morality” (J). — A few passages on the question of the value of industriousness should, even without closer elucidation, attest to the almost seamless interlocking of the basic thoughts. “Your diligence is flight and the will to forget yourselves” (Z). “In the glorification of ‘work’ . . I see the same ulterior thought as in the praise of impersonal actions of common benefit: that of fear of everything individual” (M). “Work gets more and more all good conscience on its side: the inclination to joy already calls itself ‘need for recreation’ and begins to be ashamed of itself . . Formerly it was the other way round: work had the bad conscience upon it. A man of good descent concealed his work when need compelled him to work . . ‘Nobility and honor are with otium and bellum alone’: so sounded the voice of the ancient prejudice!” (F). Of the scholar it is said, in the same key, among other things: “To him it seems permitted to squander a life on questions whose answering could at bottom be important only to one who was assured of an eternity” (U).

If, beside such depreciations of a diligence for which working has become more or less an end in itself, we set the often-invoked Zarathustra saying: “Long have I no longer striven for happiness, I strive for my work,” then surely no one will hesitate, as a matter of feeling, to recognize in work for the sake of the “work” something nobler and also, biologically, probably justified, yet he would hardly thereby evade the conviction that there does, then, apparently also occur a devotion to the thing. But in the flashing-up of this insight, the whole train of thought might appear to us strangely transformed, so that we would see the meaning-content of names like benevolence and kindness of heart with a suddenly altered eye of the soul and would no longer avoid the question that hastens ahead: but is there not, then, besides the will to power, also real love? We do not ascertain here what answer to this might be gained from far-removed motifs of thought by drawing upon Nietzsche’s whole work, and instead present the significant turn

p. 111

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

with which Nietzsche — avoiding affirmation — takes the most decisive step toward the completion of his doctrine of the will to power. “The loving girl wishes that she might prove the devoted faithfulness of her love by the unfaithfulness of her beloved. The soldier wishes that he might fall on the battlefield for his victorious fatherland: for in the victory of his fatherland his highest wish is victorious too. The mother gives to the child what she withdraws from herself, sleep, the best food, in some circumstances her health, her fortune. — But are all these unegoistic states? . . Is it not clear that in all these cases man loves something of himself, a thought, a longing, a product, more than something else of himself, that he thus divides his being and sacrifices the one part to the other? . . In morality man treats himself not as individuum but as dividuum” (MA). — “By giving yourselves up with enthusiasm and making a sacrifice of yourselves, you enjoy that intoxication of the thought of now being one with the powerful one, be it a god or a man, to whom you consecrate yourselves: you revel in the feeling of his power, which is attested precisely once again by a sacrifice. In truth you only seem to sacrifice yourselves; rather, in thought you transform yourselves into gods and enjoy yourselves as such” (M). With this, however, we have already arrived at the subject of the next chapter.

PDF 116

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON

SELF -OVERCOMING

We first give Nietzsche the floor. “In every ascetic morality man worships a part of himself as God and has need, for that, to diabolize the remaining part.” “The most usual means which the ascetic and saint employs in order to make life still bearable and entertaining after all consists in occasional waging of war and in the alternation of victory and defeat. For this he needs an opponent and finds him in the so-called ‘inner enemy.’ In particular he exploits his inclination to vanity, to the lust for honor and for domination, and then his sensual desires, so as to be permitted to regard his life as a continual battle and himself as a battlefield on which good and evil spirits wrestle with alternating success . . The intention is not that he (man) become more moral, but that he feel himself as sinful as possible . . That was the last pleasure which antiquity invented, after it had grown dull even to the sight of the combats of beasts and men.” “Now the saint practices that defiance against himself which is a close relative of the lust for domination and still gives even the loneliest the feeling of power; now his swollen sensation leaps over from the desire to let his passions run free into the desire to make them collapse like wild horses, under the mighty pressure of a proud soul; now he wants a complete cessation of all disturbing . . sensations, a waking sleep . .; now he seeks the struggle and kindles it within himself because boredom holds its yawning face up to him: he scourges his self-deification with self-contempt and cruelty . . ; he knows how to lay a snare for his affect, for example that of the most extreme lust for domination, so that it passes over into that of the most extreme abasement, and his incited soul is torn from all its joints by this contrast; and finally, when visions, conversations with the dead or with divine beings even

p. 113

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

become the object of his lust, then it is at bottom a rare kind of voluptuousness that he desires, but perhaps that voluptuousness in which all others are tied together in one knot” (MA). “The triumph of the ascetic over himself, his eye turned inward in the process, which sees man split into a sufferer and a spectator and henceforth looks into the outer world only in order to gather from it, as it were, wood for his own pyre, this last tragedy of the drive for distinction, in which there is only one person left, who chars within himself — that is the worthy conclusion that belongs to the beginning: in both cases an unspeakable happiness at the sight of tortures! Indeed, happiness, conceived as the most vivid feeling of power, has perhaps nowhere on earth been greater than in the souls of superstitious ascetics. This the Brahmins express in the story of King Viswamitra, who drew from thousand-year penances such strength that he undertook to build a new heaven” (M). “Everyone who has ever at any time built a ‘new heaven’ found the power for it only in his own hell.” “Only the will to self-maltreatment provides the presupposition for the value of the unegoistic” (ZG).

With the building and furnishing of new heavens, or, spoken less vividly but more plainly, with the invention of new ideals, there is indeed something to it, and likewise with Nietzsche’s connoisseurship regarding those problems which one usually, and he himself probably as well, takes for religious problems pure and simple, although they are rather religious problems of an already irreligious historical humanity and by no means those of the genuine piety of the unhistorical primeval age. No one has ever descended so undauntedly, and as it were in a matter of life and death, as Nietzsche to the infernal hearth of origin of legislating wishes of the will (very much to be distinguished from “wishes of the heart”), and to no one before him was it granted to uncover mercilessly that the self-respect of the man religious or moral in this fashion is to be had only at the price of the most shameful defilement of the image of reality! Before we take up this strongest position of Nietzsche’s, let the following be noted in particular with regard to the passages just offered on the origin and meaning of self-overcoming.

According to Nietzsche it is always an inadequacy of life, felt as agonizing, from which, for reasons of indispensable self-respect, Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 8

p. 114

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the ascetic finds himself in flight, and it is exaltations of self-negation by means of which the negating self raises itself to the feeling of an uncanny power, in order henceforth to borrow from this the standard of its valuing and in particular for the valuation of its own type. To explain self-overcoming and all moral values that flee from reality, Nietzsche must therefore posit a weakness of his will to power, more precisely even an impotence of it, because, presupposing the opposite, mere desirabilities would evidently not come about at all; whereas according to our view it is the consciousness of will itself that, measured against the feeling of life, finds itself in a state of lack. But however one may conceive it, it at any rate becomes clear from this why all mere wishing, together with the values arising from it, indicates the “flight from the possession of a disvalue” even when it clothes itself in the form of the “desire for the possession of a value,” and why self-overcoming cannot be separated from self-exaltation, self-sanctification not from self-abasement.

Next, we establish that the whole manner of speaking would lose all meaning without the assumption that the subject is able to split itself into two parts, indeed in a certain sense into two egos, of which the one — at the same time in the role, already familiar to us, of a “spectator” who is, after all, highly involved — owes its exaltations to “looking on” at the tortures of the other. It is the “great stroke” and the proprium of Nietzschean thought to derive the process of the formation of ideals in principle from self-fissuring; and in his doctrine of the body and other deep soundings of his philosophy of life he would also have had the means to explain the occurrence of self-fissurings. But by relocating the will into life itself, he has, strictly speaking, robbed himself of the possibility of assuming self-splittings, and would at least, insofar as he nevertheless does so unconcerned about the threatening self-contradiction, have to renounce explanations for them; as indeed the passages cited really make no attempt at all in this direction, while attempts undertaken elsewhere will prove to be sham explanations.

If, on the other hand, one holds will and life metaphysically apart, then the fissuring in question needs a justification so little that it rather appears in the light of a requirement necessary to thought. In fissurings,

p. 115

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

partitionings, splittings of the personality there merely emerges, more sharply and offensively for consciousness, the ever-present essential incompatibility of the spiritual and the vital side of the ego, or more briefly of spirit and soul. If, viewed thus, life supplies the standard, and not some will to power, and if accordingly the form of personhood itself cannot be comprehended without a loss of value, then the re-entry into full life that might perhaps be wished for would be purchased only with the downfall of the ego, consequently of the spirit, consequently of the will in every form. Such remarks prepare the criticism of Nietzsche’s voluntarism that now sets in, stage by stage, more sharply, though we shall first pursue that voluntarism further in his sense.

8*

PDF 120

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ENVY

OF LIFE

Since the sentences of a Callicles handed down to us — indeed, if one likes, ever since the annihilating words with which Heraclitus, also valuing the “good Eris” differently from Hesiod and Nietzsche, scourged root and branch the public outlet for envy of the Greeks known as “ostracism,” as follows: “The Ephesians ought, as many of them as are grown up, to hang themselves one and all and leave the city to those not yet grown; for they have banished Hermodorus, their ablest man, saying: among us no one shall be the ablest, and if anyone is, let him be so elsewhere and among other people,”21) — there has never been a lack of voices that warned us not merely against the dangerousness of envy in general, but in particular also against its arts of forgery. From the last century and a half alone one could fill several volumes with them. A rich yield would be supplied by Schiller (perhaps under the motto: “The world loves to blacken what is radiant — and to drag the sublime into the dust”), no small one by Goethe (“Against great advantages in another there is no means of rescue but love”), Lichtenberg, Stendhal, Gobineau, Jean Paul, Byron, Balzac (“For it lies in human nature to destroy what one cannot possess, to deny what one does not understand, to insult what one envies”), Busch, a very considerable one by Schopenhauer (as also by Gracián, whom he quoted so gladly and so excellently!), and many others.

Yet even if one were to extract the vital sap from the totality of such utterances, it would still not contain the nourishment of a theory that declares all moral values that have existed hitherto to be the creative achievement of a particular kind of envy, which we call envy of life, while Nietzsche prefers for it the foreign word “ressentiment.” Here too the paths of Stirner

p. 117

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and of Nietzsche part completely. For whereas the former, with his disillusionments, actually pursues the aim which Nietzsche, at least for the most part, merely believes he pursues, namely, to have all values judged and executed by the anarchic will to power of the ego-point, which alone would thereafter still have value, alone still have validity, the aim of Nietzsche’s technique of disillusionment is best described in the following words of his own: “to wed to the bad conscience the unnatural inclinations, all . . aspirations toward the beyond, toward what is contrary to the senses, contrary to instinct, contrary to nature, contrary to the animal*, in short, the ideals hitherto, which are one and all ideals hostile to life, ideals that slander the world” (ZG).22) Stirner, then, combats all abstract bonds of the will with the individual will declared sovereign; Nietzsche, with instinctive, animal, natural life, more briefly with the life of the body, which is organized and never anarchic, combats the claims of the — will, even though he wrongly undertakes to interpret the processes of life themselves on the analogy of acts of will. His aim concerns us directly as little as does Stirner’s, and our occupation with his proclamations of a “master morality” against “slave morality,” which to be sure cannot be avoided, serves solely to secure their yield of insights into the nature of historical man, and therefore proceeds only to the extent required for that purpose. First, however, we must now explore the spheres of experience of envy.

The striving for success in general and the striving for recognition in particular is, as we know, an outbidding striving. If A considers it desirable to possess “capital,” and believes he has reason to assume that B possesses considerably more of it, it may happen that he envies him the greater possession; whereupon at times the “good Eris” may arise, in consequence of which he throws himself with redoubled zeal into earning money, at times also the “bad Eris,” in consequence of which he seeks to rob B of his treasures, even if he would derive no “advantage” whatever from it. For as soon as he believed himself to possess at least as much as, or even more than, the other, no matter whether thanks to the increase of his own possessions or thanks to the diminution of the other’s, the occasion for competition would have fallen away. Among the circumstances by virtue of which the “bad Eris” would gain the victory might be thousandfold constraints of external conditions that would make it extremely difficult for the envier to “compete” with the one envied (think of the son of a factory worker

p. 118

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

compared with an heir of high finance): we should still always have to posit a trait of character as well, because otherwise it would remain inexplicable that by no means in all cases does the rivalrous striving, when condemned to failure, aim at injuring the one better placed. If envy can unquestionably stoke the inclination to compete, the capacity for envy does not suffice to explain the fact that a certain sort of envier just as unquestionably strives above all for the belittlement of all objects of envy. We have no intention of presenting the attainable totality of varieties with which a thorough investigation would acquaint us, but by means of a few cases of intensification steer at once toward an extreme case, which alone is able to furnish the basis for any theory of ressentiment.

If we leave external conditions entirely aside, we become convinced that, with respect to the possibilities of competition, extraordinary differences obtain according to the kinds of objects to which envy extends. The pupil A, whose envy is aroused because his schoolmate B “can do” some subject better than he, e.g., jumping, climbing, mental arithmetic, geography, Latin, finds a barrier to the success-guaranteeing exercise of his “good Eris” in differences of natural endowment, and this barrier will make itself felt all the more, the more it is differences of quality that ground the differences in value of the achievements. Here let a parenthetical remark be permitted: the more, in the conditions that impel any achievements whatever within a group of people, an estate, a profession, a nation, an age, mere emulation predominates over interest in the matter itself, the more the emphasis of value shifts from the qualitative to the quantitative, until mastery (of whatever kind) is finally displaced by the “record,” which is contrary to life and on principle sensitive only to the higher number, no matter whether in versifying, endurance dancing, or grass eating. This in defense against the attempt — hopeless, to be sure, but in the age of records perhaps tempting — to derive masterly achievements of the plastic arts, such as those of the Greeks, from the intensity of “competitions”!

It may, of course, take a long time before the pupil painfully convinces himself of his weakness of endowment, and he no longer needs to do so at all if the gifted one happens to be somewhat lazier and more indifferent still than he is gifted. Let us now suppose the case that A envies B his greater beauty; he feels himself ugly beside him

p. 119

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and must experience again and again how B, merely through his attractive exterior, reaps successes of which he, A — rightly or wrongly, that is beside the point — deems himself a hundred times more worthy; then only his “bad Eris” could still come into effect, unless a mainspring of another kind, gradually grown strong, were to draw him away from esteeming those successes of beauty (or his hating envy were to turn into admiring love, which, however, would no longer belong to our investigation). We must, however, take one more step in order to find that class of objects of envy in face of which the envier would no longer even retain the possibility of compensating himself by a change of interests.

We had considered, in the first place, envy of possessions (in the widest sense of the word), in the second place envy of traits, and indeed first of those of an endowment for achievement, usually called talents, in the third of those of being a Sunday’s child, by virtue of which a thousand amenities fall to their bearer without any particular effort on his part (just as, conversely, a thousand things go wrong through no fault of his own for the polar opposite, the “unlucky bird”), and we turn our attention in the last place to certain gifts of man in comparison with which those named before lose their luster and which for the present we provisionally gather up into the somewhat broad concept of the psychic capacity for happiness. Fairy tales and poets of all times and peoples vie in making it credible to us, in verse and prose, that the rich hypochondriac, who need deny himself no wish, still cannot get rid of his ill humor, while the beggarly journeyman on the highroad sings, quite like a lark, the song of his invincible cheerfulness. Fairy tales and poets often paint only the land of wishes; but this depiction is sheer reality. “Ill humor, however, sluggish dejection, and all these disenchantments, that is the true delusion . .; for it spoils the original beauty of the world.” We today feel ourselves, by and large, cast far away from a state such as lights up in the counter-gleam of the wondrous words of Eichendorff just heard; but we scent enough of it to be able to know that we have grown poorer by just so much in a happiness which — never to be exhausted — wells up from inner wealth.

The capacity for happiness, as was said, is still an all too broad concept. If there is found among the cheerful and the light-hearted, the unburdened

p. 120

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

bearers of the “light disposition,” also the merry fellow, the joker, the wit, and the “good-for-nothing,” whose feebly undulating happiness at times seems suspect of shallowness and superficiality and would occasionally, no doubt, be found too light on the metaphysical scale of values, compared with the dark raptures of many a melancholy, enthusiastic soul. If, accordingly, we replace the all too ambiguous term happiness by the capacity for fullness, for depth, and for breadth of lived experience, two things become evident: that the “talent” for it has been dealt out by nature to ages, peoples, and individual beings still more unequally than purchasable possessions are according to idiotic human statute, and also, however, that the lack of it is more irremediable, more incorrigible, more unexchangeable than any other lack! Since we know of life solely and exclusively through our own lived experience, we are entitled, indeed obliged, to call the one who experiences more deeply, more fully, more fervently, more ardently, more expansively, the one richer in life, and to call the envy of the less well endowed directed at this envy of life. But if I feel, and believe I know: for him there bloom purple seas, secret-guarding shafts, luminous ethereal heights of life from which I am excluded, excluded for lack of organs of the soul for them, much as, for lack of bodily organs, the deep-sea fish is excluded from the wonders of the realm of air and the alpine bird from the wonders of the deep sea, and if I am unable to admire or to love him on that account, then I no longer escape my envious hatred of him through any compensation, and must accordingly seek to extirpate him from the world. The jealous beauty may indeed hope to attain her goal if by means of vitriol she merely destroys the beauty of her rival; but the one eaten up by envy of life out of his forsakenness by life would, even with the enslavement, imprisonment, and disenfranchisement of those beloved by life, find himself only half satisfied and consequently not satisfied at all, because he could not help striking, with every torment he inflicted on them, a new chord, however mingled with woe, on the keyboard of their possibilities of rapture.

For the sake of the mortal hatred that is inseparable from such envy, Nietzsche calls the raging of its bearers against life their revenge (= ressentiment) for having been put at a disadvantage by life, and this is the one cornerstone of his critique of morality: that he derives the legislative idealism, of which we shall have to speak presently, point by

p. 121

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

point from measures of retaliation on the part of all those who “came off badly” in life. He knew well enough that envy of life ultimately has murder in mind; but we do not venture to decide whether he saw through it completely that in the end it actually comes down to that, as, for example, Heine, who is extremely fruitful in this respect, knew it thanks to the fearlessness of his self-observation, even though he usually veils it, juggler-like, in the misty clouds of verses open to interpretation23). Enough: Nietzsche’s gaze is directed not so much at the end, the Golgotha as it were, as at the stations of suffering along the way to it, or rather at the hypocritical arts of disposal with which the “preachers of death” succeeded and succeed in so blinding mankind that it goes to the slaughterhouse voluntarily and with hosannas. — One reason, however, why the said way became a detour even without necessity, we do not wish to pass over in silence: even with the annihilation of all those beloved by life, envy of life would still not have attained its goal so long as the memory of them and their works had not also been extinguished; from which it follows at the same time that, hand in hand with the technique of revaluation soon to be discussed, there goes the falsification of the past, likewise already noticed by Nietzsche, but not explored with equal penetration.

Whoever does not happen to subscribe to the comfortable belief in inner perception may, to be sure, be tempted to object: a person’s possessions are tangible or inferable for the envier, capacities for achievement make themselves noticeable to him in manifold ways, and beauty and its lucky lots lie before his senses; but how does he know of purple seas and ethereal heights of the soul, if experiencing them is precisely supposed to be denied him, and how, in turn, could he envy them in the one rich in life without a knowledge of them? Unfortunately Nietzsche did not pause for a moment over the questions, indeed several of them, of great significance, that the objection would encompass, and so we confine ourselves to giving only so much of the answer as is required to show that the doctrine of revenge on life out of exclusion from life has nothing to fear from doubts of this kind.

To the one born blind the colored side of the world is closed, and he can only imperfectly gauge how much he lacks thereby; but that he lacks something of which he hears others speak with rapture, this he knows for certain. Here, to be sure, we are dealing with sensory experiences which one commonly neglects to bring into as intimate contact with experiences of the soul

p. 122

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in the narrower sense as would accord with the truth, and therefore makes up for only with effort. Let us, then, choose a second example! When the witch, in a trance state amid the most extreme tortures, perhaps already gnawed at by the flames of the stake, begins to smile, indeed to sing, then the breathlessly greedy crowd of gaping flatheads will, to be sure, never understand or know what sort of bliss it may be that a person feels who is about to be roasted; but that this experience of the witch belongs to the genus of blisses seems to the crowd gruesomely, incomprehensibly certain, and it calls it a satanic bliss, instilled by the prince of hell. Let one hold to this immediately convincing occurrence, which makes vivid, considerably even beyond what has been offered so far, the possibility of origin as well as the manner of conduct of “ressentiment,” and, insofar as one does not wish to venture the dissection oneself, let one rest content with the assurance that it supplies without gaps the elements whose appropriate combination makes intelligible not merely the knowledge of a that along with a what invented for the most part erroneously, but to some extent even the peculiar vehemence with which the wounded will reacts against the certainty of being excluded.

We cannot refrain from weaving in here a passage from Schopenhauer, who of all Nietzsche’s predecessors contributed, if we are not mistaken, by far the best precisely to the doctrine of ressentiment. “That man, at the sight of another’s enjoyment and possessions, should feel his own lack more bitterly is natural, indeed unavoidable: only this ought not to arouse his hatred against the more fortunate one: but it is precisely in this that envy proper consists. Least of all, however, should it arise where the occasion is not the gifts of fortune or chance or of another’s favor, but those of nature; because everything innate rests on a metaphysical ground . . But unfortunately envy does just the reverse; it is most implacable toward personal advantages; hence understanding, and genius above all, must first beg forgiveness in the world wherever they are not in a position to be permitted proudly and boldly to despise the world . . For envy directed at natural gifts and personal advantages, such as beauty is in women and intellect in men, there is no consolation of the one kind and no hope of the other; so that nothing remains to it but to hate those so favored bitterly and implacably. Hence its sole wish is to take revenge on its

p. 123

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

object. In this, however, it finds itself in the unfortunate position that all its blows fall powerless as soon as it comes to light that they proceeded from it. That is why it hides itself as carefully as the secret sins of lust, and now becomes an inexhaustible inventor of ruses, tricks, and dodges by which to veil and mask itself in order to wound its object unseen. It will, for example, ignore with the most unselfconscious expression the advantages that consume its heart, not see them at all, not know them, never have noticed or heard of them, and thus prove itself a master of dissimulation. With great subtlety it will seemingly overlook entirely, as insignificant, the one whose brilliant qualities gnaw at its heart . . But above all it will be at pains, through secret machinations, carefully to deprive those advantages of every opportunity to show themselves and become known. Then it will send out against them, from the dark, censure, scorn, mockery, and slander . . No less will it enthusiastically praise insignificant people, or even the mediocre, indeed the bad, in the same kind of achievement*. In short, it becomes a Proteus of stratagems in order to wound without showing itself.” (From “Zur Ethik,” Reclam vol. V, pp. 221–222)24). With all this, to be sure, Schopenhauer is far from locating the formative hearth of moral values in inner attitudes at least analogous to those whose private effects he describes so aptly. — But we must now at least sketch how the extremes of fullness of life look in the language of a doctrine that grants reality only to the will to power, and therewith we enter upon Nietzsche’s tendencies.

If one sets up the equation: values of the will = moral values, then Nietzsche is just as little an immoralist as Stirner, except that he founds the new morality he strives for not, like the latter, on the unfettering of self-will, i.e., of the ego, but on its binding through race-breeding values of life, which he clothes in the formulas of his doctrine of the will. He too, then, believes that mankind must be perfected, and is clear in his mind that in this way a violent intervention takes place in life which can by no means appeal to legal validities, since it is indeed he who first sovereignly determines what is to be called right and what wrong. But he now distinguishes change through taming from change through breeding, and traces the two methods

p. 124

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

back to two organizers different in type and combating each other: to the noble-warlike type, which for him represents the fullness of life, and to the priestly type, which for him represents the deficiency of life and its insidious vindictiveness.

“Selfishness is worth as much as the one who has it is worth physiologically*.” “At all times men have wanted to ‘improve’ mankind: this above all was called morality. But under the same word the most diverse tendencies are hidden. Both the taming of the beast man and the breeding of a particular species of man have been called ‘improvement’ . . To call the taming of an animal its ‘improvement’ is in our ears almost a joke. Whoever knows what goes on in menageries doubts that the beast is ‘improved’ there. It is weakened, it is made less harmful, through the depressive affect of fear . . it becomes a sickly beast. It is no different with the tamed man whom the priest has ‘improved.’ In the early Middle Ages, when the Church was indeed above all a menagerie, men everywhere hunted down the finest specimens of the ‘blond beast’; they ‘improved,’ for example, the noble Teutons. But what did such an ‘improved’ . . Teuton look like afterward? Like a caricature of man, like a monstrosity: he had become a ‘sinner,’ he was stuck in a cage, he had been locked up among nothing but terrible concepts . . Speaking physiologically: in the struggle with the beast, making it sick can be the only means of making it weak . . Let us take the other case of so-called morality, the case of breeding a particular race . . The most magnificent example of this is furnished by Indian morality, sanctioned as religion as the ‘Law of Manu.’ Here the task is set of breeding no fewer than four races at once: a priestly one, a warrior one, a trading and farming one, and finally a race of servants . . Obviously we are no longer among animal tamers here: a kind of man a hundred times milder and more rational is the precondition for even conceiving the plan of such a breeding . . How wretched the ‘New Testament’ is beside Manu, how bad it smells! But this organization too needed to be terrible; this time not in the struggle with the beast but with its antithetical concept, the unbred man, the hodgepodge man, the Chandala. And again it had no other means of making him harmless . . than making him sick; it was the

p. 125

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

struggle with the ‘great number.’ Perhaps there is nothing more repugnant to our feeling than these protective measures of Indian morality” (G).

About what is actually supposed to be bred, and not merely among the Indians of Manu but everywhere that wishful images of breeding hover before men at all, we are more precisely informed in the 9th main part of J, “What is noble?”, and especially in No. 262, from which we give at least a few more sentences. “Here men are together and thrown upon themselves who want to make their kind prevail, mostly because they must prevail or else run the terrible risk of being exterminated. Here there is lacking . . that protection (under which) variation is favored; the kind needs itself as a kind, as something that can make itself durable precisely by virtue of its hardness . . in constant struggle with its neighbors or with the oppressed, who are . . threatening rebellion. The most manifold experience teaches it to which qualities . . it owes the fact that, in defiance of all gods and men, it is still there, that it has always been victorious: these qualities it calls virtues, these virtues alone it breeds to greatness. It does so with hardness . .; every aristocratic morality is intolerant, in the education of youth, in the disposal of women, in marriage customs, in the relation of old and young, in the penal laws . .; it counts intolerance itself among the virtues, under the name ‘justice.’” But with this we are already at the essential character of the organizers.

According to Nietzsche, as is well known, all mankind divides into masters (nobles, aristocrats) and slaves, and each of the two kinds has its leaders, who represent most perfectly the basic direction of the will to power peculiar to each. We now speak of the master men, and indeed with regard to their leader natures. They are characterized by “perfect functional certainty of the regulating unconscious instincts” (ZG), by a “superabundance of plastic, reshaping, healing power, which also makes one forget” (Mirabeau; further Napoleon, “the incarnate problem of the noble ideal in itself” (ZG)), by an overcharge of power and an activity inseparable from it (ZG), hence by inwardly conditioned self-activity (spontaneity), by unlimited self-assertion (in the sense, however, of the assertion of their type), and by an equally unlimited intolerance together with a hardness that shrinks from nothing toward the slave man who lacks their advantages. Seen from the standpoint of the will, the noble man accordingly represents the will equipped with

p. 126

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

all means for the realization of power, which either assimilates every outside to itself or ruthlessly subjugates it; seen from the standpoint of life, he ought to represent an ascending life, whose “selfishness” would be necessary and imperative because it would signify an unceasingly creative overflowing. One senses at once that the two aspects by no means agree, and we shall show later that they fall apart altogether. Here, for reasons of the development of our thought, we hold predominantly to the aspect of power.

If one looked only at the historical individuals by whom Nietzsche exemplifies his master type — besides the two already named, there are mentioned, for instance, Caesar, the Hohenstaufen emperor Frederick II, Cesare Borgia, Frederick the Great —, one could hardly resist the impression that here we have an ingeniously composed wishful image to which no human being who ever existed has even approximately corresponded. If, on the other hand, one keeps more strictly to the standard concept of the type, or at any rate to a personification, heightened into the mythically leonine, of the spirit of certain ages, peoples, groups, then it cannot be denied that one feels enriched in visual power for not a few traits, previously not seen at all or seen falsely, of, for example, the Indian Kshatriyas, the Japanese samurai, the Homeric and in part still the Periclean Greeks, the Germanic “barbarians,” the Scandinavian Vikings, the Moorish conquerors, but above all of the Romans, who the longer, the more furnished Nietzsche with the model that he sought to recreate poetically in his bearer of master morality; whereby he contributed, if not to the solution, then at least to the exhibition of the riddles of Caesarism that had fallen into oblivion, and completed his opposition to the Empire-style conception of antiquity of the 18th century through the shift of the weight of significance from Hellas to Rome, a shift whose consequences are still incalculable25). It must at any rate be held fast that, notwithstanding the picturesque lights and colors which do after all belong to Nietzsche’s palette, this type certainly stands nearer to the essence of the Caesarian than to the romantic vision of the heroic, and above all refuses to be corrupted into any sort of “overman.”

A few words suffice about the “morality” of the noble man of this sort. Such men call good what corresponds to their kind, but the kind opposed to it bad. If one wishes to see such goodness and such badness each unfolded on a sample card of traits, then on the former would stand: truthfulness, hardness, bravery, contempt of death, the highest

p. 127

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

chivalry and an age-hallowed bearing in dealings with one’s equals, together with the most violent arbitrariness toward the Chandala, self-control, the capacity for reverence, gratitude, piety, pride; on the latter: mendacity, cowardice, narrow-heartedness, self-interest, unfreedom, doggishness. The bearer of the positive traits is honored by the noble man, even though he were his enemy unto life and death; for “he demands his enemy for himself, after all, as his distinction, he endures . . no other enemy than one in whom there is nothing to despise and very much to honor” (ZG); the bearer of the negative traits is despised by him. Bad and contemptible coincide for him, and justice, that virtue which — almost Platonically — comprises everything in itself, consists for him in giving everything to the noble and withdrawing everything from the ignoble (according to the principle “to equals equality, to unequals inequality” (G))26) and, if necessary, in safeguarding at the stake of his life the “privilege of the fewest” (ZG). In the responsibility for this his right becomes an inexorable duty. — One may, by the way, gauge from this ethics of nobility, compiled precisely according to Nietzsche’s own utterances, how immeasurably far our destroyer of slave morality was from anarchy!

Even one who might be inclined to see in all this only the possibly appealing precipitate of poetic shaping powers, not so very different from those which, within the framework of another age and taste, enabled a Schiller to create, say, the Marquis Posa (who is, after all, a type, not an individual being), would in all probability have to acknowledge that this pattern is eminently suited to making visible, by contrast, all the “dark sides” of the life-negating type, down to its finest wrinkles and crinklings; and whoever wished to try his hand at the attractive task of discovering how Nietzsche arrived at his type might perhaps find out that through the dissection of the “ideals” he had discovered a type of value-creating envy, had formed from it, in accordance with his belief in the will to power, a positive type by inversion, and enriched this with traits stemming from somewhere quite different, from life itself. But however that may be, Nietzsche’s findings on the procedure of envy of life hit the mark blow upon blow, and would retain their fundamental significance even if his master man were only a gripping phantasm.

p. 128

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

The slave man proceeds, with respect to the creation of values, in a way somewhat similar to what we just now conjecturally assumed of Nietzsche’s formation of theory: he calls the fullness of power from which he is excluded and of which he is afraid damnable, devilish, evil, and subsequently invents in addition a concept of the good by glorifying all the traits of his incapacity for it! Busch’s verse: “The good, this proposition holds — Is always the evil that one leaves undone,” would be complete in this respect if we were to add: because one lacked the courage or the strength for it. But what a diabolical masterpiece of forgery value-creating revenge has thereby actually accomplished upon life might appear to us in faithful enlargement through a transposition to the sphere of sensuousness, where, to be sure, it would mostly remain impracticable. Suppose the fox, who avenges himself for his failure with the grapes by declaring them sour, knew how, beyond this, to convince his fellow animals that the sweetness of the grapes was poisonous and that it was wise to spurn them, or suppose the ugly rival instilled, through God knows what juggler’s arts, into the majority of the human race the delusion that ugliness of body was divine but beauty devilish, and brought it about that the bearers of beauty became the object of the fanatical abhorrence of the now legitimate disposition: then one would have vividly before one’s eyes what, with respect to the highest values of life, herd and slave morality and — let us say it at once — that of Christianity above all has actually brought about; since, after all, the demonizing of bodily beauty really did belong, among other things, to the program of the ecclesia militans! That is the other cornerstone of Nietzsche’s critique of morality.

Unable to participate in the life that is denied him at least through loving veneration of those rich in it, unable also to commit suicide, unable finally to create substitutes for himself, the bearer of envy of life becomes the insatiable hater of life and, in the face of the impossibility of extirpating those beloved by life together with the memory of them and their works at a single stroke, accomplishes a kind of creative deed: he invents the “evil enemy,” and this is none other than precisely the one rich in life, especially in the form of the powerful, the sovereign, the noble, “reinterpreted by the poisonous eye of ressentiment” (ZG). But when Nietzsche occasionally adds that the subsequent invention of the “good” concerns him, the hater of life, himself (ZG), then it should be maintained, on the contrary (as

p. 129

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

also shines forth clearly, however, from other passages), that it concerns only his incapacity for the opposite, inasmuch as it unfolds this incapacity in such dispositions as are harmless, useful, and beneficial for him, the one who is in truth infamous. Here, therefore, everything connects that Nietzsche considered for the negative critique of the mainsprings of the herd, such as obligingness, helpfulness, pity; which we have already treated in the chapter on love of one’s neighbor. This time we cite a somewhat larger series of supporting passages, so that one may feel the compelling force of Nietzsche’s theory of ressentiment and the deadly weight of his thrust to the heart against European “idealism.”

“Whoever is dissatisfied with himself is continually ready to take revenge for it” (F). “While all noble morality grows out of a triumphant saying of Yes to oneself, slave morality says No from the outset to an ‘outside,’ to an ‘other,’ to a ‘not-self’: and this No is its creative deed . . In order to arise, slave morality always first needs a counter-world . ., its action is fundamentally reaction” (ZG). “The fact ‘I am perishing’ translated into the imperative: ‘you shall all perish’” (EH). “On such a soil of self-contempt . . every . . poisonous plant grows . . Here the worms of feelings of revenge and rancor swarm; here the air stinks of . . things unconfessed; here the net of the most malignant conspiracy is constantly being spun, the conspiracy of the suffering against the well-constituted and victorious, here the aspect of the victorious is hated.” “Oh, how they . . thirst to be hangmen. Among them there are in abundance the vengeful disguised as judges, who constantly carry the word ‘justice’ in their mouths like a poisonous spittle . . always ready to spit upon everything that does not look dissatisfied and goes its way in good spirits” (ZG). “Thus I speak to you in a parable, you who make the soul whirl, you preachers of equality! Tarantulas you are to me, and hidden vengeful ones! . . For that man be redeemed from revenge: that is for me the bridge to the highest hope and a rainbow after long storms. — But the tarantulas, of course, would have it otherwise. ‘Precisely this shall be called justice by us, that the world be filled with the storms of our revenge,’ thus they speak with one another. — ‘Revenge will we practice and abuse upon all who are not equal to us,’ thus the tarantula-hearts vow. — ‘And “will to equality” — that itself shall henceforth become the name for virtue;

Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 9

p. 130

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and against everything that has power we will raise our outcry!’ — And when they become subtle and cold, it is not the spirit but envy that makes them subtle and cold” (Z). “For an ascetic life is a self-contradiction: here there reigns a ressentiment without equal, that of an unsatisfied instinct and will to power that would like to become master . . over life itself, over its deepest, strongest, lowest conditions; here an attempt is made to use strength in order to stop up the sources of strength; here the gaze is directed, green and spiteful, against physiological flourishing itself . . against . . beauty, joy; while in failure, atrophy, in pain, in misfortune, in the ugly, in voluntary loss, in unselfing, self-flagellation, self-sacrifice a pleasure is felt and sought” (ZG). “The concept ‘God’ invented as the counter-concept to life — in it everything harmful, poisoning, slanderous, the whole mortal enmity against life brought into one horrible unity! The concept ‘beyond,’ ‘true world’ invented in order to devalue the only world there is . . The concept ‘soul,’ ‘spirit,’ finally even ‘immortal soul’ invented in order to despise the body . . The concept ‘sin’ invented together with the instrument of torture belonging to it, the concept ‘free will,’ in order to confuse the instincts, in order to make mistrust of the instincts second nature!” (Eh). “Weakness is to be lied into a merit . . and impotence that does not retaliate into ‘goodness’; anxious lowliness into ‘humility’; submission to those whom one hates into ‘obedience’ . . The inoffensiveness of the weak, cowardice itself . . here comes to a good name, as ‘patience,’ it is even called the virtue; not-being-able-to-take-revenge is called not-wanting-to-take-revenge, perhaps even forgiveness (‘for they know not what they do — we alone know what they do!’). They also speak of ‘love of one’s enemies’ — and sweat while doing so . . They are wretched, there is no doubt . . but they tell me their wretchedness is a choosing and a distinction on God’s part . . perhaps this wretchedness is also a preparation, a trial, a schooling, perhaps it is even more — something that will one day . . be paid out with enormous interest in gold, no, in happiness. This they call ‘blessedness’ . . . What they demand they call not retaliation, but the ‘triumph of justice’; what they hate is not their enemy, no, they hate ‘injustice,’ ‘godlessness’;

p. 131

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

what they believe and hope is not the hope of revenge, the intoxication of sweet revenge . ., but ‘the victory of God, of the just God, over the godless’; what remains for them to love on earth is not their brothers in hatred, but their ‘brothers in love,’ as they say, all the good and just on earth” (ZG).

More than once, by the way, in the ‘world history’ known to us, the party of those vengeful out of envy of life has betrayed what their “morality” ultimately comes down to. Let one read over the passages that Nietzsche gathered in A 45 from Mark, Matthew, Luke, and Paul, or in the 15th chapter of the First Essay of ZG from Thomas Aquinas and Tertullian, and one will have to confess, provided one finds the courage for it, that every attempt at reinterpretation founders on them. But there is no need of the excited rhetoric of Christians. For in the sober language of philosophical equanimity their ancient forerunners, the Stoics, reveal to us the very same course of development of “humanity”; for which it may suffice to have referred to these words of their most celebrated herald: ‘The best way to take revenge on someone is not to repay evil with evil!’ (Marcus Aurelius).

Here we further add Nietzsche’s two mightiest pronouncements on the nature of the state and on socialism, because they must definitely be regarded as the two strongest applications of his doctrine of ressentiment. “State is the name of the coldest of all cold monsters. Coldly it lies, too; and this lie creeps from its mouth: ‘I, the state, am the people.’ . . Annihilators they are who set traps for the many and call them state: they hang a sword and a hundred appetites over them. — Where there is still a people, it does not understand the state and hates it as the evil eye and as sin against customs and rights . . But the state lies in all the tongues of good and evil; and whatever it says, it lies — and whatever it has, it has stolen . . Confusion of the tongues of good and evil: this sign I give you as the sign of the state. Verily, the will to death this sign signifies! Verily, it beckons to the preachers of death! — Far too many are born: for the superfluous the state was invented! — Just look how it lures them to itself, the many-too-many! How it swallows them and chews them and chews them again! . . Yes, a hellish artifice was invented there, a horse of death, clattering in the finery of divine honors! . .

9*

p. 132

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

State I call it where all are drinkers of poison, the good and the bad: state, where all lose themselves, the good and the bad: state, where the slow suicide of all is called — ‘life.’ — Just look at these superfluous ones! They steal for themselves the works of the inventors and the treasures of the wise: culture they call their theft — and everything turns to sickness and trouble for them! — Just look at these superfluous ones! Sick they always are, they vomit their gall and call it a newspaper . . Just look at these superfluous ones! Riches they acquire and become poorer thereby. Power they want, and first of all the crowbar of power, much money. — . . There, where the state ceases, there first begins the man who is not superfluous” (Z). “Socialism is the fantastic younger brother of an almost decrepit despotism, whose heir it wants to be; its endeavors are thus in the deepest sense reactionary. For it desires a fullness of state power such as only despotism has ever had, indeed it outbids everything past in that it strives for the formal annihilation of the individual: who appears to it as an unjustified luxury of nature and is to be improved by it into an expedient organ of the community . . . It needs the most submissive prostration of all citizens before the absolute state, such as has never existed; and since it can no longer even count on the old religious piety toward the state, but rather must involuntarily work continually toward its removal — . ., it can only hope for existence here and there for brief periods by means of the most extreme terrorism. Therefore it prepares itself in secret for reigns of terror and drives the word ‘justice’ like a nail into the heads of the half-educated masses, in order to rob them completely of their understanding (after this understanding has already suffered greatly through half-education) and to create for them a good conscience for the evil game they are to play” (MA). Note: published in 1878!

We had placed the description of the type of the master man before his principles of value, and have just now, conversely, begun with the description of the slave man’s mode of valuation, because the characterization of his type, which must now be supplied, brings us, it is true, essentially closer still to the nerve center of Nietzsche’s thought, but precisely thereby into a field of problems that must be critically plowed up. — While on the plus side we distinguished between leaders and led merely nominally,

p. 133

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

on the minus side the herd to be led and the leading shepherd, who, whenever, wherever, and in whatever form and disguise he may appear, is a priest in essence, are thoroughly distinct. This explains itself simply enough: among the men of power, the genius for finding laws that is required here too — say Manu, Zarathustra — stands to the others roughly as a practical prudence that potentiates success stands to acting from instinct; among the incapable and those forsaken by life, on the other hand, the shepherd stands to the others roughly as the physician to the sick. The very close relation thereby posited between priest and physician, both understood in a relatively original sense, unquestionably persists through the whole breadth of the historical transformations of their appurtenances: from the sorcerer-priest of primitive peoples, who is infallibly at the same time a medicine man, to the “pastor of souls” of a church that dispenses to its confessors the ‘consolations of faith’; and it shimmers through unmistakably when, conversely, one expects of the capable physician a certain skill in the knowledgeable concomitant treatment of souls! Nietzsche drew the veil from the grounds and background grounds of all priestdom; but before we speak of this, we must know what the minus side actually is, how it came about, through what leaders and led complement each other, and in what they differ.

We reach the goal most quickly by starting from the last question. The ascetic priest shares with the suffering their sufferings, but not their helplessness against them. “He must himself be sick . ., but . . unimpaired . . in his will to power.” “He brings salves and balm with him . ., but first he needs to wound in order to be a physician; in stilling the pain that the wound causes, he at the same time poisons the wound” (ZG). This, roughly, is the leitmotif that may show us the way to a more precise orientation. — The will to power of the priest-physician is, to be sure, his own will to rule over the herd and finally over all mankind; but, more deeply understood, it is the will of sickness itself, which created in him a consciousness and with it the means of in truth making war, under the appearance of a struggle against sickness, rather on health. For Nietzsche, and indeed in fact, it does not matter all that much how far one attributes to the false “savior” a consciousness of this as well: usually he will not possess it, and in any case never with the clarity of Nietzsche, in whom

p. 134

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

— for reasons to be discussed later — the same disposition, thanks to such consciousness, rages against itself; rather, he will act, with instinctive genius, indeed quite properly out of the instinct of sickness become genius, like the slyest of deceivers, to be sure, yet in doing so will commonly believe in the “holiness” of his measures himself. For the present we must, as far as is at all possible, merely report, and we ask the reader, should contradictions strike him at once, to rest assured that their resolution will follow within this chapter.

Among the measures of the priest-physician Nietzsche distinguishes the relatively “innocent” ones from the thoroughly infamous ones. In order to understand the former, we must anticipate some features of his conception of the type of the sick person to be treated. These so-called sick persons naturally suffer from some malformation or other of their body, the consequences of which, however, do not come to their consciousness as other bodily illnesses normally do, but in the form of paralyzing feelings of depression and displeasure. When Nietzsche likes to use words such as “feeling of fatigue,” “feeling of exhaustion” for this, one must nevertheless not think here of feelings of fatigue of the body; rather, what must be understood by them is a listlessness that tinges everything, something remotely comparable to “constitutional ill-humor,” a growing apathy and innermost incapacity for enjoyment, an inability for any heartfelt sympathy, ultimately and at bottom the exclusion from life that we have described. For only thus does the second main feature of the said sickness become to some extent intelligible: the enormously overemphasized reactivity; for even if life were equated with the will to power, a simply weak will would after all by no means be the same as a violently reacting will, which, at least for the group of the sick most important to us, as we shall presently discuss, plays the decisive role. The sick of this group “enjoy their suspicion . ., the brooding over wickednesses and apparent injuries; they rummage through the entrails of their past . . for dark, questionable stories where they are free to wallow in a tormenting suspicion and to intoxicate themselves on their own poison of malice; they tear open the oldest wounds, they bleed to death from scars long healed, they make evildoers of friend, wife, child, and whatever else stands nearest to them. ‘I suffer: someone must

p. 135

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

be to blame for it’ — thus thinks every sickly sheep” (ZG). Here now the priest-physician steps in and says to them: “‘Quite right, my sheep, someone must be to blame for it: but you yourself are this someone, you yourself alone are to blame for it, you yourself alone are to blame for yourself!’” With this “the direction of ressentiment is — changed” (ZG). Thus arise the paralogical concepts: guilt, sin, corruption, with which those who believe in them are at least “among themselves,” strictly speaking alone with themselves. But what is achieved thereby, and in what would the harmless remedy actually consist?

There are several of them, of which the most important runs: precisely that you will and wish all sorts of things is your perversity; cease to will and to wish, and you are “redeemed” from your sufferings. For the translation of this doctrine of salvation, spread throughout the world but above all Indian, which turns to the individual without Christian missionary zeal, into the reality of a cure there serves a “system of hypnotizing means,” such as fakirs, Hesychasts, and also many medieval ecstatics have variously developed and practiced. Detachment from sensuousness, turning away from the world, not-knowing, inner stillness, finally the eventlessness and sufferinglessness of deep sleep sanctifies itself as unio mystica with God. — Already somewhat less harmless is the prescription of mechanical activity. “That a suffering existence is thereby relieved to a not inconsiderable degree is beyond all doubt: today this fact is called, somewhat dishonestly, ‘the blessing of work.’ The relief consists in the interest of the sufferer being diverted on principle from suffering . .” For to mechanical activity belong “absolute regularity, punctual, thoughtless obedience . ., the filling up of time, a certain permission for, indeed a discipline in ‘impersonality,’ in self-forgetting” (ZG). Since later we shall be able to cast only a side glance at it, let only the parenthetical remark find a place here that Nietzsche regards the spirit of science as a diluted ascetic one and the scientific conduct of life as a diluted Christian one, and in the most diverse places (especially in U, J, and ZG) has put forward the reasons for this with a dialectic whose brilliance and acuity must sweep even the opponent away to admiration. Here let the passage suffice that fits precisely this context: “The capability of our best scholars, their thoughtless diligence, their head smoking day and night, their

p. 136

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

very mastery of their craft — how often does all this have its real meaning in no longer letting something become visible to oneself! Science as a means of self-anesthesia: do you know that?” (ZG).

Still somewhat less harmless, and already playing over into the opposite side, is the already discussed ordaining of the “happiness of the slightest superiority” in the form of public spirit and “love of one’s neighbor,” about which we add that this class of values too was invented by the priest-physician; for on this there now climbs up, with the prospect of growing strong into an expansive power and of positing demanding values on its own part, the self-feeling of our reactively ill-humored ones; whereby the sickness, which has by no means been removed, would be on the point of spreading over to the healthy, or at least of narrowing their living space.

We are dealing, let it not be forgotten for a moment, always with an inner discord, and we establish with regard to the cures so far: they are not directed against this duality, let alone against its real causes, but aim, like narcotics, at the numbing of the consciousness of suffering, and this partly through an overall damping of the feeling of life, partly through the production of certain triumphs of the will at the expense of the body. If we lay the utmost emphasis on this, and place the overall valuation of all conduct in its service, then we have arrived at the absolutely soul-murdering practices and at that method which redeems not so much life from sickness as rather sickness from life, and this not, say, through turning away from life, but through favoring its spread over the remaining domain of health; whereby the spur of competition would, to be sure, be removed in a somewhat surprising way! The ascetic ideal thus serves an unheard-of debauchery of feeling. Every affect can be used for it, but none more successfully than the feeling of guilt and the bad conscience. “‘Sin’ . . has hitherto been the greatest event in the history of the sick soul . . Man, suffering from himself . . is to understand his suffering itself as a state of punishment” (ZG).
