Thirsting for pains and ever more pains, he rises in convulsions of self-torture to the most unheard-of raptures of power, which strive to transform the whole world into precisely that nothing which he, the bearer of the now self-affirming will to nothingness, including the belief in the divinity of nothingness, strives after for himself with fanatical fervor. “To think evil

p. 137

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is to make evil. — The passions become evil and malicious when they are regarded as evil and malicious” (M). “Christianity is an uprising of everything that crawls along the ground against that which has height: the gospel of the ‘lowly’ makes low” (A).

Only from this does one recognize to what extent, according to the programmatic declaration that we placed at the head, the priest-physician is to be regarded as an inventive genius in matters of envy of life and, as it were, serves as the helmsman of ressentiment. Now that the type, too, has thereby become somewhat known to us, we are all the more eager to learn how it could actually have come into being. — We here assemble, for the plus side as for the minus side, the designations most favored by Nietzsche, and remark that the terms on the left as well as those on the right are in each case of equal meaning.

Plus side. Minus side. Ascending, growing life Sinking, declining, decadent, degenerate,

deteriorated, poisoned, corrupted, ill-con‐

stituted life. Fullness, superabundance Poverty, impoverishment. Mighty, strong, growing will to power (= Weak, impotent will to power (= exhaushappiness) tion, self-torment, self-contempt). Evil, fear-inspiring (= good) Bad, contemptible (= good). Greatness Lowliness. Affirmation, gratitude, reverence Negation, hatred, nihilism.

Even if we accept the erroneous equation: life = will to power, a single glance at the foregoing synoptic table nevertheless betrays to us that on the left as on the right two groups of meaning, which in each case fall completely apart, have been wrongly equated with one another. If we stay first with the upper division, “life,” then growing life may well go together with superabundance and fullness: but why should an impoverishing and self-exhausting life necessarily be something poisoned and corrupted? Weakness, after all, is not venomousness, and venomousness can be paired with the mightiest strength! Since it is in principle a matter of indifference for vitalistic units of meaning whether we use them of the soul or of the body, we best make the untenability of the equation clear to ourselves by applying it to the body.

p. 138

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Is a constitutionally or temporarily severely exhausted man, a very anemic man, a man in extreme old age, and whatever other kinds and degrees of fatigue there may be, therefore poisoned, corrupted, deformed, crooked, cramped, and in that sense ill-constituted? Let one picture to oneself, conversely, one of those creeping infections that go on eating their way underground (say, lues), and no one will wish to maintain that its decisive symptom is tiredness and exhaustion! And now let one think, finally, of psychic forms of poverty such as lack of interest, indifference, unexcitability, dullness, apathy, and set beside them the “evil eye” of spiteful envy, the scheming arts of the jaundiced intriguer, the triumphs of malicious glee, and above all the tremendous manifestations of energy of ressentiment, of whose vehemence precisely the above excerpts from Nietzsche irresistibly convinced us, and one asks oneself in astonishment how so penetrating a mind could confuse such things with fatigue and exhaustion! It would remain possible that poverty of life offers a particularly favorable soil for psychic infections; but apart from the fact that the investigator of phenomena can commit no worse error than the confusion of states of affairs with the presumed causes of states of affairs, there are certainly still quite other reasons for the infectability of the cell of life, which we shall mention further below.

It was naturally not a lack of acuity that drove Nietzsche’s thinking here in an impossible direction, but the same drive-like compulsion that had induced him to carry the will to power into life. For if life, indeed reality itself, is at bottom nothing but will to power, then all differences of quality ultimately go back to differences in the quantum of will to power, and every kind of ill-constituted life would consistently have to consist in a too little of will, appearing thus as a declining, sinking, fatigued life. — However, even when we turn our eyes to the lower half of the synoptic table, where the will to power predominates, the contradiction confronts us, and almost even more glaringly. We understand to what extent a strong will to power might have happiness in its train, a weak one fatigue and apathy; but we by no means understand why the strength of the will to power must expend itself in affirmations, and we reject as absurd the notion that the mere weakness

p. 139

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of the will should explode in deeds, works, and words of the most malignant negation, the most long-lasting hatred, the most cunning vindictiveness!

When we developed above the priest-physician’s attempts at a cure in Nietzsche’s sense, it will have struck more than one of our readers that at least the first of the “harmless” remedies, the Indian doctrine of Nirvana, does not agree at all with the others. Nietzsche’s works give us numerous important insights into the inner relation of Buddhism to Christianity; but nowhere was he able to tell us unambiguously in what the two agree, and still less has he told us in what they fundamentally diverge. The inflamed concept of sin, the real explosive of Christianity, has no place at all, after all, in eastern Asia; and if we rightly hold the belief in Nirvana responsible for the deepening of the capacity for suffering and of sensitivity to pain, we could nevertheless by no means of dialectic derive from this condition the teeth-baring snappishness, curse-spitting venomousness, and aggressive conversion-mania of — Pauline Christianity!

In order to do justice to Nietzsche’s concepts of the type of the minus side, we must first strike from them everything that has crept in from declining, sinking life, from the feeling of fatigue and weakness of will, and hold instead to the reactivity, which he too, at any rate, overemphasized and in any case alone made use of in his conclusions, arising from poisonedness, corruptedness, deformity, crampedness, ill-constitutedness. Nietzsche’s unique strength lies in the characterization of envy of life, in the unmasking of its ressentiment, in the demonstration of the “world-historical” consequences of a value-creating vindictiveness. But how this arose, how it could have arisen at all, that he did not know, although he secretly knew that it had remained denied to him to fathom it. Of this we shall now make sure.

“With regard to artists of every kind I now make use of this chief distinction: has hatred against life or superabundance of life here become creative?” (NcW). That is a clear and acceptable contrast. “I call an animal, a species, an individual corrupted when it loses its instincts, when it chooses, when it prefers what is disadvantageous to it” (A). Here we would already have to ask: disadvantageous in what respect? The will to power of these corrupted ones,

p. 140

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which according to Nietzsche is life, has, with the help of the falsifications of which their instinct made them capable, indeed celebrated precisely its highest triumphs! “Every art, every philosophy may be regarded as . . a means of help of growing or of declining life: they always presuppose suffering and sufferers. But there are two kinds of sufferers, first those who suffer from the superabundance of life, who want a Dionysian art . . and then those who suffer from the impoverishment of life, who demand of art and philosophy rest, stillness, a smooth sea, or else intoxication, convulsion, stupefaction. Revenge on life itself — the most voluptuous kind of intoxication for such impoverished ones!” (NcW). In the emphasized “or” the thought breaks, and with the addition that revenge on life procures the most desired intoxication, something thoroughly new announces itself, which is, of course, precisely the thing on which everything exclusively depends! “Religious neurosis appears as a form of ‘evil being’: of that there is no doubt. What is it? Quaeritur” (ZG). This Quaeritur is repeated analogously in several places: it is not only the question that Nietzsche cannot answer, but at the same time the question mark that he places behind his own answers. We convince ourselves, however, how hopelessly he in fact fails in the attempt, which to be sure he also undertook alongside, to make the origin of revenge on life comprehensible.

Because, in accordance with his equation: life = will to power, he must regard the generation and intensification of the wishing state as essentially of the same kind as the generation of an “inner world” in general, he arrives at the dazzling finding that that something in the body which we are accustomed to call the soul arises through inhibition of drive-impulses naturally directed outward. “All instincts that do not discharge themselves outwardly turn inward — this is what I call the internalization of man . . The whole inner world, originally thin, as if stretched between two skins, has spread apart . . has acquired depth, breadth, height, in the same measure as the discharge of man outwardly has been inhibited” (ZG). Even in the face of this brilliant thought we must stress the necessity of the question: is it the inhibition of natural unfoldings of the drives, e.g., as a result of tighter group formation and the growing power of custom, that brings about internalization, or is it an internalization taking place for other reasons, with which, among other things, the propensity to regulate the drives by all manner of statutes to be held sacred

p. 141

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

goes hand in hand? Should someone believe he had found, through inner analysis, a way to the solution of the question, he would above all have to test his results against the history of the inhibitions of drives, beginning from the taboos of primitives down to the most delicate feelings of responsibility of a Protestant city-dweller of the 19th century, and he would further have to try to establish, drawing on many accompanying phenomena, how far an internalization inhibited in its drives is to be interpreted from the point of view of the impairment of life, how far from that of the deepening of life. This much, however, should no longer be doubtful: under otherwise comparable circumstances inwardness grows with the growing obstruction of immediate unfolding of the drives, and likewise the other way round.

If, then, Nietzsche’s greatness proves itself here in a fundamental discovery, for which, namely, thanks to its thousandfold application by him, the thought has to count, the limit of this greatness at once shows itself to us in classic form when we read the sentences that follow upon those just adduced. “Those fearful bulwarks with which the political organization protected itself against the old instincts of freedom . . brought it about that all those instincts of the wild, free, roving man . . turned against man himself. Enmity, cruelty, the pleasure in persecution . . all of that turning against the possessors of such instincts: that is the origin of ‘bad conscience’” (ZG). Out of inwardness, at one stroke, “bad conscience” has come to be, and thus out of the poet the liar (“The poets lie too much”; “All gods are . . poets’ contrivance” (Z)), out of Homer — we have heard it already — a thwarted Achilles, out of Goethe a Faust who missed the mark, and out of Nietzsche, that we may not pass it over in silence, that which he declares himself to be in his last work (WzM), a — nihilist. We have thereby already touched upon the fearful self-contradiction that Nietzsche must accept in order to let envy of life come into being, and we shall now uncover it completely.

“I take bad conscience to be the deep sickness into which man had to fall under the pressure of that most fundamental of all the changes he has ever experienced; that change when he found himself finally enclosed within the spell of society and of peace.” The half-animals, hitherto adapted to war and to roaming, “no longer had their old guides for this new unknown world

p. 142

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

, the regulating drives that guide unconsciously and surely; they were reduced to thinking, inferring, calculating, combining . ., . . to their ‘consciousness,’ to their poorest and most fallible organ! I believe that never on earth has there been such a feeling of misery, such a leaden discomfort, — and at the same time those old instincts had not all at once ceased to make their demands! Only it was . . seldom possible to do their will: in the main they had to seek . . subterranean satisfactions for themselves . . . Man, who, for lack of external enemies and resistances, forced into an oppressive narrowness and regularity of custom, impatiently tore himself apart, persecuted, . . maltreated himself, this animal that rubbed itself raw against the bars of its cage . ., this deprived one, consumed with homesickness for the desert, who had to create out of himself . . a place of torture, a . . dangerous wilderness, this . . desperate prisoner became the inventor of ‘bad conscience.’ With him, however, was ushered in the greatest and most uncanny sickness, from which mankind has not recovered to this day, man’s suffering from man . . Let us add at once that, on the other hand, with the fact of an animal soul turned against itself . . something so new . ., unheard-of . ., full of contradiction and full of future had come into being on earth that the aspect of the earth was essentially altered thereby . . . Since then man has been counted among the most unexpected and most exciting throws of the dice played by the ‘great child’ of Heraclitus, be it called Zeus or chance; he arouses for himself an interest, a tension, a hope, almost a certainty, as though something were announcing itself with him, something preparing itself, as though man were not a goal but only a way . ., a bridge, a great promise” (ZG). However exciting and gripping this is as said, it is at bottom false. Either the procedure here depicted is the greatest and most uncanny sickness, and then, if it is not halted in time, it ends sooner or later infallibly in dissolution and total decay; or it is “a way, a bridge” to a superhumanly great future, and then it only appeared in the light of an uncanny sickness, without in truth being one. We stand here before the most inescapable either-or, and when Nietzsche in the same breath replaces it by a both-and, the unresolved conflict of his motives of thought betrays itself thereby most sharply. How little, incidentally, he himself was able to believe in his upward glance of hope

p. 143

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is betrayed to us a few pages later by the following sentences: “Here is . . the most fearful sickness that has hitherto raged in man; and whoever is still able to hear . . how in this night of torment and absurdity the cry love, the cry of the most yearning rapture, of redemption in love has sounded, turns away, seized by an invincible horror. In man there is so much that is dreadful! The earth has been a madhouse for too long already!” (ZG). We think that suffices. — But how did such a gruesome sickness come about?

“To the presupposition of this hypothesis on the origin of bad conscience belongs, first, that that change was not a . . voluntary one, . . but a break, a leap, a compulsion, an ineluctable fatality, against which there was no struggle and not even a ressentiment. Second, however, that the fitting of a hitherto uninhibited . . population into a fixed form . ., that the oldest ‘state’ . . was formed as a fearful tyranny . . I use the word ‘state’: it goes without saying whom I mean by it — some pack of blond beasts of prey, a race of conquerors and masters, which, organized for war and with the power to organize, unhesitatingly lays its fearful claws upon a population perhaps tremendously superior in number, but still formless, still roaming . . . Its work is an instinctive creating of forms, impressing of forms; they are the most involuntary, most unconscious artists there are: in short order something new stands there . . a structure of domination that lives, in which parts and functions are delimited and related to one another . . They do not know what guilt, what responsibility, what consideration is, these born organizers; in them there rules that fearful artist’s egoism which has a gaze like bronze and knows itself justified in advance for all eternity in its ‘work’ . . It is not in them that ‘bad conscience’ has grown . .; but it would not have grown without them, . . it would be lacking if, under the pressure of their hammer blows, . . a tremendous quantum of freedom had not been . . removed from the world and made, as it were, latent. This instinct of freedom, made latent by force . . that, only that, is in its beginning bad conscience” (ZG).

Here no evasion is possible any longer: the strong will to power, affirmation of life, creative fullness, in short the plus side, necessarily brings forth the minus side, and life is sick in itself. Were that so, then I can no longer affirm life,

p. 144

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

without also affirming envy of life, ressentiment, the revenge raging against life, the counterfeitings of idealism, weakness lied into merit, and Nietzsche’s pathos that battles against them, a pathos of the utmost hatred and of the utmost contempt, would be sheer folly. If, on the other hand, I dare to ascribe any cognitive value whatever to such a pathos, then what drives Nietzsche to the following words is not merely false but itself a gruesome manifestation of life-hostile nihilism: “Life is essentially appropriation, injury, overpowering of what is alien and weaker, suppression, hardness, . . and at the least . . exploitation” (J). And consequently the equation: life = will to power is false down to its very foundations.

It would be an easy matter for us to point out the same self-contradiction in Nietzsche’s attempt at a genealogy of right and wrong, further and above all of criminal law, of genuine justice, for which one may compare the second essay of ZG, in particular its 11th chapter (with the polemic against Dühring’s theory of revenge); but we must restrain ourselves all the more since our concluding critique will in any case show that the self-contradiction in question runs from beginning to end through everything Nietzsche wrote. If instead we here indicate in a few words the metaphysical foundations with whose help we ourselves have sought, and believe we have in part also found, an answer to those questions, this happens in part in order to attest beyond doubt that it is very definite counter-views that help us to uncover Nietzsche’s self-contradictions, but then and above all so that the reader may not always merely hear that something has been done wrongly there, but may at least see a possibility of how it might be done better, a possibility which, given the necessarily purely dogmatic formulation, wishes only to be commended to his reflection.

We separate metaphysically from the cell of life the spirit as an extramundane power, and with Nietzsche we find the key to the essence of the spirit not in the intellect but in the will. If for that reason we also say will instead of spirit, this accordingly signifies the metaphysical ground for the fact that in man, and only in man, the animal drive-impulses were for the most part transformed into impulses of will, and the drives gave way to a corresponding extent to the interests struggling for predominance. What brought about such a turn we leave

p. 145

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

undecided, and we meet the naturally obvious objection that nothing is more convenient, but also nothing more questionable, than simply to invent a new principle instead of explaining from given means, with the remark: supposing it can be proven with all rigor that lived experiences cannot be traced back to acts of will, nor acts of will to lived experiences, then we would not get around the assumption of at least two principles (ἀρχαί) even if every insight into the causes of their coupling were denied us for the present and even forever. That proof, however, can be furnished, although we cannot enter into it here27).

This premised, we now believe we see in the cell of life two fundamentally different conditions for the efficacy of the destructive will, to which there corresponds an equally fundamental difference of direction in the “spiritualizations.” — To understand this, one must know that in the cell of life soul (= the gift of beholding and of transformation) and body (= the capacity for sensation and self-movement) belong together polarly, so that neither could the soul live without the body nor the body without the soul, and every change on the one side goes hand in hand with a polarly opposite one on the other. — For the one direction of spiritualization, the East Asian — most profoundly thought through by Taoism, but best organized, with the help of a very noticeable admixture of the other, by Buddhism — the psychic occasioning grounds could most readily be compared in some measure to what Nietzsche sought to characterize as weakening, fatigue, exhaustion of life. More precisely, we are dealing with a disturbance according to which the cell of life suffered a loss in the intensity of its bodily pole, thus in vehemence of sensation and in strength of the drives, and consequently the soul suffered a loss in embodied presence, with a purity of its beholding that was at first unweakened, indeed less hindered by the body. The waking contents of lived experience thus approach, from loss of weight, the contents of dreams, and the idealisms from which we must read off in what way the parasitism of the spirit made use of the disturbance move without exception in the direction of a growing volatilization of the world, with the result, of course, of an estrangement from the world on the part of their bearers, whose aura may allure but can never infect.

The European direction of spiritualization, on the other hand, rests on a disturbance according to which, at intensities of tremendous tension, the cell of life Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 10

p. 146

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

falls prey to decomposition: the bodily pole becomes hostile to the psychic pole, the psychic pole to the bodily pole, and the idealisms one and all bear the stamp of inflammatory party programs, whose purpose it is to stir up strife, to deepen discord, and to wear down not only man against man but the life of the whole planet against the — will. Even the medieval interlude, seemingly aimed at the disembodiment of the soul, served in truth the most extreme schooling of the will and prepared the unsouling of the body that has been advancing rapidly since the “Renaissance.” This process — within, a gangrenous putrefaction — turns outward as murder spreading with frenzied speed. The end is ineluctable downfall; but no one can say how long the will-heated larva of the body will prolong its seemingly alive, life-devouring existence on the blood of all the “last Mohicans” of humankind, of animal-kind and plant-kind, before it itself perishes, how long the golem that is just now rising in sneering triumph will yet find occasion, before it bursts, for outbidding mimicry. Meanwhile we may well be nearer the goal than sages and the highly learned, than even men of letters let themselves dream28). — But let us return to Nietzsche and assure ourselves briefly how it was precisely he who lit the way ahead for the somber truths that we have just shown as if by flashlight.

PDF 151

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON THE PSYC HOLOGY

OF CHRISTIANITY

Of Nietzsche’s creative conflict between the life of the body and moral values, the pulsing point is formed by the conflict between the life of the body and Christian moral values, and with his doctrine of value-creating revenge on life he merely generalizes what he believed he had ascertained of the spirit of Christianity. We would therefore have nothing more of significance to add to the findings of the previous chapter, did not the application of a psychological theory to a fact of the history of religion offer the opportunity to show, in Nietzsche’s character as a thinker, a line without which our picture of his science of the soul would suffer from a most sensitive gap.

Whatever other occasioning grounds for the almost demonological-seeming pluralism29) of his manner of contemplation may have dwelt in our thinker, the circumstance that it is Christian wishes of the will which he pursued, with the clearest knowledge of the fact, into their most deceptive masquerade costumes, would alone have sufficed to enable him, in passing, to uproot a philosophical prejudice which — likewise grown up on Christian soil — claimed for itself like no other the dignity of impartial objectivity: namely, the faith, planted before judgment and sustaining it, in man, humanity, the faculty of knowledge. But what he opposes to such fictions, in which only the claims to omnipotence of Christendom and its καθ’ ὅλην τὴν γῆν find expression, are not, say, any particularities of individual beings, but the overarching characters of estates, peoples, ages, religious communities, cultures.

You speak of man, so he says in substance, but you mean the Christian, and therefore you apply to real man a canon which for all antiquity and for the whole of non-Christian humanity

10*

p. 148

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is invalid; you speak of reason without having asked and examined how many of the inner demands that are called rational by you are merely demands of a will bred in Christian fashion; you pursue physics, botany, zoology, write history and the history of evolution, busy yourselves with the descent of man, with the origin of science, the arts, morality, and whatever else you have, by your estimation, “brought so gloriously far,” and you have not the faintest inkling that in doing so you are led by the leading-strings of the imperative to find yourselves as the hidden purpose and crown of reality, that is, the bearer of Christian wishes for omnipotence! It was indeed reserved for the arrogant narrow-mindedness of the Christian to call the ridiculously short interlude of man capable of writing — “world history,” indeed with unexampled effrontery to let the course of the world, and as it were time itself, begin with the presumed year of birth of the real or supposed founder of his particular articles of faith, as though the aeons before had been merely the prelude to this event, with which the actual “play” on the stage of reality only now began!

If we must say today that with Nietzsche the psychology of culture in the grandest style began, we would nevertheless have only a modest prospect of continuing it if we have become clear that it obliges us first and foremost to interrogate our inclinations of thought, means of thought, aims of thought, our readinesses for holding-true and for doubting, our affirming as well as negating feelings of certainty, our conscience of truth as well as of value, as to their dependencies on the course of development of our Christian so-called culture. Whoever today still philosophizes about the character of man without such a preliminary interrogation, in order afterward to draw from it the characters of the Negro, the Chinese, and the present-day European, should in fact rather devote himself to banking or the postal service or some other useful occupation, and whoever today still pursues so-called critique of reason (= science of consciousness) with the scholastic naïveté of Kant would urgently be advised, insofar as his gifts permit, rather to till, e.g., the field of mathematics, where fine successes might perhaps bloom for him, instead of merely contributing a little in this way to the renewed heightening of the Chimborazo of — empty nuts! “Kant’s success is merely a theologian’s success” (A). With such partly harmlessly professorial, partly embarrassing and confusing, but always thoroughly unsound trifling

p. 149

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

it is over, since Nietzsche, once and for all. They too will die off, exactly as Christianity in its depths is already dead, notwithstanding the noise-crazed counter-clamor of today in which an almost endemically widespread hysteria groans itself out. But Christianity is dying off, and indeed down into its epistemological twig-tips, not, say, through Nietzsche and in consequence of him (consciousness is no effective power!), but, so to speak, from lack of sap, after it has fulfilled its “mission,” of which Nietzsche’s works, together with even his life, are for the one who knows the unmistakable symptom30).

If it is true, as we are told, that Hegel is just now experiencing a splendid resurrection, it might be necessary to set forth expressly why it was not he, say, who began, long before Nietzsche, with a psychology of culture or at least with its preparation. For what matters is not whether by the man one simply understands one’s own stock of unshakable convictions and supposed certainties about world and spirit, perhaps with a reassuring sidelong glance at Goethe’s: “Only despise reason and science — Man’s very highest power,” or whether one distills from it an “absolute spirit,” an “absolute will,” and now conceives so-called world history as its development toward the “consciousness of freedom.” Rather, the second mode of interpretation is merely the absolutizing of the first, and the Hegelian doctrine that “reason rules the world” is precisely the most extreme and most pernicious form of the denial of that multiplicity onto which the outlook of Nietzsche opens for us; for according to it all diversity of estates, peoples, races, cultures, ages would indeed appear as a mere difference of stage in the realization of one and the same monon, namely the spirit; whereby we would be bound to interpret them even consciously and systematically in the sense of more or less successful preliminary exercises for our, the Christian, present. Hegel and Nietzsche stand opposed to one another insofar as the most violent and, if one will, the most powerful attempt at the extreme unification of what happens stands opposed to the attempt at a multiplication that finally points out into the indeterminate. — Hegel was certainly a significant mind and surely no lesser than, say, Schopenhauer; none the less he attended — predominantly consciously and from conviction — philosophically to the business of the Restoration and of Christianity, and his present-day progeny, should there be any, would — only

p. 150

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

probably less consciously — attend philosophically to the business of — high finance.

Besides this, to be sure, Nietzsche’s object of interpretation is incomparably more concrete. True, he too kept overwhelmingly to the written remains of peoples and ages, and besides these, for the last three centuries, to their musical remains, while his power of apprehension and his understanding for the products of the visual arts were unpenetrating and meager; but who before him had read the records with such delicacy of hearing for choice of words and shades of meaning, for intonation, measure, and rhythm, and had known how to draw from them in such measure what they hold of the “spirit of the times,” not merely by virtue of their conceptual content but beyond that through their expressive content! And if others before him were already able to do so, they were poets (such as Shakespeare, Schiller, Kleist, further Stendhal, Balzac, but also Tieck, Mörike, Stifter, etc.), who with the help of their gift of untaught necromancy conjured up figures of the past, whereas the great philologists and writers of world history, and even the historians of culture (in the manner of a Freytag or Scherr), including the philosopher of history Hegel, extraordinary as their achievements are, seem to us far more to construct out of the views of their own time than to have been able, unconcerned with these, to mirror the alien times, from which we cannot entirely except even Burckhardt, revered by Nietzsche as his model31). With his ability, despite an always passionate partisanship, indeed out of it, to characterize the life-essence and style of whole epochs with a few turns of phrase in such a way that they stand before us like pictures, Nietzsche represents a “class of his own,” into which other names cannot be ranked without difficulty.

But not only peoples, ages, cultures, but also, within each culture, the estates stand out against one another in the magic mirror of Nietzschean pluralism with a colorfulness never seen before; and if we were inclined, to be sure, to regard his type of the master man rather as a work of wishing, which nevertheless provided a serviceable pattern for the assessment of oligarchic associations, his priestly type, by contrast, is a discovery, provided only that one thinks in this connection of the religion-founding geniuses and above all of the shapers and active promoters of Christianity. Here there is so little lack of individual examples

p. 151

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that more or less any one of them harbors, besides its particular traits, the traits of the type that Nietzsche has shelled out; hence one could adduce a great series of cases, among them, besides priests by profession, such as Church Fathers and popes, also a variety of mighty statesmen and politicians, and indeed almost exclusively of Christendom. If we name, say: Paul, Tertullian, Constantine (!), Augustine (!), Charles (!), Philip II, Torquemada, Gregory, Innocent, Loyola, Cromwell (!), Robespierre (!), there would hardly be even one among them to whom Nietzsche’s words, sufficient in themselves, would not apply: “Look at the faces of the great Christians! They are the faces of great haters” (M).

But it now lay in the nature of the matter that Nietzsche, out of so many available haters, had above all to single out the one whom we mentioned in first place: the Jew Saul, who in consequence of an epileptic fit with visionary accompanying phenomena on the famous road to Damascus thenceforth called himself Paul32). Overbeck33) probably knew it earliest and at least wrote down for himself what every critical theologian can know today, that it was Nietzsche who, if not first, then more thoroughly and penetratingly than his predecessors, established that historical Christianity, with all its churches and sects, is a creation of Paul. This, however, is to be understood as follows. If, in accordance with the biblical saying “By their fruits ye shall know them,” we assess the essence of Christianity by the deeds of Christendom, that is, by the destruction of the heathen temples and unforgettable images of the gods, indeed of the whole culture of antiquity, by the witch-burnings and autos-da-fé, by the extermination of the Moors and their no less unforgettable culture in Spain, by the gruesome extirpation of the gold-guarding Incas, by the atrocities of the slaughters of the Albigensians, the Stedingers, the Templars, etc., the great plundering expeditions of the so-called Crusades, the nights of St. Bartholomew, the Thirty Years’ War, the enslavement of all “primitive peoples” (the Negro trade!) and — to land at once, passing over more recent events, at the end effect visible today — by the capitalization of the entire surface of the planet (up to the peaks of the Himalayas and down to the abysses of the deep sea), then we must admit that a bloodier religion has never existed on earth, and will agree with our philosopher when

p. 152

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

with regard to Dante’s inscription above the gate of hell, he says: “Over the gate of the Christian paradise and its ‘eternal bliss’ the inscription ‘I too was created by eternal hatred’ might in any case stand with better right” (ZG)34). The thorough study of Nietzsche’s analysis of the motives of Paulinism, however, now teaches us that with this it is not the fragmentarily transmitted sayings of that enigmatic and for the most part legendary Jesus of Nazareth, but indeed in fact the doctrines that are in accord which we possess documentarily in the apostolic epistles of Paul. With this, however, the psychology of Christianity would have transformed itself into a psychology of Judaism, and that is what it comes to with Nietzsche in all earnest. Here, however, an interjection is required.

Nietzsche was so little an “anti-Semite” that there is hardly any sort of human being he despised more thoroughly than — “anti-Semites”! Whoever took the trouble, as we have, to extract from Nietzsche all the passages that refer to the Jews, to “anti-Semites,” and to the — Germans, would infallibly arrive with us at the following memorable result: he admired the Jews in the highest degree, despised “anti-Semites,” and hated the Germans, and indeed with that blind hatred which, in order to justify itself, does not shrink from the most monstrous distortions of the facts! Had he lived to see the “World War,” he would have been one of the most furious partisans of the Entente! (Why he nonetheless volunteered in 1870 we believe we can fully explain; but that would go beyond the framework of this work.) Since the compilation of the sayings in question would fill a volume, we content ourselves with pointing, by way of a note, to a few particularly characteristic ones, and only those about the Jews35), and answer the question that must urgently be raised in view of this, in what sense the word “Jew” is actually used by Nietzsche, with the explanation: the Jews are to him the people of the mightiest and most successful priestliness hitherto. Of his innermost attitude toward the priest we shall speak later; here we remove for the time being the appearance of a strange ambivalence in his manner of valuation as follows: if he directs his gaze to the depth, strength, persistence, unconditionality, and inexorability of the priestly will to power, to its incomparable wisdom, cleverness, and cunning in the choice of means, to the genius of its arts of adaptation and skills of reinterpretation

p. 153

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

, then he becomes the admirer of the priest and consequently of the Jew as the most perfect form of representation of the priestliness meant here; if, on the other hand, he directs his gaze to the fact that the priestly will is a will to power, directed against life, of envy of life and of a life diseased to the marrow, poisoned, ill-constituted, then he becomes the passionate combatant of the priest and thus once again of the Jew as the most perfect form of representation of this kind of morbidity. We naturally regard what is admired and what is fought against as two inseparably connected sides of one and the same thing, and may then, to be sure, call it neither simply the priestly nor even the Jewish, but may with no less right borrow for its designation the name of a god, as Nietzsche did, as we shall yet hear, for its counterpart with the designation of the Dionysian, and we accordingly call the foundation of disposition meant and unveiled by Nietzsche Yahwism. It is certain at any rate that it was in fact demonstrated by him: historical Christianity is Paulinism, and Paulinism is a special form of Yahwism.

In an earlier note we reproduced an effusive eulogy that Nietzsche paid to the Romans. It stands in ZG at that passage where he grippingly sets forth that European history is to be conceived as the struggle between Rome and Judah (read: Yahwism), and it is found immediately before the following sentences, which allow one to recognize, to one’s astonishment, how even into his hatred the admiration mentioned mingles: “The Jews, conversely, were that priestly people of ressentiment par excellence, in whom there dwelt a popular-moral genius without equal: one need only compare the peoples of related gifts, say the Chinese or the Germans, with the Jews, in order to feel what is of the first and what of the fifth rank. Which of them has won for the time being, Rome or Judaea? But there is no doubt at all: just consider before whom one bows down today in Rome itself as before the epitome of all the highest values . ., before three Jews, as is well known, and one Jewess (before Jesus of Nazareth, the fisherman Peter, the tentmaker Paul, and the mother of the aforementioned Jesus, named Mary)” (ZG).

We have already given it to be understood that we consider Nietzsche’s psychology of Christianity to be simply correct; and we would

p. 154

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

therefore not wish to conceal, before we bring forward some supporting passages, that his discovery, if one wishes to call it that with us, has a not inconsiderable prehistory, from which at least three facts deserve to be cited, because they are perhaps apt to rid many a reader of the prejudice that Nietzsche’s picture of Christianity is the isolated caricature of an eccentric, and might thus contribute to one’s not letting oneself be deterred by his passionate tone from examining it with caution and deliberation.

If we disregard the present — we mean the “World War,” of which we were witnesses, and its still far more horrifying consequence, namely the extermination of almost the whole Russian people — there has probably been no more hideous event in the history handed down to us than the French Revolution, which among other things made trousers out of the skins of the executed and wigs out of the hair of beheaded blondes, and of which its most thorough connoisseur, Carlyle, judges: “Better than all Frenchmen thrives now the native stock-jobber.”36) Now it belongs among the memorable things that one ought ever anew to snatch from oblivion that, when at last Danton too stands with his group of followers before the dreadful Fouquier-Tinville, his most faithful adherent, Camille, answers the question as to his age as follows: “My age is that of the bon sansculotte Jésus, an age fatal to revolutionaries!”37) In agreement with this, already in the first half of the last century the French writer Roux, in his Histoire parlamentaire, celebrated the French Revolution as “a struggle of life and death” “to realize at last the Christian religion after 1800 years of preparation!”38)

The second fact is that in 1847 the Romantic Daumer published, under the cover title Die Geheimnisse des christlichen Altertums, a book in which he seeks to prove, partly from reports and traditions, partly through interpretation of certain symbols and customs, that Christian antiquity was essentially the secret cult of a fearful Molochism with cultic anthropophagy continued for centuries. For the meritoriousness of his revelations he appeals to a passage from Bayle, which today too is again worth reading39), and offers, all in all, the literal underpinnings for Nietzsche’s: “Christianity is a metaphysics of the hangman” (G).

p. 155

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

The book — of unmistakably paranoid character and therefore to be recommended only to literary gourmets — brings to the secret history of Christianity an abundance of the rarest material, some of which fits all too exactly the insights attainable today by the science of the soul for one to be able to dismiss it summarily as “superstition.”

A third, quite different fact of significance we see in the utterances which Marx, the founder of socialism, made about Christianity in 1844 in the Deutsch-französische Jahrbücher von Ruge und Marx (pp. 209 to 247). Here are some sentences from them: “Judaism reaches its high point with the completion of civil society; but civil society completes itself only in the Christian world. Only under the dominion of Christianity, which makes all national, natural, moral, theoretical relations external to man, could civil society separate itself completely from the life of the state, sever all the species-bonds of man, put egoism . . in the place of these species-bonds, dissolve the human world into a world of atomistic individuals standing in hostility over against one another. Christianity sprang from Judaism. It has dissolved itself back into Judaism. The Christian was from the outset the theorizing Jew, the Jew is therefore the practical Christian, and the practical Christian has become a Jew again. Christianity has overcome real Judaism only in appearance . . Christianity is the sublime thought of Judaism, Judaism is the common practical application of Christianity, but this practical application could become a general one only after Christianity had theoretically completed man’s self-estrangement from himself and from nature. Only then could Judaism . . make alienated man, alienated nature into alienable, saleable objects fallen prey to the servitude of egoistic need . . Christian egoism of bliss necessarily turns, in its completed practice, into the bodily egoism of the Jew, heavenly need into earthly need, subjectivism into self-interest.”40) After this we bring forward evidence from Nietzsche.

“The Jews — a people ‘born for slavery,’ as Tacitus and the whole ancient world say, ‘the chosen people among the peoples,’ as they themselves say and believe — the Jews have brought about that miracle

p. 156

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of an inversion of values thanks to which life on earth has received a new and dangerous charm for a few millennia: their prophets have fused ‘rich,’ ‘godless,’ ‘violent,’ ‘sensual’ into one and for the first time coined the word ‘world’ as a word of shame . . (With this begins) the slave revolt in morality” (J). “It was the Jews who, against the aristocratic equation of values (good = noble = powerful = beautiful = happy = beloved of God), dared the inversion with a fear-inspiring consistency and held it fast with the teeth of the most abysmal hatred (the hatred of impotence), namely: ‘the wretched alone are the good, the poor, impotent, lowly alone are the good, the suffering, deprived, sick, ugly are also the only pious, the only blessed in God, for them alone is there blessedness, — whereas you, you noble and mighty ones, you are for all eternity the evil, the cruel, the lustful, the insatiable, the godless, you will also eternally be the unblessed, accursed, and damned!’ One knows who has inherited this Jewish revaluation” (ZG). “The Christian, this ultima ratio of the lie, is the Jew once more — three times even . . . ‘Judge not!’ they say, but they send to hell everything that stands in their way. By letting God judge, they judge themselves; by glorifying God, they glorify themselves; by demanding the virtues of which they happen to be capable . ., they give themselves the grand appearance of a struggle for virtue . . By pushing their way through in the manner of sneaks, sitting in the corner . ., they make a duty of it: out of duty their life appears as humility, as humility it is one more proof of piety . . . Morality is commandeered by these little people; they know what there is to morality! . . The reality is that here the most conscious conceit of the chosen plays at modesty: one has placed oneself, the ‘community,’ the ‘good and just,’ once and for all on the one side, on that of ‘the truth’; and the rest, the ‘world,’ on the other. That was the most fateful kind of megalomania that has hitherto existed on earth: little abortions of bigots and liars began to claim for themselves the concepts ‘God’ ‘truth’ ‘light’ ‘spirit’ ‘love’ ‘wisdom’ ‘life’ . ., in order thereby to fence off the ‘world’ against themselves, little superlative Jews, ripe for every kind of madhouse, turned values as such around toward themselves, as though

p. 157

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

only the Christian were the meaning . . also the last judgment of all the rest.” “The anarchist and the Christian are of one descent . . . Their aim, their instinct is directed only at destruction.” “Christianity was the vampire of the imperium Romanum” (A). “Christianity gave Eros poison to drink: he did not die of it, to be sure, but degenerated into vice” (J). “But this is what happened: from the trunk of that tree of revenge and hatred, Jewish hatred . . there grew something just as incomparable, a new love, the deepest and most sublime of all kinds of love . . But let no one suppose that it grew up as, say, the true negation of that thirst for revenge, as the opposite of Jewish hatred! No, the reverse is the truth! This love grew out of it as its crown . . , which, with the same urge, as it were in the realm of light and of the heights, was out for the goals of that hatred, for victory, for spoils, for seduction, with which the roots of that hatred sank ever more thoroughly and covetously down into everything that had depth and was evil. This Jesus of Nazareth . . . was he not precisely seduction in its most uncanny and irresistible form, the seduction and the detour to precisely those Jewish values and innovations of the ideal? Has Israel not, precisely by the detour of this ‘redeemer,’ this apparent adversary and dissolver of Israel, attained the last goal of its sublime vindictiveness? Does it not belong to the secret black art of a truly great politics of revenge . . , that Israel itself had to deny before all the world, as something mortally hostile, the true instrument of its revenge, and nail it to the cross, so that ‘all the world,’ namely all the opponents of Israel, could unhesitatingly bite at precisely this bait?” (ZG).

PDF 162

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

CONSCIOUSNESS AND LIFE

Had Nietzsche presented his researches in the form of a system, his doctrine of envy of life would have furnished the roof-ridge, and that of the relation of consciousness to life the foundation. In contrast to the prejudice — now, incidentally, on the wane — that he examined exclusively ethical values, it cannot be repeated often enough that, while with the knife-edge of his critique he always pursues the moralistic counterfeitings, he follows them with special predilection into the domains of the intellect and of supposedly “pure” rationality, and has thus made contributions to what has hitherto been called epistemology hardly less weighty than to the testing of moral values. Precisely this is his distinguishing peculiarity and the novelty of his schooling in suspicion, that he seeks and finds the stage-management of wishes and interests in regions where before him one had least suspected them, and teaches us to recognize in the faculty of judgment, to a large extent, an apparatus that is invented, built, and set in operation for purposes essentially other than those of knowledge — by the will. “The whole apparatus of knowledge is . . directed not at knowledge but at the taking possession of things.” “Science: transformation of nature into concepts for the purpose of mastering nature.” “In summa, science is preparing a sovereign ignorance” (WzM).

In a broadly comprehensive treatment of the subject we should have to give here an outline of the history of the concept of the unconscious, to show that Nietzsche, with his own concept, links up not with Leibniz but with Romanticism, to investigate the relations to Schopenhauer’s “blind will,” to which the intellect, after all, likewise stands somewhat as a lantern with which the will lights its way ahead. With such thoroughness we should never come to an end here, and we therefore refer to our appraisal of the late Romantic Carus

p. 159

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

(as an introduction to his principal psychological work Psyche, republished by the Eugen Diederichs Verlag), where the questions mentioned are touched upon and at least partly discussed. By contrast, it shall now be our task to assemble everything that Nietzsche set down by way of valid knowledge concerning consciousness, and at the edge of the tremendous brightness which that knowledge spreads to make visible the darkness polarly belonging to it, and with it the innermost meaning of his doctrine of life. Material for this is offered by all his works, but by none more abundantly than WzM. By making our selection of supporting passages in such a way that all of them agree without friction, we cut out with a deliberate incision what Nietzsche owes, by way of lasting findings for the science of consciousness, to the life-affirming side of his nature, and in the next chapter we shall all the more easily satisfy ourselves that his opinions contradicting these bear witness to that same life-denying trait of his nature by which he was misled into equating life with the will.

It is not theoretical considerations about the relation of consciousness to a so-called unconscious, such as had occupied a Leibniz and still occupy the scholars of the schools in quite similar fashion today, but rather the question of the value of consciousness in relation to life, with which, if we are not mistaken, the extraordinary turn of Western thought decisively begins for the first time in Goethe — the turn according to which the cause of essential imperfections of existence would have to be sought in consciousness, until then praised above all things.

All our most honest striving

Succeeds only in unconscious moments;

How then could the rose bloom

If it knew the glory of the sun!

To these verses of Goethe’s let us add the following from the great number of his prose sayings pointing in the same direction: “For in all pleasant and good states the soul loses consciousness of itself . . and is reminded of itself again only by unpleasant sensations” — “The senses do not deceive, but judgment deceives” — “Man cannot long . . remain in the conscious state; he must plunge back into the unconscious; for therein lives his root” — and one is in a

p. 160

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

position to continue with utterances of Nietzsche’s so similar that the sameness of the motives of the two suffers no doubt. “We deny that anything can be made perfect so long as it is still made consciously” (A). “To all action belongs forgetting: just as to the life of everything organic there belongs not only light but also darkness.” “Is life now to rule over knowing . . , or is knowing to rule over life? Which of the two powers is the higher and decisive one? No one will doubt: life is the higher, the ruling power; for a knowing that annihilated life would have annihilated itself along with it.” The bearer of the higher rationality has annihilated and lost his instinct; he can now no longer, trusting in the ‘divine animal,’ let the reins hang loose when his understanding wavers and his way leads through deserts” (U). Further: “‘The senses’ do not lie at all. What we make of their testimony, that is what first puts the lie into it” (G). Finally: “The strength of pieces of knowledge lies not in their degree of truth but in their age” (F). “The most reliable knowledge or belief cannot give the strength for the deed nor the skill for the deed . . First and foremost, the works! That means practice, practice, practice! The ‘faith’ that goes with it will come of itself” (M).

In consequence of the same motive, the same conviction in both: what has grown is more perfect than what is made; what is lived through or accomplished without consciousness is more perfect than what is lived through or accomplished with consciousness; even what requires consciousness for its coming into being has its relative perfection not from consciousness but from the fullness of vitality behind it; this fullness can exist without consciousness as the root can without the blossom, but consciousness cannot exist without unconscious life, as the blossom cannot without the root; the cogito sum demands a vivo cogito, and — in Goethe first sounding faintly, in Nietzsche the leitmotif — the cogitare is, in relation to the vivere, to be regarded not as a blossom at all but rather as a disturbance of growth, which must first be incorporated, assimilated, and transformed into a vital function before it is able to serve the organism. We have thereby circumscribed somewhat more precisely than in Part One the theme of the genuine vitalism of the modern age, which leads in a straight line from Goethe through Romanticism to Nietzsche, in order to “make an epoch” in and with him. We have meanwhile informed ourselves to some extent about the meaning of the vitalistic standard and will shortly do so

p. 161

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

more precisely; here we first develop the most important consequences for the science of consciousness, presupposing, of course, everything already offered on the subject in Part One, and we recommend arming oneself against any fits of the spirit of doubt in the following way.

If the intellect (= immediate substrate of consciousness) is, according to Schopenhauer, a μηχανή of the will, and according to Nietzsche an organ first of the living body and secondarily, once again, of the will, insofar as a will to power is supposed to manifest itself in the body as well, then it would remain at least doubtful whether it could produce knowledge at all, while it would be beyond doubt that it had either to fulfill certain tasks in the growth of the body or to serve the expansion of the will’s power; in consequence of which, however, this insight too would then be burdened with the questionableness of whether it is a genuine piece of knowledge or rather, once again, merely a belief which the will could not renounce without renouncing along with it its existence in life. In the next chapter we shall appreciate how much Nietzsche tormented himself over this, but here we help ourselves with the following consideration. Consciousness is in any case reflection upon something. This may refer ever so often to fictions, that is, to objects which were involuntarily invented on the occasion of wishes of the will: it can at the very least refer to the reality of itself, and can then also become aware of the wishes of the will by which it was misled into those fictions.

Now, however, we know that these fictions one and all hang on one and only one hook, namely on the reality of the ego, and accordingly rest one and all on the one and only delusion that all other reality consists of egos acting upon one another in the manner of wills. We have further convinced ourselves that “other reality” cannot possibly consist of such egos, and we add here to what was adduced at that time: if it actually did consist of them, there would be no consciousness of appearance at all, and indeed not even a consciousness of spatiality, because the ego is a spatially unextended point, and still less a consciousness of temporality, because the ego can be thought only as a supratemporal (even if time-related) state of affairs. The “things” — or whatever “in-itself” we might otherwise foist upon the spatiotemporal flux of appearance — cannot appear at all and are, in relation to the “given” images of the world, just as much merely reference points necessarily Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 11

p. 162

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

carried into them for purposes of orientation, as our ego is the really existing point of support of relating thought41). If we now take reality, in distinction from the ego-point that dwells solely in us, as a “world of appearances,” then there is not and cannot be in reality: unity, number, thing, duration, substance, being, cause-effect, sameness, repetition; and so all judgments that would assert the reality of even one of the objects of thought meant by the concepts enumerated would be inventions, more correctly falsifications of reality, and by no means pieces of knowledge.

Should someone object here that then, surely, the principle of identity would be abolished and with it thinking itself, we should for the present have to remind him: the principle of identity holds rightly with respect to the ego that is capable of consciousness; but nothing compels us, because we can grasp something only with its help, be it the spirit in us or be it the reality of appearance, to engrave our standard into extra-spiritual reality. Rather, this oldest and most tenacious fiction is just as superfluous and just as foolish as if we were to assume that, because we can state the length of the room in centimeters, the distance between the walls of the room consists of centimeters and consequently cosmic space itself of cubic centimeters, and that, because we can state the duration of a process in seconds, the duration and therefore cosmic time itself consists of such! As surely as it is not space but our spirit that divides space into cubic centimeters, and as surely as it is not time but our spirit that divides time into seconds, so surely is there in reality not the spatial interval called a “centimeter” that is identical here and now with the centimeter of there and then, and not the temporal interval called a “second” that is identical here and now with the second of there and then; and as surely, moreover, as the splitting of space into billion-billionths of a cubic centimeter always yields only spatiality again and never a unit different from space out of which space could be constructed, and as surely as the splitting of time into billion-billionths of a second always yields only temporality again and never a unit different from time out of which time could be constructed, so surely does our measuring and nothing-but-measuring thought, does reflection as such, presuppose the world of appearance as something “given,” for whatever reasons, and

p. 163

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

as something eternally unattainable for the comprehending faculty of judgment!

The reverse side of Heracliteanism in the science of consciousness, thus circumscribed, may in meaning and in substance be designated the chief fruit of Nietzsche’s epistemological researches. Secondary findings, which explain themselves, are his disclosures connected with it concerning the nature of the object of thought and the mostly unexamined conditions of the applicability of logical thinking. “We operate with nothing but things that do not exist, with lines, surfaces, bodies, atoms, divisible times, divisible spaces . . , cause and effect . . In truth a continuum stands before us, of which we isolate a few pieces” (F). “We need ‘unities’ in order to be able to reckon: that is no reason to assume that such unities exist. We have borrowed the concept of unity from our concept of the ego . . If we did not take ourselves to be unities, we should never have formed the concept ‘thing’” (WzM). “Compulsion is not demonstrable in things at all: the rule proves only that one and the same occurrence is not also another occurrence. Only because we have read subjects, ‘doers,’ into things does the appearance arise that all occurrence is the consequence of a compulsion exerted upon subjects” (WzM). These two sentences comprise three, and with their exposition even five, fundamental insights: thing and so-called law of nature are at bottom the same — namely, the principle of sameness made objective — the principle of sameness follows from the nature of the ego — the ego is essentially a doer (= substrate of acts) — the “objects” are externalized “subjects,” and the occurrence taking place between them accordingly appears in the light of constraints effected by the will. “An occurrence is neither effected nor effecting. Causa is a capacity to effect, invented and added to the occurrence.” “The properties of a thing are effects on other things” (WzM). “When Kant says, ‘the understanding does not draw its laws from nature but prescribes them to it,’ this is completely true with regard to the concept of nature which we are compelled to connect with it . . , but which is the summation of a multitude of errors of the understanding” (MA). Incidental remark: what Kant calls “appearances” are without exception objects of thought (= things produced by the spirit through working over the appearances

11*

p. 164

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

as unities); of the space of appearance and the time of appearance, on the other hand, not a syllable occurs in the whole “Transcendental Aesthetic”! “Logic is tied to the condition: supposing there are identical cases.” “The law of contradiction . . thus contains no criterion of truth, but an imperative concerning what is to count as true . . . In fact logic holds . . only of fictitious entities which we have created. Logic is the attempt to comprehend the real world according to a schema of being posited by us” (WzM). That is literally correct, provided only that we add in thought that “the world” to which we apply the schema of being befalls us in some form independently of the measuring activity of our ego, or, as the technical term has it, is “given.” “We can comprehend only a world that we ourselves have made” (WzM). This one unveiling of the meaning of comprehending activity weighs more heavily than mountains of epistemologies, but in genius it is almost surpassed by the sentence of the youthful Novalis: “We know something only insofar as we can express it, i.e., make it.”

We do not investigate here what cognitive value and above all what use value belongs to the opposed concepts, of which at least two — continuum and occurrence — are mentioned in the supporting passages above; enough that the propositions which deny the substrate concepts their value as reality are illuminating, irrefutable, and of a definiteness free of doubt. But if we ask whence a thinker who, as we shall learn in the next chapter, had moreover fallen prey, in the science of consciousness, to an extreme spirit of doubt, should have such certainties, then pointing to the assumed dependence of his spirit on the body would still leave open the further question why it is precisely the most general substrate concepts against which so weighty an attack is opened. The right of the drives against custom, statute, and rule was, after all, repeatedly proclaimed, and even if general interests were mostly confused with it (“Sire, give freedom of thought!”), still in the “Sturm und Drang” of the eighteenth century, for example, the passions at least spoke up audibly (“Of the right that is born with us — of that, alas, there is never any question!”); but far from arriving thereby at a Heracliteanism of disposition, one ended rather in a measured cult of beauty, in Apollinism, indeed Platonism, and so roughly on the opposite side. Not in the

p. 165

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

drives as such, and still much less in the passions, which are always already infused with spirit, may we therefore hope to find that state of life whose surgings carry Nietzsche’s vitalism with its point turned so decidedly against the value of consciousness. Rather it is their psychic pole in the distinguishing sense, from which the bodily nature that here acts as prompter to the spirit receives its nimbus and a gift of persuasion in comparison with which even the dialectic of the passions seems meager and poor to anyone who has been graced to hear its voice even once, and who now, upon sufficiently sharp reflection, unfailingly names the fructifying lived experience along with it: exuberance, intoxication, rapture. (Words such as inspiration, enthusiasm, ecstasy, bliss, rapturous fervor are today already too pale for it.)

Since the “Dionysian” has, since The Birth of Tragedy, become a painful fashionable catchword (and recently even the “cosmic,” which we ourselves favor), with which all manner of hysterias garland themselves, it must first be said again what Nietzsche meant by it and what he accomplished with it, and finally what could have induced him to christen his view with the name of precisely this god of the Greeks. A few words about the provenances must precede this. Since antiquity, more or less every metaphysical thought of polarity, but especially the philosophy of polarity of Romanticism, has set the following series, belonging together in each case (and, incidentally, extendable at will), over against one another: left, below, night, darkness, rest, earth (telluric, terrestrial, chthonic), passivity, south, woman — — right, above, day, brightness, motion, sun (solar, uranian, sidereal), activity, north, man. Since already some natural philosophers of the Renaissance, but especially, once again, the Romantics, erroneously mixed with this the entirely different opposition of life and spirit, or body and spirit, or even soul and spirit, they added on the left and on the right the following further members: Dionysus, the daemonic, the ganglionic plexus, “feeling,” instinct — — Apollo, the divine, the cerebral system, reason, sober deliberation. In this sense, for example, Fr. Schlegel distinguishes “the quiet deliberation of Apollo” from the “divine intoxication of Dionysus.” Since Apollo is in fact a hybrid formation, made up of the age-old hallowing of the light- and day-pole of the world and an intention toward spiritualization originally quite foreign to the cult of light, the Dionysian cannot be understood at all from his standpoint,

p. 166

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and so it is most instructive that Nietzsche, who was able to see one side of it more sharply than anyone before him, in turn carries traits of the Dionysian into his Apollinian, but precisely thereby finds himself compelled to oppose to both together the spiritual, in the symbolically conceived person of Socrates. We cannot pursue this further here and confine ourselves to the remark that everything Nietzsche really meant by his “Dionysian” springs by no means from its supposed opposition to a so-called Apollinian, but from its deeply thought-through opposition to everything spiritual (and especially to the precipitates of consciousness).

The use of the Dionysian for — to put it briefly — the dark hemisphere of life was thus by no means new. But numerous traits can be distinguished in it, which in the end we could once again arrange in two series polar to each other, one of which would correspond to the symbol of the turning circle, the other to the center of the circle. Common to both is the condition which we would describe equally well with the following words: pregnancy, the necessity of giving birth, dependence on fate (necessity, Ananke, Heimarmene). Their difference, however, we characterize most briefly as the difference between the clinging readiness for conception and the roving rut of procreation. Although Romanticism lived out and poetized both sides, the profundity of its research nevertheless applied quite predominantly to the former. It was on the way to the center, even if not it, but only a later genius, was able to reach it42). Nietzsche, on the other hand, was always on the way to ever more distant, ever more widely stretched peripheries. Over Romantic vitalism the sentence of Novalis would fittingly stand: “Inward goes the mysterious way”; and hardly, even with the greatest thoroughness, could we portray the commanding state more aptly than Görres does in a few words when making known his conception of primordial man: “In the speech he utters, the muffled language of the elements sounds on; the peculiar accent of each can be distinguished in it. Necessarily, therefore, this history must be the transition of physics into life* . . Man in this period is somnambulic; as in magnetic sleep he walks, unconscious of his consciousness, in the deeper consciousness of the world; his thinking is dreaming in the deeper nerve-tracts; but these dreams are true*.” From here one understands without further ado

p. 167

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the predilection of Romanticism for the “night-polar” manifestations of life, for presentiments, clairvoyance, “magnetic rapports,” “mesmerism,” sympathetic cures, the sensing of metals, dowsing, etc., nothing but phenomena to which Nietzsche paid little attention and devoted predominantly disparaging remarks, as well as its inclination toward prehistory, toward symbolism, toward myth. If we were to compare this state of creative absorption, of brooding vital moisture, and of the deeply passive being-carried-away by the world-essences preparing themselves, to the silent massing of the thundercloud, then the polar opposite state would resemble the outbreak of the storm with thunder and lightning, of which, by way of exception, a passage from Eh may give us a notion, even if only one side of the matter, namely the inspirational one, finds some expression in it.

“Has anyone, at the end of the nineteenth century, a clear notion of what poets of strong ages called inspiration? If not, I will describe it. — With the slightest remnant of superstition in oneself one would . . hardly know how to fend off the idea of being merely incarnation . . , merely medium of overpowering forces. The concept of revelation, in the sense that suddenly, with unspeakable certainty and subtlety, something becomes visible, audible, something that shakes and overturns one to the depths, simply describes the facts. One hears, one does not seek; one takes, one does not ask who gives; like lightning* a thought flashes up, with necessity, in its form without hesitation . . A rapture whose tremendous tension sometimes discharges itself in a flood of tears, in which one’s step involuntarily now rushes ahead, now grows slow; a complete being-outside-oneself with the . . consciousness of a host of fine shudders and tremblings down to one’s toes; a depth of happiness in which the most painful and gloomiest do not act as an opposite but as conditioned . . , as a necessary color within such an overflow of light; an instinct for rhythmic relations that spans wide spaces of forms . . Everything happens in the highest degree involuntarily, but as in a storm of the feeling of freedom, of unconditionedness, of power, of divinity . .”

Let anyone hold to this testimony who does not command, from within himself, sufficient experiences of inner bursting to scent out to some degree, in every sentence of Nietzsche’s, but most especially in his contributions to the doctrine of life, the wellspring in a bodily nature that was, though held in check,

p. 168

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

orgiastic. What Nietzsche, as a discoverer, christened out of such experiences with the name of the god of the trieteric night festivals, thereby at the same time opening up, more deeply than had ever been done before him, the maenadic side of Greek, but more or less of all, antiquity, is the orgiastic trait of reality itself. Nothing of the kind had taken place until then43). Men had lived orgiasms, as nature itself does; had poured themselves out orgiastically to the sound of bronze cymbals and Phrygian flute melodies; had captured the streams of fire in the dithyrambic word; knew customs, preparations, symbols, all overgrown by a jungle thicket of myth; but never yet had anyone philosophized orgiastically. The concept of the ecstatic, of which there have been effects in thought at several periods, is much wider, whereas for self-reflection upon the orgiastic a few beginnings may be found at most among disciples of the Sufi order. Nietzsche was aware of this. “The very fact that Dionysus is a philosopher, and that gods therefore also philosophize, seems to me a novelty which is not without its dangers and which might perhaps arouse mistrust precisely in a philosopher” (J). And everything best, deepest, and truest that he had to offer by way of findings in the science of life are fragments of a philosophy of orgiasm; and whatever else he offers is worthless. Once one has become clear about this, one knows not only the motives of his critique of the substrate concepts and the ultimate meaning of his Heracliteanism, but one will also find, through the breaches he struck in the yard-thick cocoon of chimeras, the way to new truths, indeed to a new kind of thinking. But since Nietzsche himself was no longer able to set out upon the way, we too must content ourselves with pointing out the breaches; which, for brevity’s sake, may be done by enumerating point by point the certainties that fall to the lot of the orgiast’s feeling of life when it reflects upon itself.

I. The trait common to the two poles of the ecstatic feeling of life, that of roving orgiasm and that of clinging chthonism — the trait of passive necessity — would by itself alone suffice to shake to its foundations the belief in a world-fabric of intersecting causal chains, because that trait would not merely be underivable from it but would even be wholly incompatible with it; for whatever the enigmatic principle from the mysticism of the spirit called “causality” may

p. 169

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

mean, a principle whose sense no researcher has to this hour been able to state unambiguously, this much is certain: it presupposes the duality of that which exerts an effect and that which receives an effect, and conceives the process of effecting, by analogy with the intuitive content of contact (impact), as something befalling from without that which receives the effect. Even if the thought did not have to founder on self-contradictions, which several thinkers of rank — above all Lotze — have treated conclusively, the passive feeling of life would nevertheless contradict it with the concept drawn from that feeling of a necessity that would indeed be a “must,” but a “must” coming from within and, even in the case of the most extreme tragedy, never compelled, and that in this way requires the interconnection of everything distinguishable to be set against the atomization into centers acting externally upon one another.
