This thought, very simple in and of itself, which seems difficult only to us today, who have grown accustomed to the late artificiality of “causal thinking,” held unchallenged sway in mankind for many millennia, at least at the level of feeling, before it was slain, at least for the “educated,” by the philosophical school of the Stoics — those transgressors against fate and cunning advocates of the sovereignty of the calculating will — together with the pre-Christian belief in an absolutely real and calculable-in-advance causality. Nietzsche’s counter-explanation, and his tracing of its origin to the tactics of a fettered will, therefore at the same time signifies an important contribution to the understanding of the oldest forms of thought and of life.

“They imagine every necessity as distress, as a painful having-to-follow and being-compelled; and thinking itself counts for them as something slow, hesitant . . and often enough as ‘worth the sweat of the noble’ . . Artists may have a finer scent here: they who know only too well that precisely when they no longer do anything ‘arbitrarily’ and do everything of necessity, their feeling of freedom, subtlety, full power . . reaches its height” (J). For: “We have no other relation to all essential accomplishment than that of pregnancy” (M). And: “Pleasure and innocence . . do not want to be sought. One should have them” (Z). — And with what a light hand he now sketches the consequences for the science of essence and for the world-view; as if breathed on airily and surely with Chinese ink, he sets them before us, and — here a sigh may for once be permitted — how would his

p. 170

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

whole work stand there unassailable and cast in one mold, had not his shameful “will to power” got in his way!

“‘I do not know at all what I am doing! I do not know at all what I ought to do.’ You are right; but do not doubt it: you are being done, in every moment! Mankind has at all times confused the active and the passive; it is its eternal grammatical blunder” (M). That is fatalism, certainly; but man and fatum do not stand over against each other as two, as in Turkish fatalism; rather, “in truth every man is a piece of fatum . . A man’s follies are just as much a piece of fatum as his prudences; even that fear of belief in fatum is fatum. You yourself, poor fearful one, are the invincible Moira, enthroned even above the gods, for everything that comes” (W).

Should one reader or another have thought above, when we once found occasion to speak of Taoism so that it might make us more sensitive to Nietzsche’s cloudings and mixed colors, that two formations so different lacked all points of similarity, he will no longer think so, but will perhaps have to admit with surprise that between the sayings of Nietzsche just heard and many of a Lieh-tzu or Chuang-tzu hardly any points of difference could still be named. But words of the same kind run through Nietzsche’s entire work. “Over all things stands the heaven Chance” (Z). Since “chance” here means as much as unwilledness and incalculability, the following words do not contradict the sentence but complement it: “Let us beware of saying that there are laws in nature. There are only necessities: there is no one who commands, no one who obeys, no one who transgresses. Once you know that there are no purposes, you also know that there is no chance: for only beside a world of purposes does the word ‘chance’ have a meaning” (F). This incidentally contains the whole psychology of chance, the name being understood according to its everyday use. Of the morning sky it is said in the hymnic rhapsody “Before Sunrise”: “Together we learned . . to smile down cloudlessly from bright eyes and from miles away, when beneath us constraint and purpose and guilt steam like rain” (Z). If we were to gather together everything spoken in the same key, the will to power’s claim to reality would be completely extinguished, and we should find between it and the wisdom of passive

p. 171

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

necessity the most unbridgeable gulf opened up; of which the following statement may give us a further preliminary notion: “Psychologically reckoned out, the concept ‘cause’ is our feeling of power from so-called willing; our concept ‘effect’ the superstition that this feeling of power is the power itself that moves” (WzM). If the equation life = will to power held good, that would be no superstition at all; if, conversely, our feeling of willed effecting is to be superstition, then that equation is false.

II. Again common to both poles is the basic feeling of unceasing transformation; but in the clinging chthonic pole predominantly in the form of the shudder at transience, in the roving orgiastic pole in the form of the intoxication of becoming, which incessantly bursts everything that has just come to be like a husk, in order incessantly to drive forth new forms out of what is incessantly decaying. “I tell you: one must still have chaos in oneself in order to be able to give birth to a dancing star” (Z). Connected with this is the attempt, undertaken by Nietzsche first and by him alone in the most varied places of his works, to derive all artistic and poetic creation from states of intoxication, namely as their expression, an attempt whose novelty and uniqueness usually escape us today only because at least the technical terms belong to the communicative equipment of all educated writers, however little the matter itself has undergone any continuation worth mentioning; and here, to be sure, lies also a vital root of Nietzsche’s glorification of cruelty, namely as a pleasure not in the increase of one’s own power, but in destroying something existing for the sake of creative renewal.

The more strongly we think of this side of roving orgiasm as emphasized, the more it takes on the form of heroic intoxication (beside which, as we have disclosed elsewhere, stand magical and erotic intoxication)44). In the reflection of the frenzy of self-renewal, the character of the questionable, the fearful, the fateful, the ruin-threatening, the contradictory, the “evil,” the indiscriminately crushing, the sheet-lightning-flashing, and, as it were, of an ecstatically wild readiness for death emerges overwhelmingly in reality, only to be used by Nietzsche, however, not for deepening our insight into the nature of the heroic, which rather everywhere comes off badly against his Caesarean model, but indeed for that illumination

p. 172

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of the nature of tragedy which gave his imperishable first work its title and is to be booked as his metaphysical discovery. On this we may spare ourselves further words, and only the one thing may not go unmentioned, that, notwithstanding his ever more harshly outlined false doctrine of the will to power, as “the last disciple and initiate of the god Dionysus” (J) he held fast to the very end, with the clearest consciousness of the value of his achievement, to his basic conception of the tragic as the voluptuous-painful bursting of individual being; and when even in the late G he writes the gripping sentences: “Before tragedy what is warlike in our soul celebrates its Saturnalia; whoever is accustomed to suffering, whoever seeks out suffering, the heroic man extols his existence with tragedy: to him alone does the tragedian proffer the draught of this sweetest cruelty,” no reader’s soul will be too dull not to hear in it that by “sweet cruelty” is meant here not a trait of the person, but the gorgonic feature in the character of reality and the affirmation of metaphysical horror.

But thus the life of the drives becomes deified in the aura of orgiastic transformation: “What did the Hellene guarantee himself with these mysteries? Eternal life, the eternal return of life; the future promised and consecrated in the past; the triumphant Yes to life beyond death and change; true life as the collective continuation of life through procreation, through the mysteries of sexuality. For the Greeks the sexual symbol was therefore the venerable symbol as such, the real profundity within the whole of ancient piety. Every single element in the act of procreation, of pregnancy, of birth awakened the highest and most solemn feelings. In the doctrine of the mysteries pain is pronounced holy: the ‘pangs of the woman in labor’ hallow pain as such . . All this the word Dionysus signifies: I know no higher symbolism than this Greek symbolism, that of the Dionysia” (G).

Whoever is able to feel his way into such things even only by intimation has the key to Nietzsche’s rejection of all substrate concepts, especially those of being and of the thing, as well as to his renewal of the Heraclitean panta rhei. For how could the thing that persists in time be compatible with eternal transformation, how could a being that outlasts time be compatible with restless creation! — If this is self-evident, it deserves, on the other hand, to be stated

p. 173

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

that here again a vital root is to be sought of Nietzsche’s strange abhorrence of the ego combined with his simultaneous belief in the reality of the will.

The concrete ego cannot well be replaced by a verb, whereas the in fact abstract will can very well be replaced by willing, and this therefore makes it easier for the demolisher of substrates to detach the state of affairs of willing from the willing subject. If we add that Nietzsche drew his concept of occurrence from the parturient self-transforming, which truly lacks all objectivity, and that the orgiastic transformation which alone he could live through is experienced as a bursting urge, then it might seem to us at least somewhat more plausible that he exchanged being driven for activity and could then, to be sure, feel tempted to hatch a doing without a doer! “For just as the people separates the lightning from its flash and takes the latter as a doing, as the effect of a subject called lightning, so popular morality also separates strength from the expressions of strength . . But there is no . . ‘being’ behind the doing, effecting, becoming; ‘the doer’ is merely invented and added to the doing” (ZG). Note in this extremely illuminating passage the equating of doing with becoming and the comparison of the deed with lightning! In truth the matter stands the other way round from what Nietzsche assumes: it is not the doer that has been invented and added to the doing; rather, the thought that a process is done could never have flashed up in the mind of a human being without an already existing knowledge of the doer, and in this knowledge, as we heard, the vital state of egohood bears witness to itself.

III. At least predominantly, it is only to roving exuberance that the shudder of pouring oneself out belongs. Everyone knows the unforgettable sentences of The Birth of Tragedy in which Nietzsche makes the Schiller- Beethoven “Ode to Joy” the basis of his own, itself Dionysian, portrayal of sympathetic overflowing. This magic sound too recurs in all his writings and is related to the brazen sound of his philosophy of the tragic as the fragrant blossom is to the nourishing sap. For in the reflection of the shudder of overflowing, reality’s character of measureless self-squandering emerges no less overwhelmingly, the character with which Nietzsche mocked the motif of self-preservation and the poorhouse doctrine of adaptation of Darwinism at a time when the cultural philistines of all countries were still

p. 174

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

counted among its following. We hardly need assure the reader, finally, that it is ingredients from the wonders of “bestowing love” by which he was able to clothe the naked exploiter’s will of his master-man with the semblance of vitality and even to lend it that captivating charm which, so to speak, cheats us out of noticing its contradictory, mixed character. “The one poor in life, the weak one, impoverishes life still further: the one rich in life, the strong one, enriches it. The first is its parasite: the second one who bestows in addition” (WzM)45).

“I love him whose soul squanders itself . . : for he always gives and does not want to keep himself” (Z). “Genius . . is necessarily a squanderer: that it spends itself is its greatness” (G). “Whoever cannot give receives nothing either” (WzM). All feelings, the dark as well as the bright, crowded together into one soul, into one feeling: “this would surely have to yield a happiness that man has not yet known, the happiness of a god full of power and love, full of tears and full of laughter, a happiness which, like the sun in the evening, continually gives away from its inexhaustible riches and pours into the sea and, like it, feels itself richest only when even the poorest fisherman is still rowing with a golden oar!” (F). And once more from the song of sunrise: “But I am one who blesses and one who says Yes . . into all abysses I still carry my blessing Yes-saying . . But this is my blessing: to stand over every thing as its own heaven, as . . its azure bell and eternal security” (Z).

These are the fruits in thought of Nietzsche’s orgiasm, and this is the blood of his spirit. Before such trumpet blasts of the exuberance of life the prison walls built of a thousand false doctrines of the millennia sink down around us; but from the world of images behind them only flickering gleams strike in, and the voice of the prophet, whenever he would show it to us too, breaks into a cry or loses itself in a bewildered murmur46). But the cause lay not in the inner stream’s not having been broad and deep enough, but in its having been shattered on the rock of Christianness. Whoever is still able in any way to hear the tone and content of the word, we could not convince of this better than by pointing to the last fragment (696) of WzM. We give a few sentences from it together with its horribly derailing conclusion.

“And do you also know what ‘the world’ is to me? Shall I show it to you in

p. 175

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

my mirror? The world: a monster of force, without beginning, without end . . . , a sea of forces storming and flooding within themselves, eternally changing, eternally running back, with tremendous years of recurrence, with an ebb and flood of its forms . . . : this my Dionysian world of eternal self-creating, of eternal self-destroying, this mystery-world of twofold voluptuous delights . . do you want a name for this world? A solution for all its riddles? A light for you too, you most hidden, . . most undaunted, most midnightly ones? — This world is the will to power — and nothing besides!”

After such an onset of exuberance conjured once more in the word, after so urgent an intensification of similes and images up to the Sphinx’s question about the essence of the world: the phrase heard a hundred times, already worn out, already grown lifeless: will to power!

PDF 181

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

C O N C LU D I N G C R I T I Q U E Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 12

PDF 183

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

NIETZSCHE’S

SOCRATISM

We have shown how Nietzsche, equipped with insights of a science of life that had grown out of his discovering reflection on the nature of his own bodily being and its orgiastic impulses, searched through the most highly valued traits of historical man in their whole breadth for their share in the egoistic will to success, which he was able to demonstrate in character traits seemingly quite opposed to it; how he unveiled a technique of deception peculiar to the layer of the mainsprings, by virtue of which the ego conceals its selfish stirrings from the consciousness of their bearer through a whitewashing reinterpretation; how he derived the sanctification of all “self-overcomings,” and thereby of all the ideals and moral statutes of Christendom, from the measures of retaliation taken by a will that feels itself excluded from the fullness of life; how, finally, he sought the condition of so terrible a distortion of the feeling of life in a contagious disease, without, to be sure, arriving at a conclusive diagnosis of it; and we have given sufficiently to understand that and why we consider all these findings to be correct in principle, and why it would be utterly hopeless henceforth to try to philosophize about the “essence of man” without knowledge of them and without practice in the method that made them possible. We have thereby at the same time shown in detail why we cannot accuse Nietzsche of boastfulness but must grant that he is right when he says of himself in Ecce Homo: “That out of my writings there speaks a psychologist who has not his equal, that is perhaps the first insight a good reader arrives at.” This, then, in our judgment, would be what is imperishable in his work and his contribution to the science of the soul, which casts all kindred wisdom into the shade.

But already in the first Part, and then in the second,

12*

p. 180

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we have drawn attention ever more audibly to the devastating self-contradiction that lies in the fact that the same thinker who, like no other, unveils the crimes of the will to power against life undertakes to understand life itself as precisely this will to power. We have already discussed a not inconsiderable number of the difficulties and insoluble aporias into which he thereby falls; now, however, we shall uncover them in their totality, at least in outline, and in doing so we cannot, to be sure, avoid contributing something to the question of the essence of our explorer of character, inasmuch as we are resolved to trace the fundamental contradictions of his thinking back to the conditions of their origin. For the time being we make use of the anticipatory formula: he owed all his discoveries to the pagan side of his character, all his errors to its Christian side. But since we begin from the periphery and advance step by step toward the center, it is in this chapter not so much his Christianity in the narrower sense as rather his share in the merely “preexistently Christian” Socratism that we intend to bring out unsparingly; whereby we hope incidentally to convince the reader that with the Socratic faith in reason, which continued in the Stoa and in modern “science,” the history of doubting truth began, far beyond anything in sophistry. — Finally, we lay the greatest weight on the following: as surely as the pagan and the Christian element belonged germ-plasmatically to Nietzsche’s essence, so surely do the false findings run alongside the sound ones through his entire work. Though now the one, now the other element may appear a little overemphasized, there is nevertheless no writing, no treatise of his, in which both could not be demonstrated with the greatest definiteness; and Nietzsche’s development, so highly significant for thought, by no means consists in his ever having contradicted an earlier writing with a later one, but consists in an ever-growing vehemence of his thinking, arising from the ever-growing unbearableness of his conflict of soul, and in the ever sharper working out of both powers, together with the ever sharper emphasis, going hand in hand with it, on their murderous strife. (Even the sole exception, Zarathustra, is only partly one, a point to which we shall return.) All contrary opinions rest either on superficiality or on ill will.

p. 181

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

If we put together everything that can be gathered from the foregoing about Nietzsche’s theory of the formation of judgments, and if we think above all of the dissolution of the underlying concepts, then the passionate rejection of Socrates which he set forth with such brilliant reasons, especially in GdT and G, is self-explanatory. How astonished must we therefore be when we see the same thinker, on the other hand, fall prey to Socratism and consequently to a bottomless skepticism, which here and there he is on the point of applying to his own results, even if in general, inconsistently, he refrains from doing so! The prelude to this certainly not insignificant verdict was formed, in the chapter “On the Persuasive Power of Success,” by our remark that Nietzsche, while mostly explaining error from a need of the will, often also explained the perversity of willing from errors. At that time we looked for reasons to justify this, but now we must insist that Nietzsche wavered undecided from beginning to end as to whether one errs because one must will the error, or rather wills what is perverse out of an error that has come to one from elsewhere! That the latter assumption would be Socratic needs no words. But let the following be noted at once: if I am of the opinion that every hidden intentionality threatens judgment with clouding and falsification, then I have already presupposed that there are truths, criteria of truth, and methods of finding truth, because otherwise there would not be the slightest occasion to suspect the interference of the will in the process of thought; if, on the other hand, I hold the view that false (pernicious, criminal, devilish, etc.) willing is the necessary consequence of errors, then I lack, for the present at least, any handle for explaining how an error arises, and doubt stands at the door whether perhaps the truths I indispensably need for the recognition of errors may not in turn be errors!

We shall perhaps reach our goal most quickly, and at any rate in the most entertaining way, if we give a sequence of Nietzschean utterances which, not without dramatic intensification, drive us into a complete trap of self-contradictions and make a subsequent series of unbeliefs appear somewhat in the light of desperation. We begin with some unmistakable manifestations of his Socratism, but we preface them with two sentences in which his anti-Socratism, hitherto especially familiar to us,

p. 182

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

is summed up. “Moral valuation is an interpretation . . The interpretation . . is a symptom of certain physiological states . . Who interprets? Our affects.” “Our holiest convictions, what is unchangeable in us with regard to supreme values, are judgments of our muscles” (WzM). Socratic manifestations. — “Through errors about its origin, its uniqueness, its destiny, and through demands that were made on the basis of these errors, mankind has . . again and again ‘surpassed itself’; but through the same errors unspeakably much suffering, mutual persecution, suspicion, misjudgment, and still more misery of the individual . . have come into the world” (WzM). “What is unjust and erratic in the temperament of many people, their disorder and immoderation, are the ultimate consequences of countless logical inaccuracies*, superficialities, and over-hasty conclusions of which their ancestors were guilty” (M). “But what led in primeval times to the assumption of an ‘other world’ at all was not a drive and need, but an error in the interpretation of certain natural processes, a perplexity of the intellect” (F). “The race is corrupted not by its vices but by its ignorance” (WzM). “The degeneration of life is essentially conditioned by the extraordinary capacity of consciousness for error” (WzM).

We have cited five utterances; we could have cited fifty; but it does not go any higher than this, and here Socrates is outdone. All injustice, all misery, all corruption stems from errors, and not merely from one’s own but also from those of one’s ancestors, unto the tenth, unto the hundredth generation; and what is being said here is not that, once opinions have arisen, these too henceforth take part in the formative processes of the living being, but that opinion becomes the principle, one might say the wire-puller, of all formative events! Let us at once consider two possible conclusions!

We heard earlier that all convictions are judgments of our affects and of our — muscles. Of so-called self-mastery it is said in M: “Rather, our intellect in this whole process is evidently only the blind instrument of another drive, which is a rival of the one that torments us by its vehemence;” and of knowing itself it is said in F that it is “a certain relationship of the drives to one another.” If I believe

p. 183

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

this with Nietzsche, and if I believe moreover, with Nietzsche, the Socrates, then I might raise, among other things, the curious question: might not the affect too, the drive, indeed the muscle to which it belongs, be a mere interpretation, and perhaps even a wrong one, an error?! Let us recall the treatise on “Knowing and Inventing” from F, discussed earlier, which closed with the question whether in the end everything lived through might not be merely something invented. We need not look for the answer ourselves. “Pain is an intellectual process, in which a judgment decidedly makes itself heard, the judgment ‘harmful,’ in which long experience has been summed up” (WzM). One must take this as literally as Nietzsche certainly meant it, and must not hesitate for a moment to regard, accordingly, violent toothache, for example, as an intellectual process; whereby, as no one will fail to recognize, one finds oneself in the immediate vicinity of — Christian Science! What not everyone may notice straightaway, however, is the fact that Nietzsche, as the fanatic of consistency that he was, thereby brought to light, though this time unintentionally, the innermost skepticism, indeed the utter nothingism, of all Socratism and hence, to be sure, of all “scientificity”; which consisted, namely, and consists in this: that in place of life, in place of reality itself, the “intellectual process,” i.e. dialectic, claims to be held real, to be held alone real!

Only let no one be deceived by doctrinairism! Socrates presupposes a conviction in every conversation, namely his own; another Socrates could presuppose another and defend it and carry it through just as well against less skillful dialecticians; as the succession of the philosophical systems of Europe has indeed sufficiently proved. For the mere art of interpretation and skill in inference, once declared sovereign, confronts the “world” as a chess problem whose problematic starting position it determines itself. It is by no means bound to fundamental assumptions (axioms), because fundamental assumptions only take place thanks to certain compulsions of vitality, whose title to them, however, is rejected by the dialectician! We establish three things: 1. Insofar as Nietzsche was a genuine skeptic, he must have been one out of a Socratism of disposition. 2. Socratism and skepticism are merely formally different manifestations of nihilism within the framework of rationality.

p. 184

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

3. “Science” as faith in the sovereignty of the understanding is nihilism. But we have already anticipated a little, and now for the present we adduce the second conclusion.

Knowledge, so we heard earlier, is to serve life; all corruption, so we have heard now, comes from erring. If, then, I want to abolish corruption, I would have to abolish errors and erring. But would I not thereby be striving for truth? Unconditionally! Consequently nothing would be more important, nothing more necessary, than truth itself! But what if every supposed truth were only a new deception, a new seduction, another — error! For I do, after all, secretly believe with Socrates in sovereign dialectic, which, if it cannot prove everything, can quite certainly refute everything. Here only a courageous faith helps, a resolution of the will by which I forbid myself further questioning once and for all; whereby we should for the second time be nicely in the midst of Christianity and in the neighborhood of, say, an Augustine! But doubt, the doubt that cannot be appeased, the evil twin brother of faith, returns. One would have to be a saint to master it, and even the saints succeeded in this only temporarily. But one already notices that this tarantella of thought finds no end. If one were to write down everything that Nietzsche asked in the spirit of the preceding questions and counter-questions, everything he answered and replied, then a reader who immersed himself with his soul might well believe he felt something like an attack of vertigo. We content ourselves with a few passages that bear witness as much to Nietzsche’s genuine skeptical spirit as to his passionate wrestling with it. I. Truth unconditionally desirable, even if dangerous. “A question seems to weigh on our tongue and yet not to want to be uttered: whether one can consciously remain in untruth, or, if one must do so, whether death would not then be preferable” (MA). “The great majority do not find it contemptible to believe this or that . . without first having become conscious of the ultimate . . reasons for and against . . But what are refinement and genius to me . . if the man of these virtues tolerates slack feelings in himself in . . judging; if the desire for certainty does not count for him as his innermost craving and deepest need” (F). “Something might be true, although it were in the highest degree harmful

p. 185

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and dangerous; indeed, it could even belong to the basic constitution of existence that one perished from the full knowledge of it; so that the strength of a spirit would be measured by how much of the ‘truth’ it could just still endure, more clearly, to what degree it needed it thinned down, sweetened, dulled, falsified” (J). “We would all rather have the destruction of mankind than the regression of knowledge” (M). “A shout of joy of knowledge — thy last sound” (MA). II. Truth not desirable, rather decidedly to be rejected. “Is it not enough to assume degrees of apparentness?” (J). “The drive to want to have nothing but certainties in this domain is a religious after-drive . . We do not at all need these certainties about the uttermost horizons in order to live a full and capable humanity . . . Even for the greatest lovers of knowledge it is more useful if around everything that can be explored . . there lies a befogged, deceptive belt of swamp* . .” (W). “Where nosce te ipsum would be the recipe for destruction, . . misunderstanding oneself . . becomes reason itself” (Eh). III. Consummate skepticism. “Truth is the kind of error without which a certain kind of living being could not live” (WzM). “Is not living estimating, preferring, being unjust . . ?” (J). “Life . . wants deception, it lives on deception” (MA). Truths are the “irrefutable errors of man” (F). “The concept ‘truth’ is absurd. The whole realm of ‘true — false’ refers only to relations between beings” (WzM). “The intention was to deceive oneself in a useful way” (WzM). “That mountain there! That cloud there! What is ‘real’ about them? Subtract once the phantasm and the whole human addition from them . .! Yes, if you could do that!” (F). “Nothing is true, everything is permitted” (ZG) or: “Everything is false, everything is permitted” (WzM). “Parmenides said, ‘one does not think what is not’; we are at the other end and say, ‘what can be thought must certainly be a fiction’” (WzM).

From I alongside II the terrible conflict becomes evident that splits Nietzsche’s drive for knowledge so thoroughly that one must speak outright of the struggle of a

p. 186

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

will to truth with a will to deception; compared with which the skepticism that confronts us in consummate form under III, more radical than any that ever existed, might strike one, at least in part, as a despair of the intellect at itself (even though, unmistakably, an enigmatic undertone still resonates in it); at the same time, as one notices, drawing up the formulas with whose help brand-new “pragmatisms,” “relativisms,” “fictionalisms” were able to set themselves to work!

Although it has been reeled off by school learning many dozens of times, we must nevertheless repeat in passing the objection with which antiquity already hoped to undermine the position of the skeptics, which, for the sake of brevity, may be done by means of a dialogue. A. Everything is false! B. Including, then, that everything is false? Well, then we need not argue. For you yourself have declared your assertion false! A. You are hair-splitting! I mean: for the supposed truth of a proposition there is no universally binding guarantee. No one can prove the correctness of his own view, nor, to be sure, can anyone prove the wrongness of the contrary one. To please you, I express myself more cautiously: nothing is true. B. But on that you insist? A. Certainly. B. Then you have asserted the truth of the judgment that nothing is true. But if there is one truth, then there is truth in principle and consequently an indefinite number of “truths.” A. Dogmatist that you are, you translate my words into the language of dogmatics. My “nothing is true” points to the fundamental uncertainty of any judgment whatever and chooses the dogmatic form solely for the purpose of contesting your claims to certainty. The assertion that nothing is true, too, naturally remains afflicted with uncertainty. B. Then you claim truth for this uncertainty. For either there is certainty or there is not. But you evidently set it down as certain that everything is uncertain. You have defeated yourself for the second time. A. I have no thought of setting it down as certain; it merely seems to me that everything is uncertain. To you it may seem the other way round, and I do not dispute that. But beyond this “it seems to me” no mortal gets.

p. 187

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

B. So you assert, and indeed as true and certain, that something seems to you to be thus or thus. However, even if you were still in doubt about that too, you will in any case have to decide whether you doubt or do not doubt, and with every decision you will have set foot on the ground of — claims to certainty. A. Shakes his head and is silent.

This is not, to be sure, historically the exact path, but it is roughly the straight line of the path that led to the ancient “ephexis,” ingeniously varied by Nietzsche in “The Fanatic of Mistrust and His Guarantee” (W) 47)

. The famous “suspension of judgment” (its chief formula: οὐδὲν ὁρίζω = I determine nothing, and ἐπέχω = I hold back) is, it goes without saying, only a droll self-deception. For it naturally does not matter at all whether someone utters judgments, but whether he actually passes them and behaves in accordance with a definite decision; which the animal guided only by instinct by no means does, but which man, endowed with the faculty of judgment, really does in every waking moment. If, being such a one, I step aside from the block of stone lying before me, to which my neighbor drew my attention, then I have enacted the holding-for-true that could be put, among other things, into the judgment: “There lies a block of stone on which one could stumble,” and have thus not merely judged but even judged in an indubitably definite manner, although I take careful pains not to let it be noticed by words or gestures.

The antiskeptical train of thought is irrefutable; but naturally no one knew this better than Nietzsche himself, who for his part renews the Hegelian objection to the critique of knowledge with the words: “How should the instrument be able to criticize itself when it can use only itself for the critique?” (WzM), and thus clearly gives to understand that it also cannot pass judgment on its own unfitness 48). But how, then, could he nevertheless advocate fundamental doubt? Well, without the admixture of Socratism that we have demonstrated, he would not have done it as he did; but precisely the denial of truth, by which the manifestation of his skepticism departs most sharply from the manifestation of classical skepticism, as we have just come to know it, and outwardly comes closer rather to the Socratic “I know that I know nothing,” again does not fit this one either, owing to its undertone of an at times downright passionate

p. 188

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

decisiveness, which — quite unmistakably akin to Christian resolutions of faith — leads one to suspect yet another, entirely different source of his spiritual attitude. Only by now revealing to what extent the pagan Nietzsche too has a share in those negations shall we seize the self-contradiction at the root, with the important by-product that there could be something like a pagan skepticism that would be unassailable, and that in all skepticism one must sharply keep apart this component of its conditions and the nothingist one.

But this precisely does not hold of Pyrrhonism, and least of all of the attitude of a Hume. Both are essentially negative; for they answer now wholly, now partly in the negative the question whether so-called knowledge possesses a certainty independent of the person, and, insofar as they are not content with a supposed refutation of the claims to certainty, they examine the logical and above all the psychological conditions of justified holding-for-true, or, more generally, the subjective degrees of probability of reasonable opinion. A genuinely pagan doubt, on the other hand, leads to the negation of certain premises of these skeptics just as much as of the dogmatists they combat, in that, out of mistrust of the value of consciousness, it suspects the value of knowing and hence the value of truth, even if there were such a thing, and seeks, all in all, to answer questions such as these: why do you insist on knowing; why do you attribute an extravagantly high significance to the activity of knowing and to its best possible result, the so-called truths; why not attribute the far higher significance to the experiencing that is without knowledge, without which the activity of knowing too would be at an end; why do you prefer standing outside to being within; does not this too bespeak an exclusion from life, about which you deceive yourselves with your praise, indeed your canonization, of knowledge, thereby degrading life itself to the footstool of your concepts of life?!

We already pointed in the first Part to essential utterances of Nietzsche’s in this direction, and we now expressly emphasize that he recognized with penetrating sharpness, as no one before him: all previous striving for knowledge grows out of faith in the all-surpassing value of knowing and is therefore incompatible with faith in an all-surpassing value of unconscious

p. 189

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

life. “There is no such thing as a science without presuppositions. The question whether truth is needed must not only have been answered in the affirmative beforehand, but answered in the affirmative to such a degree that . . the conviction is expressed in it: ‘nothing is more needful than truth, and in relation to it everything else has only a value of the second rank’” (F). That is a fundamental insight, and if we want to call its genuinely pagan conditions a skepticism, then such skepticism has nothing more than the name in common with epistemological skepticism. For in the latter, to reveal it now all at once, just as naturally as in Nietzsche’s Socratism, there is manifested, “by the detour of the head,” a diminished feeling of reality, in consequence of which lived experience sinks to the level of reality of the act of the spirit and the “intellectual process” is thus able to step into the place of reality; in the former, on the other hand, a feeling of reality of such tremendous luminous power that, struck by its rays, the mere striving for knowledge pales and turns gray and the “truths” henceforth vegetate among the tropical trees of life as stunted little shade plants! And now let it be well noted: though the inclination, indeed the passion, for research and knowledge may dwell in me, it is not necessary that I should therefore hold this passion to be the most valuable good of life, although, to be sure, I could escape such a false belief only if, for example, thanks to orgiasms experienced at some time or other, fuller, richer, deeper states of life were present to my memory.

It would mean leaving the theme “Nietzsche” and merely setting forth a philosophy of our own if we wished to explain as what the “will to truth” would in the last analysis have to present itself to a reflection prepared in this way; and only the incidental remark may be permitted that it would receive from such reflection by far not the highest, but still a high rank, and would in particular prove to be in the service not, as all have believed hitherto, of the spirit, but precisely of life! On the other hand, we now show with penetrating sharpness in what respect a Nietzsche, although he surmised what was right, went astray as a result of his Socratism.

Under the heading “Sorrow is Knowledge,” Nietzsche once appeals in MA (109) to the following verses of Byron’s:

Sorrow is knowledge: they who know the most

Must mourn the deepest o’er the fatal truth,

The Tree of Knowledge is not that of Life,

p. 190

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

which, though hardly in the author’s sense, yet according to their actual content could really form the guiding principle of a pagan investigation of our means of knowledge, in that they admonish us to hold up life against consciousness, lived experience against knowledge, and to grasp the former from the point of view of a life-threatening process. But if Nietzsche on the one hand does indeed proceed in accordance with these verses, on the other he proceeds in accordance with Schiller’s verses, understood literally:

Error alone is life,

And knowledge is death; thereby displacing the perhaps erroneous, but perhaps also accurate and, be it noted in passing, Taoist thesis: only not-knowing is life, and knowing is death, with the unconditionally false and thoroughly nothingist thesis: only erroneous opinion is life, and correct opinion is death; a thesis in whose line of extension would lie the monstrous conviction, which in its monstrousness to be sure borders on the comic, that liveliness grows with growing stupidity! However, no one comes nearer to life by growing stupid, no one by carefully avoiding cultivating his spirit; and supposing this very spirit were an evil threatening life, still no one gets rid of it by assailing with the most violent doubts precisely those judgments that are advocated by the strongest feelings of certainty! The idiot in the asylum is an idiot only because he is possessed by spirit, and if bondage to spirit is a disease of life, then the idiot suffers from it more gravely than the sensible average mind, and the latter in turn more gravely than a thinking genius. For the ground of the possibility of erring, of every erring, even of a mere miscalculation, lies in a disturbance of vitality, and indeed with arithmetical exactness in that disturbance in consequence of which we are receptive to the infectious substance called spirit; and whoever errs more often and more deeply is to the same extent more infected than whoever errs more rarely and more shallowly; which is why, conversely, every removal of an error, and quite especially of an ingrained, millennia-old error, means for him who partakes of it an approach to life, however little the mere knowledge of a reality may for that reason ever be confused with the lived experience of this reality. If Nietzsche’s entire work, as everyone senses, is nothing other

p. 191

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

than a grand and also grandly successful struggle against errors hostile to life, then it must, to be sure, appear to us in the light of a kind of madness when the creator of this work at the same time pays homage to the opinion that life needs for its preservation and flourishing — erring, and thus at times unquestionably feels his own philosophizing to be a “kind of vampirism” (F). Out of the true and life-affirming proposition: consciousness is a disturbance of life, there has come, in the twinkling of an eye, the arch-perverse and life-mocking proposition: life rests on false judgments. The reader has of course long since noticed how this hangs together. Since Nietzsche was able, as no one else, to convince us that the will actually needs errors, coarse, subtle, and exquisitely cunning ones, in order to nest itself into life and to maintain itself there, we should, to be sure, have to believe all this of life, were we, like him, to have fallen prey to the demon that prompted him to revoke his unmasking of the will with the sacrilegious assertion that life itself is — this will! But we must dwell a moment longer on the matter itself.

We have seen how Nietzsche seeks to escape self-strangulation: there are degrees of apparentness — everything known is a kind of fiction — life needs deception, etc. Did he seriously believe this? Did he hold it to be true in the same way as he held, for example, the life-murdering tactics of ressentiment to be true? No! He wanted only to believe it because he had to want it, and he is a hundred times on the point of uncovering the self-deception that has disguised itself in this belief, a horribly absurd belief. For it is all too obvious that, without presupposing a possible truth of fictions, deceptions, and appearances, I can no more speak meaningfully of them than, say, of non-being without presupposing a being! In this regard, read again in G the high-spirited scene with the heading “How the ‘True World’ Finally Became a Fable.” How does it end, after all? “The true world we have abolished: what world is left? The apparent one perhaps? But no! with the true world we have also abolished the apparent one.” (To be sure, there follows yet a stage direction on which one may reflect; and it ends with “Incipit Zarathustra.” What this enigmatic word means we shall learn in the next chapter.) Nevertheless, Nietzsche knew this, and yet he manages to serve us up the painful nonsense that there are deceptions,

p. 192

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

errors, fictions, although there are no truths; by which one may measure the force and inescapability of the hidden compulsion to which so luminous a head succumbed! But what, then, is the right answer to these confusing questions? the reader impatiently asks us. We have, to be sure, already made it known between the lines, but now we bring it fully to light.

If what we asserted above is correct, that Socratic doubt thrives on the soil of the diminished feeling of reality, through which mere dialectic is able to step into the place of reality, then we need only mix with it a varying dose of a will to truth that is still vigorous with life, and the disposition is ready that was systematically founded by Plato and not overcome by the epistemological efforts of two millennia: a disposition which, if not consciously and deliberately, at any rate under pressure and involuntarily, equates the reality of truth, and therefore also the truth of reality, and thus in its search for truth is guided by the delusion that what it hopes to find must be a constituent of reality. But since such truths have never been found and never will be found, one falls, if one is honest, into the despair of knowledge. Why, after all, did Nietzsche in the scene just cited have to abolish the “apparent world,” i.e. the world of appearances? Because he treated the chimera of the “true world,” or, to put it less impolitely, because he treated correct judgments about the world exactly as though they were themselves a world, i.e. a reality.

The astronomer Lalande is said to have made the remark: “I have searched the heavens (with the telescope, of course) and not found God!” What would that mean if generalized? We choose an example. If there is a lawn before my eyes and I consequently judge: here is a lawn, or somewhat less simply: here is the lawn we were looking for, then I have judged correctly, my judgment is true. But the microscope, the ultramicroscope, will never be built with which one could find the content of the correct judgment, that is, a truth, anywhere in the world, e.g. in the head of the one who bears the judgment, in the way that with the help of the judgment one may well find a reality that it concerned, here, then, the lawn. Contents of judgments, correct as well as erroneous, and hence truths, do not belong to reality (as surely as the processes by whose help they are thought naturally do belong to it); and yet the Socratically sicklied will to truth

p. 193

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

has for two and a half millennia posed and decided all questions about the nature and range of knowing on the presupposition that cognitions were realities; and it is, be it noted in passing, not even the avowed forms of skepticism, but the unavowed ones, by the names of formalism, panlogism, and whatever other faith in concepts still cultivates such phrases as that of the “eternal realm of timeless ideas,” in which the nothingist core of the fundamental error breaks through completely. And what, by contrast, would the judgment actually be?

A means of communication among thinking beings about reality, arisen according to the necessary rules of an interiority capable of thought, which has any meaning at all only with reference to such beings. If I take out of the world all beings that would understand the content of such propositions as “the circle is not a square” or “the earth moves in an elliptical orbit around the sun,” then there is no longer any validity of the contents in question either, because the objects corresponding to them occur nowhere in reality. But just as contents of judgment harbor not the least bit of reality, but are merely directions for thinking intelligences, which would lead those capable of following them first to a world of objects that replaces reality, but finally always to unthinkable contents of lived experience, just as surely can they point in right as well as wrong directions, suffering, as it were, now from frequent slips of the pen, now largely free of them. Only he who no more looks for a reality in truth than he looks in the score for the piece of music that it signifies is proof once and for all against every kind of Socratism and skepticism, and need no longer purchase his faith in truth with the delusion that reality itself can be “grasped”!49)

But is it really of such consequence if one goes wrong in answering such questions; must we really connect this with a central conflict, and might it not rather be one of those lapses that are closer to the miscalculations common to all men? we hear a harmless reader ask. Well, we will give him in conclusion an indication that in a thinker of Nietzsche’s unheard-of spiritual power the fundamental epistemological conviction, be it skeptical, be it dogmatic, is of exactly as much or as little consequence as if he had, say, set forth with dazzling reasons the desirability Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 13

p. 194

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of general murder or straightaway of the instantaneous suicide of all; and for this we choose an example which, like few others, is suited to confirm our proposition that he cherished one and the same conviction from the beginning, and that from the beginning the fundamental conflict of his nature split more or less every page he wrote.

As is well known, a treatise with the title “On Truth and Lie in an Extra-Moral Sense” was found among his posthumous papers, which he wrote down in 1873, that is, even before the U. It begins with the following words: “In some remote corner of the universe, poured out glimmering in countless solar systems, there was once a star on which clever animals invented knowing. It was the most arrogant and most mendacious minute of ‘world history’: but still only a minute. After a few breaths of nature the star grew cold, and the clever animals had to die. — Someone might invent such a fable and would still not have sufficiently illustrated how wretched, how shadowy and fleeting, how purposeless and arbitrary the human intellect appears within nature. — — There were eternities in which it was not; when it is over with it again, nothing will have happened.” The first dash comes from Nietzsche; the double dash following it we have set. Up to the first dash it is the same pagan Nietzsche speaking who holds consciousness to be a kind of disease of life and shatters the substrate concepts together with the belief in causes; the sentence that then follows shimmers with a double sense, as we shall presently convince ourselves; but the closing sentence is an almost improbable deception into which the Christian Nietzsche talked the pagan Nietzsche.

When it is over with the intellect again, nothing will have happened?? In an age when the arsenal of murder machines, which no individual understanding can any longer encompass, has covered the face of the whole planet as with bloody weals and will shortly have exterminated the last of all the animal species not preserved for purposes of slaughter; when the wire-spun giant cities more and more resemble dubious oases in the literally growing desert of the earth’s surface, whose last primeval forest tree is only a question of a few years; when the humanity of former times, the humanity of temples, of cults, of images of the gods, murdered by the blows of “civilization,” lies in its death throes, and with it everything is irresistibly vanishing,

p. 195

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

of which a few profiteers are trying to save at least the mummies in museums and picture books for a while yet — in such an age a thinker who felt and knew this to his innermost core tells us that the intellect, without which at the very least nothing of the kind could have happened, will, when extinguished again, have left the world as it found it: that is so horrible an absurdity that one would have to doubt the understanding of this thinker, were one blind enough to fail to recognize in it the “fear-inspiring” power of the evil will, which breaks the point off the unmasking seer’s word about the “most mendacious minute of world history” by presenting it as a fleeting, shadowy phantasmagoria that, as it were, flitted past reality. And here and there this will breaks out and has Nietzsche bear witness to its hideous triumphs: “Hubris is today our whole attitude toward nature,” it says in ZG, “our violation of nature with the help of machines and the so unscrupulous inventiveness of technicians and engineers; hubris is our attitude toward God . . Hubris is our attitude toward ourselves; for we experiment on ourselves . . and cheerfully and curiously slit open the soul in the living body.” Note well: Nietzsche gives this his vehement applause, although his words in the process distort themselves into a grimace (“nutcrackers of the soul,” who “become ever more questionable, more worthy of questioning, and thereby perhaps also more worthy — of living?”).

But we need not go on. The minute of world history in question can appear mendacious only to him who — a last disciple of the god Dionysus — holds the will to power that hatched it to be hostile to life and dangerous to life; it can appear ineffectual only to him who accuses life itself of such arts of lying and undertakes to ennoble the “bad things” with the help of the abominable lie that they are the father of the “good things”: he, the cunning priest of the will to power. (“All good things were once bad things” (ZG). Incidentally: the theme of so-called psychoanalysis today.) We no longer doubt: something in Nietzsche wrestles passionately for truth, and something in Nietzsche is in wild flight from — truth.

13*

PDF 200

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

THE MOTIF OF OVERCOMING

Were it not the Christian in Nietzsche who, with the doctrine of the will to power, speaks to confessors of the will to power, namely to Christians, then even his sparkling dialectic and incomparable art of presentation would hardly have sufficed to conceal the self-contradiction even where it rises quite unveiled from the interior to the surface. The will to power is supposed to be life, life the will to power. Now it is precisely the priest who manifests the toughest, most ruthless will to power, one that never fails even under the most difficult conditions; he puts to shame with his arts of dissimulation and deception the recipes of Machiavelli, and becomes master over warriors, kings, over all mankind: thus he would clearly be the manifestation of life most to be revered. But it is supposed to be the will to power of a disease of life, indeed a will to power of weakness and a will to nothingness, that he represents. Should one think it credible that anyone besides Nietzsche himself, who on this point was blinded for reasons known to us, failed to notice the complete hollowness of such turns of phrase?

If life is will, then strong will means richness of life, weak will poverty of life; and if we call the latter a sickening of life, then he would suffer from it who omits what the priestly type meant here never omits, namely willing; whereas he, the constant willer, would rather embody the supreme pole of health of life. To speak of a weakness at all would be to speak of wooden iron, of black whiteness, indeed of a non-existent existent! How can I impute a weakness to sovereign willing when reality itself is willing through and through! On the contrary, the priest would now be the most extreme orgiast. Finally, nothingness — that does not exist at all, cannot exist, at least not in the sense of an opposite to the something, in a doctrine that degraded the something into a mere function

p. 197

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

— or, more precisely, into the act of will, whose only possible object would after all be the resistance of the alien will and whose only possible content the overcoming of resistance, but never by any means a goal to be described in substantive terms. One can no longer will nothingness when the state of willing has become the only something still remaining. If, on the other hand, I separate goals and willing and assess willing according to the nature of the goal, then I have set over against the will to success a world different from it down to its very foundation, and have irredeemably struck out my proposition that reality itself is the will! These are contradictions so glaring that the contradictions of Schopenhauer’s world-view romance, lumpy enough in and of themselves, shrink beside them into harmless little knots.

But the primal contradiction we have uncovered, which in purely dialectical terms consists in this, that life and will are both identified and set against each other in deadly opposition, not only gives rise to the hidden antinomies of which we made mention earlier and to the quite obvious ones we have just discussed, but splits apart all of Nietzsche’s inquiries and all of his findings, and the consequences of this are now dangerous gaps and unwelcome distortions, now propositions that are erroneous without qualification. Of these we have pointed out the struggle of his faith in truth with his disbelief in truth, his confusion of the value of consciousness with the value of truth, his indecision — as hopeless as it is tormenting — on the question, of vital importance precisely for his system, whether thinking depends on willing or willing on thinking, his actual use of the concept of the ego while verbally denying its legitimacy; and we have at least intimated, and also confirmed by a few especially convincing examples, that the opposition without which he cannot philosophize for so much as a page, the opposition of affirmation of life and envy of life, would vanish into vapor without the assumption that the will is different from life, and with it all the separations and distinctions that Nietzsche made in its name! His paganism enabled him to find them and to hold fast to them unswervingly; but with regard to his doctrine of the will this is a self-contradiction that no dialectic can remove. Before we now come closer to the motif of overcoming, already touched upon, with whose triumphant breakthrough the catastrophe “Zarathustra” begins for Nietzsche, we shall bring to notice, if only in passing, three more grave inadequacies

p. 198

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

and remark in this connection that the astonishing consistency by virtue of which they hang together logically both with one another and with all the rest bears witness, to be sure, to the consistency of Nietzsche’s thinking as well, but still more to the fact that the conflict of the soul familiar to us was in fact the ever-present condition of all his movements of thought.

I. Since Nietzsche makes no distinction in principle between the impulse of a drive and the impulse of the will, he also cannot distinguish between the conditions of the two, or, more briefly, between drives and interests, and should therefore have abolished either the drive or the will. Now although in the individual case he does treat willing at one moment as a being-driven and at another being-driven as a willing, nevertheless, in keeping with his principal doctrine, his intention (just as with Schopenhauer, only in the service of an opposite higher intention) indisputably aims at the annihilation of the drive; and from this there arise countless discords, and there arises in particular the hostile-to-life false doctrine that pathic experience, too, is activity, and that lived experience (= what befalls us, what we undergo) is at bottom an act of will.

II. If we now cast a glance back at his philosophy of orgiasm and his magnificently eloquent glorification of bestowing love, we recognize at once that while it was this which enabled him to unmask the lust for power in the most highly praised social traits of man, it was nevertheless his belief in the all-encompassing reality of the will that prevented him from tracing those traits of the soul that are exposed to infection by the will. The most important and in any case most lamentable consequences of this are: first, a brazen barrier set to his penetrating insight by the fact that for him the determination of values remains the ultimate. Life knows only qualities and neither values nor degrees; the will knows only degrees; the will, attached to life within the living space of the personal ego, transforms the manifoldness of qualities into a graded series of values. If we set Nietzsche’s supreme reference words, such as rank, distance, table of values, selection, revaluation, breeding, organization, lordliness, sovereignty, etc., beside the customary “epochistics” of the theories of knowledge and of scientific scholarship, they are more full-blooded than these; if we set them beside full lived experience itself, and especially beside his own disclosures of the content of orgiastics, they appear bloodless, shadowy, thin, unreal, indeed ghostly, as we have repeatedly

p. 199

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

had occasion to show. In this respect he compares badly with the most important thinkers of Romanticism, even if these in their turn, to be sure, lag immeasurably behind him in insight into the deceptions that endanger life. — But more than this, and here we touch upon a further defect of his voluntarism: whoever knows how to tell us about life only how it looks on the ego’s table of values runs the risk of misjudging it just as malignantly as the mere intellectualist, in that he makes judging life into the basic function of life itself and thereby displaces life with the legislating will. “Only man placed values in things . ., he first created a meaning for things, a human meaning!” “Only through esteeming is there value: and without esteeming the nut of existence would be hollow” (!) (Z). But that is precisely the nihilism that the pagan Nietzsche combats. Of this, moreover, the inexorable clarity of his spirit was granted the suicidal knowledge: “With this nihilism is here: one has kept the judging values and nothing else!” (WzM). One sees that it always comes down to one and the same thing. — Finally: all his disclosures remain incomplete in consequence. For if bestowing love exists, then it stands in deadly opposition to the exploiting will, and it would be our task to investigate not only the latter but just as much the genuine capacities for devotion. Since Nietzsche entirely neglects this, the nature of man and even of the animals stands there in a satanic light, and the word “love” in his mouth very often sounds like the cry of one mortally wounded; whereupon one might recall with horror the fact that persons about to be executed frequently stammer “mother,” calling in this way upon the being that Nietzsche forgot in his picture of the world.

III. Third, however: a proposition such as “Love — in its means war, at its foundation the deadly hatred of the sexes” (Eh) is not so much a grimace as the expression of an irresponsible narrowness. Romanticism had rediscovered the metaphysical concept of polarity and achieved its greatest and most enduring work in applying it to the interpretation of the organism. Nietzsche, confusing life with the will and accordingly understanding all differences ultimately as hostile “differences,” eliminates polarity and therefore remains, astonishingly, blind to a state of affairs without which we cannot interpret a single vital process

p. 200

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

rightly: the fact of interconnectedness for the sake of completion. What with regard to historical man was only a one-sidedness of thought, to which we owe Nietzsche’s greatest discoveries, becomes with regard to life itself a paralysis of one side. — Once again, to be sure, one may admire the consistency or the strength of the motive when Nietzsche drives the rejection of polarities up into his science of consciousness and expressly teaches that the concepts of opposition are to be abolished; whereby he regrettably becomes the pacemaker of psychological nihilism, as the period that followed has proved. “Health and sickness are nothing essentially different, as the old physicians . . believe. One must not make distinct principles . . of them, which fight over the living organism . . That is silly . . chatter, which is no longer good for anything. In fact there are only differences of degree between these two kinds of existence” (WzM). How the heart of the psychologist of the “present day” leaps in his breast at this! However, when Nietzsche invites us “to take the oppositions out of things, after one has grasped that we have put them in” (WzM), we recommend trying his prescription on the following oppositions in turn: waking–sleeping, blood–water, living–dead, being–not-being, true–false; and venture to hope that perhaps even this or that “modern” physician will at least examine the question whether it would not be more advisable and more promising to return in this respect to the “old physicians.” — — Finally, it should not go unmentioned that with this extreme Christianness of his thinking Nietzsche, the passionate denier of the ego, at bottom pays homage to — Fichteanism. The opposition between being alive and being dead not lying in reality itself but put into it by us, a mere phantom of thought, invented by the sovereign ego (or will), perhaps awaiting a revaluer of all values who will command it out: out with you! — that is either “silly chatter” or a corollary of the at least meaningful, but for all that no less fundamentally false, metaphysics according to which the so-called world, indeed reality itself, would be merely a barrier that the ego had set for itself out of its own arbitrary will! In Part One we cited Nietzsche’s ambiguous words: “Only at the end of the knowledge of all things will man have known himself.” They stand in M under the heading “ ‘Know thyself’ is

p. 201

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

the whole of ‘science’ ” and precede the following sentence, which concludes the aphorism: “For things are only the boundaries of man.” For “things,” taken literally, that would indeed be true; but here by things Nietzsche means reality, or at least the reality of the external world; for otherwise he would not have set man and his εἰς ἑαυτόν over against them! Sapienti sat! — But we wished to show the catastrophe that had to break in upon the spirit of an inquirer who held fast with such unwavering steadfastness to two mutually exclusive, indeed mortally hostile standpoints: that of the unconditional affirmation of life and that of the unconditional affirmation of the will.

Supposing Nietzsche’s master-man were not merely a work of wishful thinking but capable of life, then one thing at any rate he would not be: — a ruler by nature! That there have been ruler natures, and in part still are, is beyond doubt; how one might classify them into types need not concern us further here; enough that we are unable to conceive of the “born ruler” without the two following traits: he does not strive for power, because he has power, and he administers power by dispensing it. True, if the plebeian majority denies a ruler of this kind the exercise of his power, so that he must first take it for himself amid struggles and dangers (in which, of course, he may also fail), then to the superficial observer he seems for a time to strive for power, whereas in truth he strives for his right in no other way than would one who had been prevented by unlawful tutelage from disposing of his “fortune.” And just as such a man, on regaining the right of disposal, would by no means be a mere moneymaker simply because he had first had to fight for it, but might even hasten to spend it completely, so the genuine ruler, upon regaining the right of disposal over the power that has come down to him by the grace of fate, instantly ceases to strive for further power, even though in dispensing power he will never divest himself of his own. (Exemplary portrayals of two genuine ruler natures may be found, for instance, in Stifter’s Witiko. They look astonishingly different from Nietzsche’s master-man; but they are certainly alive!)

Once one has sharpened one’s eye for the ruler nature through so simple a consideration, one more easily discovers the contradiction

p. 202

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

from which the conception of the will to power, taken as the essence of the world, suffers within itself, that is, irrespective of whether reality contradicts it. It is of course the same one of which Stirner’s “Unique One” perishes, but compressed by Nietzsche far more weightily, because at every step it was wrested in fierce struggles from the orgiast within him. — Let us state at the outset: the Christian concept of omnipotence, taken literally, is of course only a game of thought. An omnipotent being could by its mere wish annihilate the whole world in an instant, but it could not bring it about that the world had never been there at all beforehand, in which case the act of annihilation would indeed not have been needed in the first place: that is, roughly, the most popular form of refutation of the idea of omnipotence; but it can be given in a thousand ways. The Talmudists developed puzzles of the will much as the Megarians did puzzles of reason, and know, for example, the question: can God create a stone so heavy that he himself would be unable to move it? As one notices, omnipotence is reduced ad absurdum by the answer Yes just as by the answer No. — However, much as we make the strict extensionlessness of the mathematical point perceptible to the senses by means of a little ball of graphite and have no hesitation in calling it a point, so too we can quite well conceive of a man’s sphere of power as heightened to a kind of omnipotence, and we have no doubt at all that there have been plenty of Western Caesars and Eastern despots who considered themselves more or less omnipotent and who, in this feeling, were scarcely disturbed in any sensible way, at least for a time. (To be sure, the final disturbance never fails to come: we mean death.) Nor must one forget that the will feels as an essential barrier only the alien will, and will-less nature only insofar as it experiences it, in its turn, after the manner of a counter-will. And now let us make the application, putting in the first person.

I am through and through will to power, a mighty will to power; I come up against alien spheres of will; I break through them, wrestle them down; I conquer lands, subjugate peoples, found a “world empire”; if it pleases me, with the help of the star-rocket, at once including all the planets “under the sun”; I rule to the very edge of my mental field of vision, I am practically “omnipotent.” Behold — a horrifying success! The ever-hungry will, of which after all I consist entirely, lacks resistances of equal rank,

p. 203

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in whose overcoming alone it is able to live. Only one of two things remains open to me: I hang myself, or — I strip myself of my power, become the poorest, most helpless, most powerless beggar in my realm, in which guise I have the redeeming prospect of being able to devote myself without limit to the upstart’s sport of striving for power!

Yet we need not first climb the ladder of power up to the topmost rung in order nevertheless to be threatened at every moment of our waking existence by the same disgust with existence, provided only that we consist through and through of will to power. For no sooner have I attained anything at all that I willed, be it ever so slight, be it ever so great, than it has gone stale in a flash, and in a flash I must look about me for what might now lure my hunger of will and where a strongest resistance might tower up against me, all the while secretly tormented already (traumatically, as it were) by the expectation of the horror that would infallibly be in store for me after I had overpowered this resistance too. No doubt, that is hell, nay, the hell of hells, and — necessity is the mother of invention, I sense a way out! What if each time I forbade myself my willing, the strongest appetite of my willing at any given moment, by means of that still stronger will which I myself am! I feed my greed of will with systematically practiced — self-overcoming.

We have just recounted the “moral” of the penitent’s tale of King Viswamitra (from the Ramayana of the Indians)50), which Nietzsche rightly adduces in support of his proposition that ascetic self-mortification is the emergency exit of an extreme overstraining of the will to power. It was Nietzsche who dug this up as the mother-ground of all moralities, but above all of the vengeful morality of Christianity, and it is Nietzsche who, thanks to his confusion of life with unbridled frenzy of will, in the end falls prey to this very same morality! Ecce homo!

One would hardly believe it, but it is so. Burrowing and rebelling in the depths from the beginning, betraying itself here and there in convulsive shocks and spasms, the ascetic’s faith breaks out like a jet of flame in — Zarathustra, setting the whole Nietzsche ablaze and as it were drenching him with devouring fire. The blaze was damped down once more, but never again extinguished, and its return, which was surely to be expected, would surely have meant even physical ruin.

p. 204

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Admittedly, Zarathustra too contains a series of Dionysian passages, from which we ourselves have drawn evidence, contains interpolated songs, and successfully continues the critique of the modernity of holding-for-true and of willing. But if — with Nietzsche — one has supposed it to be the truly Dionysian testimony (instead of The Birth of Tragedy, which really is), then such an aberration betrays only that it was the reader’s Christianness that did the judging and that, generous with praise in the spirit of the one praised, it lavished it upon his — most Christian work, which not by chance relapses into the language of the Lutheran Bible and into the preacher’s tone that may once have enveloped the heart of the boy (perhaps out of an inherited receptivity to it).

“I teach you the going-under of man” — that could be Dionysian, and then the corollary would run: I teach you the rising of the god! But who would doubt that what it means in truth is that with which Zarathustra begins his first great speech before the people: “I teach you the overman. Man is something that shall be overcome!” All in all, Zarathustra is an enthusiastically uncanny exegesis of the reference word “over.” Overfullness, overgoodness, overtime, overkind, overrichness, overhero, to overdrink oneself: these are a few out of the great number of over-words, partly newly coined, partly used again and again, and just as many readings of the one thing exclusively meant: overcoming. But let us first consider the surroundings of Zarathustra’s mountain landscape and the path leading to it; in doing so let one not forget for a moment that it is the philosopher of orgiasm, the dithyrambist of the “image-sparkling” bliss of pathic necessity, the renewer of faith in fate and of Heraclitean self-transformation, the singer of bestowing exuberance, the grim adversary of all purposes falsely imputed to life, the merciless unmasker of idealisms, the glorifier of blood and of this world, who now speaks to us like an arch-idealist, like a “penitent of the spirit,” like a fanatic of self-mortification. This time we give the evidence chronologically, that is, up to Zarathustra and then from that point on. U. — “Your true nature lies not hidden deep within you, but immeasurably high above you.” (God!) — The most venerable specimen of the species man is he who “at every moment . . consumes himself tragically on an impossible virtue.” — “Above even the greatest

p. 205

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

man there rises his own ideal.” — “So long as anyone craves life as one craves a happiness, he has not yet lifted his gaze above the horizon of the animal.” MA II. — “Writing should always announce a victory, and indeed an overcoming of oneself.” W. — “Every day is badly used, and a danger for the next, on which one has not at least once denied oneself something in a small way.” M. — Let one read, and read repeatedly, M 114, “On the Knowledge of the Sufferer”!
