No Eastern and no Western saint has ever described the paroxysms of self-mortification and the convulsions of ascetic pride more glowingly, more fervently, more extravagantly than Nietzsche does here: “With contempt (!) he (the sufferer) thinks of the cozy, warm, misty world in which the healthy man walks without a second thought; with contempt he thinks of the noblest and most beloved illusions . .; he takes pleasure in conjuring up this contempt as if from the deepest hell and thus causing his soul the bitterest suffering . . In a horrifying clairvoyance about his own nature he calls out to himself: ‘Be for once your own accuser and executioner . .!’ . . Our pride rears up as never before . .; (for) now we want to show that we can be ‘without guilt.’ We find ourselves in veritable convulsions of pride.” Afterward, however, we humble “without gratitude the omnipotent pride through which we have just endured the pain, and crave violently an antidote to pride.” — “You must each day also wage your campaign against yourself.” — “Our insight and faith . . rise straight upward above our head and above its powerlessness.” F. — “Men who, with an inward inclination, seek in all things for what in them is to be overcome.” — “Oh, how little you know of the happiness of man, you comfortable and good-natured ones! for happiness and unhappiness are two siblings . ., who grow up together.” — “We Argonauts of the ideal” Now follows Z. J. — “There is a great ladder of religious cruelty with many rungs; but three of them are the most important. Once one sacrificed human beings to one’s god . . Then, in the more moral epoch of mankind, one sacrificed to one’s god the strongest instincts that one

p. 206

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

possessed . . Finally: what still remained to be sacrificed? Did one not finally have to sacrifice everything comforting, holy, healing . .? did one not have to sacrifice God himself and, out of cruelty against oneself, . . worship nothingness?” — “You want, if possible, . . to abolish suffering; and we? — it seems precisely that we would rather have it higher and worse than it ever was! . . The discipline of suffering, of great suffering — do you not know that only this discipline has created all the elevations of man so far?” ZG. — “All great things perish through themselves, through an act of self-cancellation: thus the law wills it . . of the necessary ‘self-overcoming’ in the nature of life.” A. — “The spiritual men, as the strongest, find their happiness where others would find their ruin . . Their pleasure is self-conquest: with them asceticism becomes nature, need, instinct.” (!)

That is only a meager anthology, but sufficient nevertheless to show us that the soil of the alarmingly treeless landscape where we have set foot may well harbor the stones from which Zarathustra builds “mountain ranges out of ever holier peaks.” Of this, too, a few samples. “Is your soul not . . a wretched contentment?” “Overcome for me . . wretched contentment!” “Ah, this wretched contentment of two.” “When you despise the agreeable . ., there is the origin of your virtue!” (Kant would have been delighted! Recommended, by the way, as an inscription over a Trappist monastery!) “Not only onward shall you propagate yourself, but upward!” (That, in the mouth of the denier of purposes in nature!) “Virtue is will to going-under and an arrow of longing.” “A table of goods hangs over every people. Behold, it is the table of its overcomings!” “Can you . . hang your will above yourself like a law? Can you be judge for yourself and avenger of your law?” “Willing liberates.” (!) “What does he know of love who did not have to despise precisely what he loved!” (What does he know of love, we ask in return, who can talk himself into believing that he loves what he despises!) “What is the heaviest thing, you heroes . .? Is it not this: to abase

p. 207

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

oneself in order to hurt one’s pride? To let one’s folly shine in order to mock one’s wisdom?” (A typical procedure of early Christian saints.) “Are you the victorious one, the self-conqueror, the commander of the senses, the master of your virtues?” (Something similar a thousand times in Marcus Aurelius, Seneca, Epictetus and others — Protestants of antiquity!)

Thus speaks the self-flagellant and fanatical priest. But because he is chained together with the orgiast in one person, his gestures become confused and mingle with disconcerting traits. A veritable frenzy of overcomings seems to seize the prophet, until on level ground he parodies climbing and plays the “Don Quixote of knowledge” of whom elsewhere he knew how to tell us so eloquently. We give a few more passages of this kind, in order of intensification. “This is my slope and my danger, that my gaze plunges into the heights, and that my hand would hold on and support itself — on the depths!” “The arm laid over the head: thus should the hero rest, thus should he overcome even his resting.” (!) “For this is the secret of the soul: only when the hero has abandoned her does there approach her, in a dream, the overhero.” “Not on one ladder did I climb to the heights.” “Life wants to climb and in climbing to overcome itself.” “Behold, it (life) said, I am that which must always overcome itself.” “Upward goes our way, from the species over to the over-species.” “And if from now on all ladders are lacking to you, then you must know how to climb even upon your own head.” “Onward, upward, until you have even your stars still beneath you!” “Now I see myself beneath me.”

Nietzsche calls it Zarathustra’s going-under; we call it Nietzsche’s “having climbed astray.” At the end of the way, insofar as one could speak of an end here, there beckons — folly. The “Incipit Zarathustra” — we no longer doubt what it meant: self-overcoming of the drive for knowledge; and thereafter one would be free “to say Yes and No in one breath” (NcW), and the bold skepticism of “everything is false” would, if need be, accommodate itself to the “credo quia absurdum” more familiar to us. The “executioner-god,” of whom Nietzsche has the “Sorcerer” sing, is close to completing his business.

PDF 212

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

ON NIETZSCHE’S

SELF - ANNIHIL ATION

Whoever among our readers is content to have learned what, according to its title, our work had promised — Nietzsche’s psychological achievements — need take no further notice of this chapter. And if with it we now for our part make a contribution to the characterology of the creative spirit, of which we declared at the outset that it did not immediately concern us, this supplement nonetheless intends to be no more than the expression of an opinion, upon which whoever takes pleasure in it may reflect. Whether one agrees with it wholly or in part, whether one rejects it wholly or in part, neither strengthens nor diminishes the weight of the reasons we have put forward for and against Nietzsche’s findings. The dogmatic formulation henceforth therefore serves merely for brevity. — Having developed Nietzsche’s results partly by way of a conflict of the soul within him, our critique inevitably led us deeper into this conflict as well, and so it seems fitting to us not to keep silent our final formula for it, although at best it can deepen the understanding of what has already been decided, and by no means increase or extend the decisions themselves.

When people have repeatedly fallen into the easily understandable error that Nietzsche was really a poet, they overlooked the fact that the highest mastery of language has always been attained not by poetry alone, but also, for example, by the oratory of great preachers (think of Eckhart, Luther, Görres!); and when, to justify this view, people even pointed to numerous poetic prose passages in his work and to his verses, which are after all not so very few, then in the face of so unique an art of communication it ought rather, as it seems to us, to arouse surprise that poetic passages are not found far more often and that his verses only by way of exception crystallize into a genuine poem. Just set any one of our great poets and masters of verse from the last one and a half

p. 209

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

centuries beside him — Goethe, Hölderlin, Eichendorff, Lenau, Droste, Hebbel, Conrad Ferdinand, etc. — and one is at once convinced that Nietzsche would not hold his own on that field and would, through such comparisons, be irretrievably misjudged in his most proper significance.

Even the assumption, admissible enough in principle, that in this respect he was a hybrid formation (as some of the very greatest were, e.g. Goethe) and harbored at least a piece of a poet, seems to us ruled out in view of the circumstance that all the pronouncements he made about verse and poetry are not only for the most part wrong, but even remain, throughout, far beneath the level of his spirit; which, in a thinker of the most astonishing clairvoyance for the plastic arts in general and for musical composition in particular, can only be explained by an innermost lack of kinship with precisely the poets. Meter, he thinks in MA, lays over reality “the veil of impure thinking” (151, similarly W 105); the poet drives his thoughts along festively “on the chariot of rhythm,” because they could not go on foot (189); he is a deceived deceiver (II 32), the “valet of some morality or other” (F 1), a jingle-bell buffoon (by which Shakespeare is supposed to have meant himself in “Caesar” [F 98]), the “foster brother of the liar” (F 222); he talks as the people like to hear, in that he mostly puts forward things learned from books and from priests (!), yet considers himself the mouthpiece of God (MA II 176). On rhythm and the origin of poetry, F 84 puts forward — with all due respect — childish notions unworthy of a Nietzsche; and finally, as regards the at least witty Zarathustra sermon “On the Poets,” it is impossible not to hear the coquetry in the tone with which Zarathustra assures us in the third person: “But Zarathustra too is a poet.” No, Nietzsche was not even a piece of a poet, although thanks to his miraculous mastery of language he offers sentences of the most suggestive urgency in abundance, and poetic passages as well.

But read, after the sermon on the poets, the other one, “On the Priests,” and whoever then still doubts where Nietzsche belongs one would hope in vain to teach better with a thousand reasons. “Here are priests: and even if they are my enemies, pass by them quietly and with sleeping sword! — Among them too there are heroes; many of them suffered too much: so they want to make others suffer Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 14

p. 210

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

. . My blood is related to theirs; and I want to know my blood honored even in theirs. — And when they had passed by, pain assailed Zarathustra*” (Z).

With this, then, every doubt would be removed concerning the formula that would comprehend his nature: he was the battleground of the orgiast whom he portrayed for us with the ascetic priest whom he unmasked for us, or — in the abbreviating language of myth — the scene of the struggle between Dionysus and Yahweh. We know of no second “world-historical” example of it! Often the conflict Dionysus–Socrates was and is to be found, more often the conflict Socrates — Yahweh; but that one and the same personality should belong at once to Dionysus and to Yahweh is a case of the rarest and most terrible kind. From the standpoint of the Yahwist it signifies a priest who has gone off the rails; from the standpoint of the Bacchant, an orgiast who was put under a spell to “dance in chains”; from the standpoint of life, inescapable self-annihilation; but from the standpoint of knowledge, a tragic stroke of fortune of the first rank: for it is precisely to the high tension of this opposition that we owe the luminous intensity of Nietzsche’s thinking, which in this way reveals not so much a personality as its unstoppable self-annihilation.

Once one has this key, riddle after riddle resolves itself effortlessly, far more than can still be touched upon here; and one will, to be sure, scarcely be able to close one’s mind to the conviction that Nietzsche knew this too: he who sympathized passionately with all the tortured (e.g. with Pascal, e.g. with the Indian at the stake (M 135)), who calls himself a “self-executioner” and solemnly proclaims in Z: “We all bleed at secret sacrificial altars, we all burn and roast in honor of old idols,” and from whose mouth there breaks again and again the cry of torment for redemption (e.g. ZG II 24), although in theory he rages against nothing more wrathfully than against the Christian idea of redemption.

What the two symbolic names mean requires no further elucidation after what has gone before, and only concerning Nietzsche’s Dionysian side may a closing remark be in place, in view of our rejection of his supposed poethood. If anyone is capable at all of inner explosions, this will color the whole mood of his life and, if he is a creator, will impart to everything he brings forth a vibrating, glowing, flickering, storming or surging that can only be relived, regardless of whether he writes poetry, thinks, composes, paints, or builds. For that very reason,

p. 211

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in the realm of language no less, the magic of the word is realized through the poet alone; and measured against it Nietzsche’s unsurpassed art of speech strikes us as a translation into the medium of language of other forms of expression of orgiasm, namely, if we are not mistaken, of the intimately related forms of the dancer and the musician. (For it is at times as though music trickled through him down to his toes and fingertips, and his verses seem rather to be melodies, melodies accompanying the steps of a dance, for which the dancer only afterward invents the words! Read with this in mind, for example, the “Cheerfulness, golden one, come!” with its constant syncopations). —

The conflict would of course have found its end, and there could henceforth scarcely be any talk of creative self-annihilation, had one of the two powers decisively gained the victory. If we now consider the consequences that had to ensue once this did not happen, then the first thing in which our explanation proves itself is that we now illuminate down to its deepest ground the connection between Nietzsche’s inexorable knowledge and his — flight into doubt. The orgiast in him experiences the priest in him as an intolerable disturbance and consequently comes to reflect on the life-hostile meaning of priestliness; the priest in him experiences precisely this awareness as an intolerable disturbance and drives its bearer into life-falsifying doubt, because only thus does he escape despair over himself! What, after all, distinguishes the doubter Nietzsche from all the professional doubters there have ever been? That all the doubters there have ever been profess uncertainty and represent a kind of blasé attitude in knowledge, whereas Nietzsche with the pathos of combative rage and certainty wages war on — knowledge as something not in the least doubted, but personally ruinous to him! My truths must not be truths, otherwise I myself am judged by them: that is the deepest meaning of Nietzsche’s doubt born of despair. — He knew it and betrayed it in many places, but nowhere more clearly than in the unfettered and fetterless Eh: “Does one understand Hamlet? Not doubt, certainty is what drives one mad . . We are all afraid of the truth.” — Well then, the Yahwist in Nietzsche fears the truth, and it is the Yahwist who forces him to recant by whispering to him: life has need of deception!

In the same way we shed light down into the deepest ground of his almost boundless

14*

p. 212

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

glorification of pain. “It almost determines the order of rank how deeply human beings can suffer” (J 270). “Only great pain is the ultimate liberator of the spirit” (NcW, Epilogue). “Increscunt animi, virescit volnere virtus” (G, Preface; usable as a motto over Nietzsche’s entire work). Such a transfiguration of pain stands, of course, in the most extreme contradiction to his most merciless disclosure, namely that Christianity’s cult of suffering is merely the most persuasive means of seduction of vengefulness, and is itself so arch-Christian that the incorrigibles have repeatedly tried to claim him for Christianity by virtue of “unconscious testimony.” But who could endure such tortures without believing that they must in the end be good for something! Yet this belief, too, did not stand firm before the piercing ray of the most incorruptible of spirits; and with this we come in conclusion to a question on which no explorer of the soul should leave untried his powers, to the question: what did Nietzsche actually suffer from; with what did he have to wrestle on and on to the point of despair; out of which struggles did he constantly emerge — an almost broken triumphator — victorious? Our question concerns the actual facts of the phenomenon. That his soul was the scene of the murderous battle between Yahweh and Dionysus is our attempt at an explanation of his torments; but in what did these torments consist, and what did he experience in them?

No one will seriously want to answer by pointing to his bodily sufferings (including his presumed infection), although it would be a thought as profound as it is supportable in many ways to regard Nietzsche’s life and fate too as a symbol and thus to gather it together with his work into an overarching whole. But even someone who knew as little of Nietzsche’s biography as the author of these lines will nonetheless recall a number of documents from his youth that already breathe the same gloominess, although at that time there could be no question of severe bodily infirmity; and he would reject outright any explanation resorting to it upon comparing this or that passage in Nietzsche’s letters about his physical ill-health with the most important testimonies of suffering in his work. Read with this in mind the Epilogue to NcW; read above all the treatise mentioned above, “On the Knowledge of the Sufferer” (M 114), where the words from the cross, “My God, why hast thou forsaken me,” and the — dying Don Quixote are adduced; read — indeed, what could one read of Nietzsche’s

p. 213

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in which the fullness of life would not be confused with the greatness of bearing that proves itself at the stake, in which health, contentment, gentleness, warmth, the “coarse, dull, brown pleasure” (NcW) would not stand in the light of an at the very least most highly questionable, if not downright contemptible, thing, in which the second, “higher” health, the second, “more malicious” taste, the second, “more dangerous innocence in joy” (NcW), and whatever else one brings home from the “abysses of suspicion” — no, from the convulsions of despair — would not be praised almost with self-exaltation — read, and ask yourself whether bodily pains, however violently they may have played their part, could ever by themselves have brought in their train such a feeling of being newborn, with the never-absent undertone of having just escaped a terrible danger, and that by the last summoning of one’s own resistance. And once one has become convinced that this assumption cannot be carried through, one will perhaps lend a willing ear to the answer with which we now apply our disclosures about the nature of despair in Part One to the passion story of the greatest among the explorers of the soul: Nietzsche struggled all his life with the most violent temptation to suicide, and his pain-trembling triumphs were each time shot through by the consciousness of having mastered it again and again with almost superhuman exertion, and very often only by the narrowest margin.

Whoever traverses Nietzsche’s works with this knowledge in hand will make astonishing discoveries. We give here only three more pointers. “The tremendous tension of the intellect, which seeks to hold its own against pain, . . is . . powerful enough to defy all enticements* to suicide” (M 114). Now, Nietzsche countless times interprets on the presupposition of the desirability of suicide, as if it were among the natural inclinations of man one of the most powerful. “As soon as any war now breaks out, there always breaks out with it, precisely in the noblest of a people, a pleasure kept secret, to be sure: they throw themselves with delight toward the new danger of death, because in sacrifice for the fatherland they believe they have at last the long-sought permission, the permission to evade their goal: war is for them a detour to suicide, but a detour with a good conscience” (F 338). Here only a long dash is still fitting.

p. 214

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

At the beginning we tried to depict a little the fearfully flickering tints in which Nietzsche’s collected work appears embedded. We now know their meaning. The color-wild lights and flames of Dionysian exuberance play before the bottomless darkness of despair, and if by means of them Nietzsche finds words of the power of the poetic orgiast, we should nevertheless ourselves know of only a few of them to set as equals beside those words with which, in contrast to them and edged by their radiance, the background sections of horror at an “eternal damnation” come, as it were, into view. We know from the literature of all times and peoples of no second example (not in Byron, not in Lenau, not even in Dostoevsky) that would offer us the world in the reflection of the suicidal mood, with equal perfection of form, with such enervating urgency as these few sentences from Nietzsche: “A winter’s day lies upon us, and on high mountains we dwell, dangerously and in penury. Brief is every joy and pale every gleam of sunlight that creeps down to us along the white mountains. Then music sounds, an old man turns a barrel organ, the dancers whirl about — it shakes the wanderer to see this: so wild, so closed, so colorless, so hopeless is everything, and now in its midst a sound of joy, of thoughtless, loud joy! But already the mists of early evening creep up, the sound dies away, the wanderer’s step crunches; as far as he can still see, he sees nothing but the desolate and cruel face of nature” (U). — But similar prose poems are threaded through his entire work.

Finally a word in explanation of the thought that Nietzsche himself mistakenly held to be his greatest thought: the thought of “eternal recurrence.” — Here some things must be said beforehand. Most people have at some time had the experience of déjà vu, even if some are in the habit of forgetting it afterward. In a place still unknown to one, one enters a room in which three unknown persons are sitting around the table in the lamplight, and has in a flash the feeling: I have seen all this exactly so once before. We shall not linger over the explanation of this, which is in any case hardly all too difficult, and remark only: the thought of recurrence would not spring from it. — On the other hand there are always only a few people who have at some time had the genuine “experience of recurrence” — among them the author of these lines, twice in very early years, when he still knew nothing of Nietzsche and the Pythagoreans and was nevertheless

p. 215

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

inevitably led to the same false explanation, which he also wrote down at the time — and this must now be distinguished from déjà vu. For while the latter contains merely the once-before, but without any indication of time, in the “experience of recurrence” there is joined to the once-before a shock remotely akin to the feeling of vertigo, in consequence of which one believes one is gazing as though through an immeasurable abyss of ages, in comparison with which not only all pasts that can be experienced, but even the thought-out feelings of pastness, which in a quite distinguishable way attach themselves to historical dates and which, for instance, move the murder of Julius Caesar into quite a different distance from, say, the Revolution of 1848, shrivel to nothing. The “experience of recurrence” cannot for the present be explained; for the explanation which the Pythagoreans invented for it and which Nietzsche and others have repeated, metaphysically obvious enough, to be sure, but almost childlike, suffers from an error of thought whose more detailed discussion we may spare ourselves. — It may seem noteworthy that Nietzsche unquestionably had the experience, more noteworthy that he revived the old explanation of it as the ever-same, indeed ever identical, repetition of the entire course of the world; but it must arouse astonishment that he, to whom the metaphysics of physics was, all things considered, a matter of indifference, makes this explanation from then on the thoroughbass of his world-view and partly overlooks, partly cloaks with threadbare reasons its incompatibility with his Heracliteanism! The motives for this, then, do not lie in the “experience of recurrence.”

Consider: a Heraclitean of Nietzsche’s resoluteness knows no things, hence also no sameness of things, hence also no repetition of any kind. The similar may return, the same never. Conversely, the assumption of repetitions forms the decisive hallmark of mechanistic thinking, regardless of whether one has in view the number of plates manufactured after one and the same factory pattern, or the revolutions of a wheel of calculated speed, or finally a world-wheel each of whose revolutions takes billions upon billions of years. That is plain as day. Yet precisely ceaseless repetition, the emblem of all mechanism and the most unconditional counter-formula of life, is affirmed by the Heraclitean Nietzsche and, in WzM, where he is on the point of uncovering the contradiction,

p. 216

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

marked off, by emphasis on the horrifying prospect of the infinity of repetitions, from the merely finite world-machine of mechanism: an extreme culmination of the baroque ideal by the name of perpetuum mobile! However, the whole business leaves not the faintest doubt about the true motive. “ ‘It was’: thus is called the will’s gnashing of teeth and loneliest affliction. Powerless against what has been done, it is an angry spectator of all that is past . . . Until the creating will says to it: ‘But thus I willed it . . Thus shall I will it!’ ” And: “Was that life? Well then! Once More!” (Z).

Put yourself in the mood of a thinker who reaches for ever more incantatory formulas in order to prove his affirmation of life to himself against the despair which, out of the unhappy certainty that in him, too, the one belongs among the haters of life, threatens him with suicide, and one knows why he had to end with the doctrine of recurrence, although it is despair once again. With the declaration: this selfsame life, wrested a thousand times from the will to self-annihilation, I want to live again a thousand and a thousand times, he has achieved the utmost that can be devised, not indeed in affirmation of life, but certainly in negation of negation. It is the defensive formula of the most unbending self-assertion against the inclination to self-annihilation.

Nietzsche burned up; but in the heat of this blaze two demons locked in mortal combat with each other forged his double-faced work, which, in keeping with such an origin, marks with its truths as with its errors the most advanced outpost that reflection directed upon itself has reached thus far.

To page 4. — The appearance of the new edition of our Prinzipien under the title Die Grundlagen der Charakterkunde is imminent.

To page 18. — For more detail compare our essay Vom Wesen des Bewußtseins.

To page 20. — This naturally does not in the least exclude that the practical man of life should avail himself of the results of the explorer of the soul in an analogous way, as, say, the engineer avails himself of the results of the physicist. Rather, he will surely do so to his advantage. We possess today a character diagnostics that accomplishes excellent things in some fields and is in fact already often called upon with success by, e.g., great industrialists. But precisely these men by no means feel moved on that account to want to become characterologists themselves, and they are right!

4)

To page 31. — If one wishes to convince oneself of this glaringly, let one take up any professional psychologist who has had occasion to express himself elsewhere as well about human nature, and one will invariably make the almost comical discovery that he practices a double bookkeeping, in that in his natural conception of character he seems to have completely forgotten his psychology, and in his psychology completely forgotten his natural conception of character! We choose two examples, a great one and a small one, which lie more than two millennia apart.

If we open Aristotle’s treatise “On the Soul,” we hear of the sense functions, of perceiving, opining, thinking, of reason, of drive and of self-motion; with which the chief concepts of this science of the soul would be pretty well exhausted. If we open his “Ethics,” we hear the same thinker, unhindered by his psychology, philosophizing in a manner still valuable for characterology today not only about affects such as anger, fear, defiance, envy, joy, love, hatred, jealousy, pity, but also about traits such as courage, licentiousness, insensibility, liberality, stinginess, drunkenness, boastfulness, ambition, gentleness, irascibility, dexterity, shame, anxiety, malicious glee, sense of justice, courage, hopefulness, self-confidence, greed for gain, obstinacy, quarrelsomeness, truthfulness, etc.

The second example: We would have to compile a little lexicon of concepts if we wished to enumerate all the character traits which a Kraepelin employs in his Psychiatrie in his excellent (if not exactly laconic!) descriptions of states and portrayals of natures, and without doubt rightly so. We confine ourselves to citing the following: consciousness of guilt, passionateness, aimlessness, depression, distrust, irritability, unmanageability, stubbornness, adaptability, power of resistance, inflexibility, sensitivity, tenacity, obstinacy, refractoriness, urge to activity, love of mockery, contentiousness, hair-splitting, energy, capriciousness,

p. 218

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

volatility, perseverance, inconstancy, simplicity, susceptibility to ill humor, defiance. But the same researcher, in attempting to pursue professional psychology, manages to assure us that the chief traits of personality are: capacity for performance, capacity for practice, retention of practice, special memory, excitability, fatigability, capacity for recovery, depth of sleep, distractibility, capacity for habituation. — Imagine the task set of deriving the above from these “chief traits,” and one will not hesitate to admit that the one bookkeeping knows nothing of the other!

5)

To page 33. — More penetrating elucidations of this in Wesen des Bewußtseins, pp. 29–36.

6)

To page 34. — The accents of value are, to be sure, not lacking in mobility. In “Landgrave, become hard” hardness appears desirable, and when we say that someone has shown cold resolve under difficult circumstances, or that he met his adversary with cold courtesy, then even coldness carries the feeling tone of something distinguishing. In such cases the word owes its altered color each time to its relation to the predominant color of the context: hardness, say, pleases through its contrast to an excess of softness, coldness as an expression of incorruptibility or of particular greatness. If there is a tendency in usage to repeat such combinations often, changes of meaning begin to take shape; but the dependence of the value emphases on the assignment of the traits to the personal ego remains untouched by this.

7)

To page 37. — Nearly all philosophical reporters who come to speak of the once famous ontological proofs of God of Anselm of Canterbury are accustomed, following Kant’s example, to characterize the second, which derives the esse in re from the esse in intellectu (of a being quo majus cogitari non potest), as a surreptitious inference, but they fail to elucidate sufficiently why the said surreptitious inference made so great an impression and has provoked refutations ever anew down to the present. True, today, when for the second time we possess a scholasticism, albeit in the most diluted form, hardly anyone any longer overlooks that so-called realism was merely the Platonic doctrine of the reality of concepts, or at least of certain rational concepts, in the garb of theology; but this does not explain the decidedly novel turn, together with its persuasive power, according to which from the existence of a thought there is supposed to follow the existence of the thing to which the thought refers. Finally, the apt and repeatedly voiced observation that Anselm’s Godhead, as ens realissimum, was at bottom merely the abstract being of the Eleatics, does indeed complete the preliminaries of the false proof in the history of the spirit, but gives us all the less insight into its decisive artifice, since precisely the Eleatics, the “stoppers of the world’s course,” had inferred, not, say, from the thinkability of being its reality, but from the unthinkability (in the sense of incomprehensibility) of occurrence the necessity of assuming a changeless (and therefore, strictly speaking, timeless) being.

In fact, following Augustine’s precedent, Anselm’s proof of God marks the second stage of thought on the way to the thoroughly modern discovery of the nature of the ego, whose third and actually already last, or at least penultimate, stage is attained in the Cartesian cogito sum. The Eleatics did not yet know that reflection on being coincides in essence with reflection on the ego. Augustine, Anselm, Descartes, on the other hand, make use of precisely this in three different ways, without, to be sure, any of them having declared it without circumlocution. The secret pull of the ontological mode of inference lies in the fact that from the thought of an unconditioned being

p. 219

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

we are imperceptibly led back to our reflective ego (intellectus), which, insofar as it thinks itself, is incapable of doubting its existence; just as the secret pull of the Cartesian cogito sum lies in the fact that from the thought of our reflective ego we are inescapably compelled to assume the real existence of this ego; and the ontological “proof of God” is in fact and in truth an ontological proof of the ego.

8)

To page 38. — Should anyone, finally, out of shyness of names, wish at all costs to avoid the little words “I” or “self” and prefer to speak of “unity of consciousness,” that too might pass, provided he kept in view that he has thereby designated, not quite unambiguously, the condition of the sameness of intermittent consciousness with respect to the course of time, and that this is something essentially different from the temporality of the vital nexus to which it is attached. Meanwhile, whoever seriously tried to replace the personal pronouns I, you, he by “my unity of consciousness,” “your unity of consciousness,” “his unity of consciousness” would probably abandon such tastelessness most quickly, since he could not after all help noticing that the scorned pronouns had crept back in from behind by means of the “my” and “your” and “his”!

9)

To page 59. — There must, by the way, despite the opposition of the two thinkers, soon to be uncovered, lie something in common in the moods of life that enable the spirit to such terrible negations as both of them practice; for otherwise we would not know how to explain that a Stirner, as often as he momentarily exchanges the jet flame of his steel-cutting critique for the torchlight of pathos, displays an illumination even reminiscent of Nietzsche. “O you my much-tormented German people, what was your torment? It was the torment of a thought that cannot create a body for itself, the torment of a haunting spirit that dissolves into nothing at every cockcrow and yet languishes for redemption and fulfillment. In me too you have long lived, you dear — thought, you dear — specter. I almost fancied I had already found the word of your redemption, had discovered flesh and bone for the wandering spirit: then I hear them ringing, the bells that bring you to eternal rest, then the last hope dies away, then the last love hums itself out, then I depart from the dreary house of the deceased and turn in to the — living . . .” (Reclam, p. 253).

10)

To page 71. — For those who are less familiar with Nietzsche’s writings and therefore cannot estimate from their own experience to what extent the motif of the “nearest things” runs through his work, the following passage from W may find a place here: “One meets, when one looks about, always with people who have eaten eggs all their lives without noticing that the oblong ones are the tastiest, who do not know that a thunderstorm is beneficial to the abdomen, that fragrances smell strongest in cold clear air, that our sense of taste is unequal at different places in the mouth, that every meal at which one speaks well or listens well brings harm to the stomach. One may not be satisfied with these examples of the lack of a sense for observation; all the more should one concede that the very nearest things are very poorly seen, very poorly heeded by most people.”

To page 80. — Aesthetics — in the present meaning of the word, as is well known, an invention only of the 18th century — is either a nothing or a side of biology and in particular of the science of expression. Hitherto it was predominantly the former, as one most quickly convinces oneself, at least up to the year ’68, from Lotze’s still excellent Geschichte der Ästhetik in Deutschland. Since then, naturally, some things, and among them some valuable things, have been added,

p. 220

but fewer, after all, than word-happy optimists would have us believe, and this especially through the efforts of the scholars of art.

13)

To page 81. — How matters actually stand can only ever be decided case by case, and belongs to a domain of questions that we have delimited as the “tectonics of character.” Here relative unity presents two opposed chief types, according to which all the remaining cases can be meaningfully arranged: the type of strong one-sidedness from the scarcely contested predominance of one mainspring, and that of balance (“harmony”) from the complementary relation of mainsprings almost equally emphasized. The chief forms of disunity then follow of themselves.

14)

To page 83. — Nietzsche’s addition: “Forgive me the jest of this gloomy grimace and turn of phrase” betrays that, out of a slight fright at the “turn,” he estimated the motive for it far too superficially. — There is still another way of following a thinker than the one we pursue here, in examining the intellectual content of his judgments and of his justifications, and that is the weighing of his affirmative as well as negative favorite words. It does not, to be sure, lead to decisions about the correctness of his system, but it leads by the most direct path into the core of his nature. If one were to read through Nietzsche with this in view, one would see the conflict of the soul, which will emerge ever more clearly for us, expressed in the strife of two symbols: of a passionate striving-toward-the-heights (think of his poem of the albatross!) and of an equally passionate boring-into-the-depths, from which wells up his predilection for the extraordinarily frequent “abyss.” But the abyss (βυθός) belongs to one and the same series of symbols as: night — earth — woman — “instinct” — body; according to which the supposedly “gloomy turn” of the “abyss of suspicion” attests precisely the “depth” of suspicion and its origin in the resistance of the blood close to the heart against the life-endangering centrifugality of spirit. Even the “malicious squint,” in such a light, acquires something of the gnome-like and goblin-like character of the lower earth beings in the face of the danger of withering through the undamped sharpness of the rays of the ether.

To page 87. — From the 18th chapter of Der Fürst, after the translation by F. N. Baur. Newly revised by Hanns Floerke. Verlag der Deutschen Bibliothek in Berlin.

To page 87. — The classic presentation of the pietistic view of the world, with its deification of success and of all the diplomatic arts that help toward it, is still offered by Jung-Stilling’s autobiography, a book whose most careful reading cannot be recommended enough to every explorer of the soul. It is no accident either that “God as banker” forms its chief theme.

To page 95. — Only in passing do we touch on Nietzsche’s mistrust, already documented in Part One with a few pertinent quotations, of the undertaking to construct the doer from his deeds and in particular the artist from his works. Not only the deed, the work too is for him already a success, and the creator of the work a subsequent overpainting of reality in which this success guided the brush. His doubts about the value of “genius” that set in here are gathered fairly completely in the Zarathustra sermon “On Redemption” and culminate perhaps in the sentence: “But I

p. 221

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

never believed the people when they spoke of great men, and kept my belief that it was an inverse cripple * who has too little of everything and too much of one thing.” Already according to MA 231, the mutilation of an organ often gives occasion “for another organ to develop unusually well, because it has to perform its own function and yet another,” and in the same place it is already said that men evidently overestimate everything salient and submit to everything that would have power; for in truth “every talent is a vampire that sucks blood and strength from the other powers” (260). — Since we must of necessity confine ourselves to the main lines of Nietzsche’s findings, let the above references suffice, from which anyone can read off for himself that it needed only isolation and exaggeration once more for some busy physicians of the present to arrive at their flat-minded theories of giftedness.

To page 103. — “Every bit of teasing already shows what pleasure it gives to vent our power on another and to bring it to the pleasurable feeling of superiority” (MA 103). That is one of those sublime experiences of which Nietzsche calls a full treasure house his own. Another remark from M (30) gives us an exemplary instance of how he imagines the transformation of judgments into feelings that no longer let anything of the former be recognized. “It is still an all too paradoxical . . novelty that the morality of distinction is in the last analysis pleasure in refined cruelty. In the last analysis — that is to mean here: each time in the first generation. For when the habit of some distinguishing action is inherited, the ulterior thought is surely not inherited along with it . . .; and provided that it is not pushed back behind it again by education, there is already in the second generation no longer any pleasure of cruelty in it, but pleasure only in the habit as such. This pleasure, however, is the first stage of the ‘good.’”

To page 121. — For reflection on this we recommend the following stanzas from the Romanzero, which, by the way, are among the best that Heine has “poeticized”:

Long now, for millennia long

It has seethed in me. A dark woe!

And time licks at my wound

As the dog licked the sores of Job.

Thanks to you, dog, for your spittle —

But that can only cool and soothe —

Only death can heal me,

But alas, I am immortal!

Years come and go —

In the loom the shuttle runs

Busily whirring to and fro —

What he weaves, no weaver knows.

Years come and go,

Human tears trickle, run

Down upon the earth, and the earth

Sucks them in with silent greed —

Mad brew! The lid springs up —

Hail to the man whose hand

Seizes your young brood

And dashes it against the rock.

Thank God! the brew evaporates

In the kettle, which little by little

Falls wholly silent. My spleen gives way,

My west-eastern dark spleen —

24)

To page 123. — Today, when the “market of the spirit” is full of impostors and solemn buffoons, one has no opportunity to study any of those practices more thoroughly than the one we have emphasized in spaced type, of whose popularity, by the way, already Schopenhauer was so convinced that he comes to speak of it repeatedly. Thus he says in “On Judgment, Criticism, Applause and Fame”: “A means frequently used by envy for disparaging the good . . is the dishonorable and unconscionable praising of the bad: for as soon as the bad gains currency, the good is lost.” To the notorious tactic of silencing to death there is now joined in the present the reinsurance tactic of countless “conventicles of mutual praise,” which in part stand under a chief and master whose renown is already strong enough to promote those admitted, according to the measure of their publicly professed devotion, on the little ladder of fame, and in part again form rings, trusts, combines among themselves, always according to the tacitly followed principle: You praise me, I praise you. — Posterity is in and of itself no more understanding and just than its contemporaries, and time wears away not only barracks but also shatters temples, desecrates Egyptian tombs. But one thing it does accomplish after all: it sweeps out the filth. In a hundred years the mountain-high literary rubble of the present will be so thoroughly leveled that only a few real mountains will then tower up from afar, which for the time being disappear behind it. Which these may be, we do not know; but this we do know, that they are not the celebrities of today.

To page 146. — The Romantics, far from being moonlight-drunk enthusiasts, cast deep glances into reality such as were denied to the mechanists who followed them, and in many respects foresaw the calamity visible today even to dull eyes; to which, among other things, let the following saying of Jean Paul (from his Levana) bear witness: “Finally the spirit of eternity also holds up to us our shamelessness, with which we let the passionate heat of the . . fire of greed, of which . . the ancient peoples and the great men abstained or were ashamed, play in our darkness as a fireworks of honor, and says that we, alive only in hatred and hunger, like other decaying corpses keep only the teeth undecayed, the tools of both, of revenge and of enjoyment.

p. 223

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

Passionateness belongs precisely to the sickliness of the age; nowhere does so much effervescence, slackness, softness toward oneself and inexorable selfishness toward others dwell as on the sickbed. But on this bed lies this century.”

29)

To page 147. — The deeper ground of the pluralism — his pagan polytheism, of course — lends his style that peculiarity outshining all secondary traits from which no even somewhat poetic writer has since been able or is able to escape, and through which the elevated diction at least of German prose, but to some extent probably already of all Europe, was thoroughly altered: namely, to use in the plural countless nouns for objects that one otherwise used preferably in the singular, and among them various ones whose conceptual content resists plurality. Nietzsche speaks not of sun, moon, truth, heaven, etc., but of suns, moons, earths, truths, heavens, worlds, afterworlds, etc. With some exaggeration, but accurately in sense, one might say: all the nouns in Z stand in the plural. To this is naturally added a quite enormous overemphasis on the indefinite article. These are (demonologically conditioned) usages of language that our entire literature up to Nietzsche did not know. “A strong wind is Zarathustra to all lowlands” (instead of the lowland) — “a hailstorm to all fields of grain” (instead of the field of grain) — “stinking fires” — “And which were the ten reconciliations and the ten truths and the ten laughters?” — “for stars your soul thirsts” (instead of for the stars) — “you songbirds of my hopes” (instead of hope) — “the visions and dearest wonders of my youth” — “victory of my overcomings” — “resurrections” etc.

J 263 — The Bible as a book “of depth and of ultimate significance.”

M 68 — The Jews the people “that has driven the imagination of moral sublimity higher than any other people.”

M 205 — The Jews on the point of entering, justifiably, upon the dominion of Europe.

Eh “Why I Am So Clever” 4 — Boundless praise of Heine. “One will say one day that Heine and I (!) have been by far the foremost artists of the German language — at an incalculable distance from everything that mere Germans (!) have done with it.” Similar eulogies of Heine in three different places of G.

WzM 671 — “The anti-Semites do not forgive the Jews for having ‘spirit’ — and money. The anti-Semites — a name for the ‘short-changed.’”

Of declarations of the same kind there are dozens more in Nietzsche.

To page 154. — It reads: “Language is too weak to express the atrocities that Christianity has committed, whether to exterminate pagan idolatry, or to root out the heretics, or to keep down the sects that separated from the main church; the history of it arouses horror; one is appalled by it if one is only somewhat gentle; a good man cannot read these things without becoming wicked himself; he cannot refrain from cursing the memory of the authors of these atrocities.”

41)

To page 161. — As is well known, it has been urged against Kant’s “thing in itself” that it could at the very least not be inferred from appearances, because one would thereby have carried into reality itself the relation of cause and effect, which according to the Analytic is after all of purely subjective origin. However acute this objection, which by the way was already put forward with penetrating clarity by Aenesidemus-Schulze, it must nevertheless be said: even if causality were something objective and even if there undoubtedly were “things in themselves,” they would still be for all eternity just as incapable of appearing or of bringing about appearances as the strictly mathematical point is incapable of appearing or of giving birth to a material point!

42)

To page 166. — Namely J. J. Bachofen, whose chief works — above all Gräbersymbolik and Mutterrecht — we consider the most extraordinary thing that has been achieved up to this hour in disclosing the psychic foundations of antiquity and hence the psychic foundations of primordial man in general.

To page 168. — The most considerable effect of this so far in the field of classical philological research is, as is well known, Rohde’s Psyche, Seelenkult und Unsterblichkeitsglaube der Griechen, whose two by far most essential chapters, those which alone lend the book permanence, namely the first two of Volume II, on the Thracian and Greek worship of Dionysus, would without Nietzsche never have been written as they were written. The dependence on Nietzsche is mirrored right down to words and turns of phrase, which, as is known, did not prevent the author from mentioning his former youthful friend with not a single syllable!

To page 174. — It hardly needs emphasizing that the poets have always known this and given expression to it in manifold ways. With enumerations one would never come to an end. So let us recall only two very well-known utterances, Goethe’s: “I am the poet who fulfills himself — When he squanders his very own good,” and Eichendorff’s: “And I will not keep myself safe! — Far from you the wind drives me.”

46)

To page 174. — There are in his works not a few, if you will, mystical passages where the images with which his soul was pregnant shimmer through, sometimes even flashing up unveiled and showering the dance steps of his sentences with golden gleams. Then we divine a homeland millennia distant, perhaps of the archers from the plains around Susa and Ecbatana (the choice of the name Zarathustra is hardly an accident), certainly on mighty heights beneath the vertical rays of the sun of a blue-black firmament, where white flames of the fire cults lick upward. But only for moments does his spirit wake to this, only to tug again soon with rending jerks at the net of false concepts that holds him severed and captive. Thus with the holy madness there still mingles almost always a chilling reasoning, and only very rarely does he follow the riddling signs, whereupon truths might dawn on us such as become similarly divinable to us from Böcklin’s strongest poems in color or from the Moorish airs of Bizet’s “Carmen.” How strange it seems, then, when after the opening heavy with expectation:

Here, where the island grew between seas,

a sacrificial stone abruptly towered up,

here under a black sky

Zarathustra kindles his fires on the heights — and after the again so grand beginning of the final stanza:

Storm-driven sailors! Ruins of ancient stars!

You seas of the future! Unexplored heavens! — the promising work of words ends abstractly and palely and self-referentially with the following lines:

catch for me, the fisher on high mountains,

my seventh, last solitude!

We shall gather together some such “sparks of images.”

Whoever has not already sensed that it was the demonic power of an image that inspired in him the words cited above about “a god’s happiness” in F, to him Nietzsche himself betrays it with the almost literal repetition

Klages, Nietzsche’s Psychological Achievements. 15

p. 226

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

in Z: “This I learned from the sun when it goes down, the overrich one: gold it pours into the sea then from inexhaustible riches, — so that even the poorest fisherman still rows with a golden oar! For this I once saw * and did not tire of tears in watching.” — The same Zarathustra sermon “On Old and New Tablets” reveals to us that the hearth at which the strongest fires of his spirit were kindled is the bodily raptures of the dance: “Where all becoming seemed to me a dance of gods and a wantonness of gods, and the world unloosed and unbridled and fleeing back to itself: — as an eternal fleeing-from-itself and seeking-itself-again of many gods, as the blissful contradicting-itself, hearing-itself-again, belonging-to-itself-again of many gods: — Where all time seemed to me a blissful mockery of moments, where necessity was freedom itself, which played blissfully with the sting of freedom . . .” It would make a book if one wished to collect all the sentences in which the frenzies of the dance pulse, even if only few may equal in compelling luminosity those in praise of “Carmen”: “How the yellow afternoons of its happiness do us good! We look out the while: did we ever see the sea smoother? — And how soothingly the Moorish dance speaks to us! How in its lascivious melancholy even our insatiability for once learns satiety! — —” (FW). (The prelude to this, rich in beauty but still interspersed with what is all too much a matter of thought, is offered by J 255, which one may read for oneself.)

But into still deeper chasms we are torn by these words of consummate ecstasy: “Into your eye I looked of late, O life: gold I saw gleaming in your night-eye, — my heart stood still at this voluptuousness: — a golden boat I saw gleaming on nocturnal waters, a sinking, drinking, again beckoning golden rocking-boat!” Nietzsche does not succeed in the “Dance Song” (“The Other Dance Song”) in wresting its secret from the image-vision that robs him of his senses. “My heels reared up, my toes listened to understand you”; but it becomes only a leaping over stick and stone, because — we will blurt it out — something in him flees back reluctantly before the eye of night. But whither that could have carried him off, had the counter-spirit in him itself been broken, this the one and only Zarathustra sermon tells us from which a genuine song can at least be peeled out, the one and only song where the “armed lictors” of ever-wakeful thinking give way and the most restless of spirits is compelled to listen to the flute calls of the primordial world:

“Light am I: ah, that I were night!

But this is my solitude, that I am girded round with light.

Ah, that I were dark and nocturnal!

How I would suck at the breasts of light! . . .

Oh, it is you only, you dark ones, you nocturnal ones,

who create warmth from what shines!

Oh, you only drink milk and refreshment from the udders of light!

Ah, ice is around me, my hand burns itself on what is icy!

Ah, thirst is in me that languishes after your thirst!

It is night: ah, that I must be light!

And thirst for what is nocturnal! And solitude!

It is night: now like a spring my longing breaks forth from me,

— for speech I long.

It is night: now all leaping fountains speak more loudly.

And my soul too is a leaping fountain.

It is night: now all the songs of lovers awaken.

And my soul too is the song of a lover. —”

p. 227

original not in the corpus for this page — see the PDF edition

In the reality of images, noon and midnight belong polarly to one another, and around the hour of great Pan too there is a whispering and rustling of spirit voices, much as in the culminating dark, while the individual soul that is graced to hear such things always belongs predominantly to one of the two poles: Nietzsche to the shudder of noon, which at least once he knows how, if not to say, then to stammer in a deeply moving way:

“— What happened to me: hark! Did time fly away?

Am I not falling? Did I not fall

— hark! into the well of eternity? . . .

(The soul speaks):

‘Do let me be! Still! Did not the world just become perfect?

Oh, the golden round ball!’ — . . .

When will you drink this drop of dew

that fell down upon all earthly things,

— when will you drink this strange soul —

— when, well of eternity!

you serene, awful abyss of noon!

when will you drink my soul back into yourself?” With this compare also:

“It was at noon,

At noon, when first

The summer climbs into the mountains,

The boy with the tired, hot eyes.”

More numerous are the passages where, listening into himself, listening for things unheard-of, full of horror, he murmurs of other secrets, of the secrets of the intermediate realm, which threaten with madness. — “‘And this slow spider that crawls in the moonlight, and this moonlight itself, and I and you in the gateway, whispering together, whispering of eternal things, — must we not all have been here before? — and come again and run in that other lane, out, ahead of us, in this long, dreadful lane — must we not come again eternally?’ — Thus I spoke, and ever more softly: for I was afraid of my own thoughts and ulterior thoughts. Then, suddenly, I heard a dog howl nearby.” — Who could fail to hear that here something superpersonally real makes itself audible, and who, however, would not also feel that the half childish, half baroque assumption of an objectively exact recurrence is utterly inadequate to interpret the uncanny event!

We close with a cosmic poem, which is perfect without qualification without the stanza that Nietzsche placed before the following by way of explanation:

I look upward —

there seas of light are rolling:

— oh night, oh silence, oh deathly-still noise! . .

I see a sign —,

from farthest distances

a constellation sinks slowly sparkling toward me . . .

48)

To page 187. — The most decisive utterance of Hegel’s reads: “The chief difficulty of the critical philosophy is that, before one sets about knowing God, the essence of things, etc., the faculty of knowledge itself must first be examined; one must, it says, first become acquainted with the instrument before one undertakes the work that is supposed to come about by means of it; if it were inadequate, all the effort would otherwise be wasted in vain. — This thought has seemed so plausible that it has aroused the greatest admiration and assent and has led knowing back from its interest in the objects and its business with them to itself, to the formal. If, however, one does not wish to deceive oneself with words, it is easy to see that other interests may well be examined and judged in some other way than by undertaking the peculiar work for which they are destined. But the examination of knowing cannot take place otherwise than knowingly. With this so-called instrument, to examine it means nothing other than to know it. But to want to know before one knows is just as absurd as the wise resolve of that scholastic to learn to swim before he ventured into the water.” Enzyklopädie der philosophischen Wissenschaften im Grundrisse, Heidelberg 1830, p. 15.

49)

To page 193. — How near Nietzsche came to the fundamental insight that we have just developed is shown, among other things, by the following words: “Usually one takes consciousness itself as the . . highest court of appeal; yet it is only a means of communicability: it is developed in intercourse, and with regard to the interests of intercourse . . .” (WzM). But such things remained with him an aperçu, and so he foundered.

50)

Through penances lasting several thousand years, undertaken in order to avenge himself for wrong suffered at the hands of the Brahman Vasishtha, the king attains to “omnipotence.” The globe totters, the three worlds quake, the gods flee; no man and no god can any longer offer resistance to Viswamitra. Then — by virtue of the only intensification still possible, namely the “victory over himself,” he renounces his revenge on Vasishtha!
